Foreword
These lecture notes grew out of a PhD course on the many manifestations of the uncertainty
principle in harmonic analysis, given at the Centre for Mathematical Sciences at Lund
University in the spring semester of 2026. The subject is vast and touches not only harmonic
analysis, but also complex analysis and operator theory.
    The material presented here reflects a selection of topics from this broad landscape,
principally guided by the author’s mathematical taste. The notes are therefore not intended
to provide a comprehensive account, nor do they aim to cover the most recent developments.
Rather, they document a personal journey toward understanding some of the deeper aspects
of the subject. It is my hope that further reading, and future interactions with experts in
the field will lead to greater insights and, in time, improvements of these notes.
    To make myself clear, I do not proclaim myself as an expert in the topic, but rather
a curious explorer with intentions of expanding his knowledge in the field, and hopefully,
bringing more ambitions people on board to this journey.
    The idea for this course was initiated and strongly encouraged by my former doctoral
supervisor, Sandra Pott. I am grateful to her for suggesting me to embark on this adventure,
and for presenting me with this opportunity to broadening my perspectives in mathematics.
    I am also grateful to Bartosz Malman for his friendship and mentorship over the years.
Several of the ideas and perspectives reflected in these notes have grown out of our discus-
sions and joint work, and it has been a pleasure to include some of his recent contributions
to the topic, in these notes.
    I also thank Alexandru Aleman and Erik Wahlén for helpful discussions and advice on
preparing lecture notes. I have also benefited greatly from stimulating conversations with
Alex Bergman and Eskil Rydhe on several of the topics included here.
    I would also like to sincerely thank Tomas Persson, for encouraging me to lecture the
course despite my notes not being fully prepared at the time. In fact, his encouragement
was a decisive motivation for me to finally complete them.
    Finally, I am deeply grateful to my family and friends for their constant support. The
final parts of these notes were written shortly after the birth of my first child. Despite the
associated challenges with becoming a parent, the love and encouragement from my family
made it possible to carry this work through. Therefore, I dedicate these notes to my beloved
wife and son, Fitore & Albin.

1     Preface
1.1    Principles of uncertainty
What is meant by a principle of uncertainty? For some, it may call to mind quantum me-
chanics, where the limitations on the simultaneous measurement of position and momentum
is expressed. More broadly, one may consider systems equipped with complementary pairs
of variables, such as time and frequency or position and scale, which behave like conjugate
axes and consequently resist simultaneous sharp localization. In analysis, this phenomenon

appears through the Fourier transform, where it is reflected in precise mathematical state-
ments relating the localization of a function to that of its transform. Despite these varied
manifestations, the common structural theme that emerges is that an uncertainty principle
expresses a fundamental trade-off between the simultaneous precision of two complementary
descriptions of a system. In what follows, I will outline a (meta)-philosophical viewpoint

that, that I personally find reveals a deeper unity between the physical and mathemati-
cal aspects of the subject. Let us therefore widen our horizon and consider the following
abstract modelistic framework. Let X and Y be sets, and consider a transformation


                                            T :X→Y

which transports elements of X into Y while preserving some structure, perhaps linearity,
continuity, or something else. For instance, one may interpret T as encoding an observation
of elements in X, hence it is important that T is not too wild, so that information about
x ∈ X is still reflected in T (x). A fundamental problem is to understand how these mappings

act on certain elements, so suppose we have a subset X0 ⊂ X of elements with some desirable
properties, and likewise subset Y0 ⊂ Y on the target space of T . A natural question that
often arises is
                                 Is T (X0 ) ∩ Y0 non-trivial?                           (1)

Roughly speaking, this abstract model attempts to capture a class of problems of the form:
”Can we find objects from X0 , and whose T -transform also enjoys properties from Y0 ?”.

    This leads to the study of incompatible pairs (X0 , Y0 ), that is, pairs of sets for which
the above fails. When the compatibility fails, the obstruction is often reflects a deeper
limitation, that certain properties in X0 via the transformation T act as an impediment
for certain properties in Y0 . When these properties admit quantitative descriptions, such
incompatibility gives rise to what one may refer to as an uncertainty principle:
One cannot simultaneously impose the constraints defining X0 and Y0 beyond a certain
threshold.
In some sense, one may interpret that uncertainty principles arise from uniqueness phenom-
ena associated with specials subsets of incompatible pairs. In mathematical analysis, the

sets X and Y typically carry some linear and topological structures, and thus one is often
led to consider the following refinement the compatibility question:

                          Is T (X0 ) ∩ Y0    dense in Y0   (or in Y ).


                                                5
This brings in an additional approximation dimension to the problem, which often lies in the
heart of harmonic analysis. The most prominent setting in which such questions arise is when
T is the classical Fourier transform, acting on various spaces of functions. Its central role
does not only stem from its rich mathematical structure, but also from its interpretation
in describing the fabrics of reality, as in quantum mechanics and signal processing. For
instance, X0 could be space of compactly supported continuous functions on the real line,
and Y0 could be functions which vanish of certain prescribed on certain prescribed set, or
perhaps growth at a certain rate at infinity. At this level, the theory already displays a
remarkable variety of uncertainty phenomena.
   These notes are devoted to exploring several classical manifestations of the uncertainty
principle, primarily on the real line R and on subintervals of it, often identified with the
unit circle T ∼
              = [0, 2π).
   We shall not attempt to systematically track which results extend to higher dimensions
and which fail there. Typically, such questions are either trivial or extremely difficult, and
thus often lie close to the research frontier.
   Formally, the Fourier transform of a complex-valued function f on R is defined by
                                       Z
                               fb(ξ) =   f (x)e−ixξ dx   ξ ∈ R,
                                       R

interpreted in an appropriate sense depending on the regularity of f . On the unit circle,
that is, for 2π-periodic functions, the Fourier coefficients are given by
                                     Z
                            fb(n) :=    f (ζ) ζ −n dm(ζ)     n ∈ Z,
                                      T

                             dt
where ζ = eit and dm(eit ) = 2π denotes normalized arc-length measure on [0, 2π).
    A guiding principle throughout is that structural properties of a function are reflected,
often in subtle and indirect ways, in the behavior of its Fourier transform, and conversely.
A fundamental manifestation of this principle is provided by Parseval’s identity
                               Z                   X
                                  |f (ζ)|2 dm(ζ) =    |fb(n)|2
                                 T                  n∈Z


and its real-line counterpart, Plancherel’s theorem,
                               Z                  Z
                                         2      1
                                  |f (x)| dx =       |fb(ξ)|2 dξ.
                                R              2π R

These identities intrinsically arise from the orthogonality of the exponential functions and
reveal the underlying Hilbert space geometry of Fourier analysis. It is precisely this geomet-
ric structure that makes the theory fruitful, and once one leaves this Hilbert space realm
of, many of these results fail or require substantial modification, often leading to delicate
threshold phenomena.
    One immediate consequence is that a measure whose Fourier coefficients are square
summable, or a function whose Fourier transform lies in L2 , cannot be supported on a set of
Lebesgue measure zero. This statement is essentially sharp, and its borderline nature will
be illustrated by the Ivashev–Musatov theorem in Section 6.


                                               6
   Another recurring theme is that sparsity of Fourier support forces spatial spread. Roughly
speaking, a function whose Fourier transform is supported on a thin set must itself be widely
spread. This principle appears in many forms, beginning with the work of Paley and Zyg-
mund on lacunary series, which we discuss in Section 2.
   In a different direction, the Paley–Wiener theorem shows that the Fourier transform of a
compactly supported function extends to an entire function and, in particular, cannot vanish
too frequently. This provides a complementary manifestation of the uncertainty principle,
where strong spatial localization imposes rigidity in the frequency domain.
   We also encounter the classical Heisenberg uncertainty principle, which quantifies the
impossibility of simultaneous concentration in both position and frequency.

   A further key phenomenon is the role of logarithmic integrability. For instance, a classical
Theorem by Jensen implies that if f ∈ L1 (T) satisfies fb(n) = 0 for all n < 0, then
                                  Z
                                       log |f (ζ)| dm(ζ) > −∞.
                                   T

Thus, such a function cannot vanish on a set of positive measure, and moreover its magnitude
must be sufficiently distributed so as to be logarithmically integrable. The real-line analogue
is determined by the logarithmic integral

                                        log |f (x)|
                                      Z
                                                2
                                                    dx > −∞.
                                       R 1+x

These logarithmic integral conditions plays a central role in the theory of Hardy spaces,
but its influence extends much further. They will reappear in our study of polynomial
approximation, moment problems, and various questions of uniqueness type

  Finally, we briefly allude to nonlinear analogues of the Fourier transform, such as the
map                                R       f (ζ) dµ(ζ)
                                          T   1−ζz
                       Kµ (f )(z) :=      R dµ(ζ)        z ∈ C \ supp(µ),
                                           T 1−ζz

which arises naturally in complex function theory. While we shall not pursue this direction
in depth, it serves as a reminder that many of the principles discussed here persist, in altered
form, beyond the linear setting.


1.2    Organization
These notes should be viewed as a collection of selected topics illustrating, from different
angles, manifestations of the uncertainty principle:
   f and its Fourier transform fb cannot both be small simultaneously.

   The material is divided into seven sections, each consisting of roughly 5–7 subsections.
The final subsection of each section is devoted to further results and is intended to guide
the reader toward the research frontier.
   The sections are, to a large extent, independent of one another, and the notes are not
meant to be read in a strictly chronological order. This structure is intended to accommodate
PhD students and researchers with limited time, allowing for selective reading.



                                                   7
   In Sections 2 and 3 we work on the unit circle T, with an emphasis on Fourier coefficients.

    Section 2 begins with a brief introduction to classical representation and uniqueness
problems for Fourier series. We then turn to problems of translates, focusing on results of
Wiener and Beurling in the settings of ℓ1 and ℓ2 . This is followed by a discussion of local-
to-global properties of lacunary series and Riesz products, which are subsequently used to
obtain coarse classification results for Fourier coefficients of measures on T.

   Section 3 is devoted to the role of the logarithmic integral. We study the Riesz brothers’
theorem and its converses, and then proceed to Szegö’s theorem. We conclude the section
by considering extensions to the unit disc developed by Khrushchev.

   Section 4 concerns uniqueness problems for the Fourier transform on L2 (R). We begin
with Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle and continue with the Paley–Wiener theorem on
functions with compactly supported Fourier transform. The final part of the section treats
deeper results on measures with spectral gaps, including Pollard’s theorem, the Amrein–
Berthier theorem, and the Logvinenko–Sereda theorem on Fourier uniqueness pairs.

    Section 5 studies the role of the logarithmic integral on the real line. The main theme
is Bernstein’s problem on weighted polynomial approximation and its equivalent reformu-
lations in terms of moment problems and quasi-analyticity. We also discuss a unilateral
version due to Beurling and prove the classical theorem of Cartwright–Levinson.

    Sections 6 and 7 are devoted to more specialized topics related to uniform Fourier ma-
jorants for functions with small support.
    Section 6 treats the Ivashev–Musatov theorem and some of its consequences. The pre-
sentation is largely based on the work of T. W. Körner in [59], where one can extract a more
streamlined proof that avoids oscillatory integral techniques, and requires less regularity
assumptions, in contrast to the conventional approach in [36].

    Section 7 is devoted to the Beurling–Malliavin multiplier theorem. We present two
different proofs of this result and discuss some of its consequences. The first proof follows
the classical survey in [77] by V.P. Havin, J. Mashreghi and F. Nazarov, while the second
is based on a more recent approach suggested from the work of A. Cohen in [23].

   These notes are largely, but not exclusively, inspired by the following classical books,
which treat different aspects of the subject:

      [36] V. P. Havin and B. Jöricke, The Uncertainty Principle in Harmonic Analysis,

      [50] Y. Katznelson, An Introduction to Harmonic Analysis,

      [33] J. B. Garnett, Bounded Analytic Functions.

    We also mention further treatments of the subject in the work of P. Koosis [54, 55, 52],
as well as in the book of J.-P. Kahane and R. Salem [49]. Additional, more specialized
references appear throughout the notes.


1.3    Notations
We begin by fixing some standard notation that will be used throughout.


                                              8
    For 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞, we denote by Lp (T) the usual Lebesgue spaces on T, equipped with
normalized arc-length measure dm. On the real line, we write Lp (R) for the Lebesgue spaces
with respect to Lebesgue measure dx. In case we consider Lebesgue spaces wrt to a different
positive measure µ, we simple write Lp (µ).
    Strictly speaking, elements of Lp are equivalence classes of functions. In practice, we
will often identify such classes with a representative, since functions that differ only on a
set of measure zero are indistinguishable for our purposes.

   The Banach spaces of complex finite Borel measures on T and R, equipped with the
total variation norm, will be denoted by M (T) and M (R), respectively. If E is a compact
subset of either T or R, we write µ ∈ M (E) to indicate that µ is supported in E. Here,
the support of µ is the smallest closed set E such that |µ|(I) = 0 for every interval or arc
I disjoint from E. This notion agrees with the usual definition of support in distribution
theory.

   For a compact set E, we denote by C(E) the space of continuous functions on E. The
spaces of k-times continuously differentiable functions are denoted by C k , smooth functions
by C ∞ , and compactly supported smooth functions by C0∞ .

   We will freely use standard duality results from functional analysis, such as the duality
between Lp and Lq , and the Riesz representation theorem identifying C(E)∗ with M (E).
The reader is assumed to be familiar with these facts.

    Finally, we adopt the notation A ≲ B to mean that A ≤ cB for some constant c > 0.
If both A ≲ B and B ≲ A hold, we write A ≍ B. When carrying out estimates, the
implicit constants involved may vary from line to line, but we shall find it convenient to
simple denote by the same symbol c or C. From time to time, we shall also make use of the
standard big-O and little-o notation.

    Further notation will be introduced as needed throughout these notes.


2     Uniqueness problems on Fourier series
2.1    Classical representation problems on Fourier series
We shall here gather some classical results on Fourier series. A formal Fourier series of an
integrable complex-valued integrable function f ∈ L1 (T) on the unit-circle T ∼ = [0, 2π) is
defined as                            X
                              f (ζ) ∼     fb(n)ζ n   ζ = eit ,
                                          n∈Z

where the Fourier coefficients {fb(n)}n∈Z are complex numbers defined by
                                      Z
                           fb(n) :=       f (ζ)ζ −n dm(ζ),   n ∈ Z.
                                      T

                dt
Here dm(eit ) = 2π denotes the normalized Lebesgue measure.
    The concept of Fourier series originates from the influential work of J. Fourier in the
early 1820’s, motivated by his analytic theory of heat flow, studying the heat equation.
His fundamental insight was that complicated functions (or ”signals”) could be decomposed


                                                 9
into superpositions of simple oscillatory modes, and that the evolution of each mode could
be analyzed independently. This principle of superposition lies at the heart of harmonic
analysis, where we attempt to decompose complicated objects into simpler ones, study
them individually, and the reassemble the pieces.
   In the theory of Fourier series, the following natural and fundamental problems arise:

      (Representation and convergence) Which reasonable (periodic) functions f can be
      represented as Fourier series, and in what sense does the Fourier series converge to f ?

      (Uniqueness) Do the coefficients {fb(n)}n∈Z uniquely determine f ?

   A large part of Fourier analysis is devoted to understanding these questions in various
capacities. Typically, one is interesting in this questions for different classes of functions f .
   Before turning to convergence issues, let us record some basic algebraic properties of
Fourier coefficients, which reflect how some simple operations on functions translate into
transformations of their frequencies.
   For f, g ∈ L1 (T), the following relations hold:

   • Linearity: αf
                 \                     g (n), where α, β ∈ C are numbers.
                   + βg(n) = αfb(n) + βb

   • Translation: If ft0 (eit ) := f (ei(t−t0 ) ), then

                                                        −int0 b
                                             fc
                                              t0 (n) = e     f (n).

   • Modulation: If f (eit ) 7→ eint0 f (eit ), then

                                            eint
                                            \             b(n − n0 ).
                                                0 f (n) = f




   • Convolution: If f ∗ g denotes convolution on T, defined by
                                          Z
                                    it
                           (f ∗ g)(e ) :=   f (ei(t−τ ) )g(eiτ ) dm(eiτ ),
                                                    T

      then
                                              ∗ g(n) = fb(n)b
                                             f[             g (n).

   • Differentiation: If f is differentiable, then

                                               fb′ (n) = in fb(n).

    A convenient setting of consider Fourier series on is L2 (T), the Hilbert space of square-
integrable functions on T wrt dm:
                                       Z
                               ∥f ∥L2 = |f (ζ)|2 dm(ζ) < ∞.
                                   2
                                                T

One can easily verify that the family of monomials {ζ n }n∈Z forms an orthonormal system:
                                                        
                                Z                       1    n = k,
                                      ζ n ζ k dm(ζ) =
                                  T                     0    n ̸= k.


                                                    10
Using Weierstrass approximation Theorem (see Theorem 2.7 below), one can also show the
this system is complete in L2 (T), hence {ζ n }n∈Z actually forms an orthonormal basis in
L2 (T). We therefore obtain the beautiful Parseval identity, which intimately relates the
mass of f with total mass of all its frequencies:
                              Z                     X
                                   |f (ζ)|2 dm(ζ) =   |fb(n)|2 .
                                 T                        n∈Z


From this, it follows that Fourier partial sums
                                              X
                           SN (f )(ζ) :=             fb(n)ζ n ,     ζ ∈ T,
                                             |n|≤N


converge to f in the norm of L2 (T):
                                             X
                     ∥f − SN (f )∥2L2 :=            |fb(n)|2 → 0,      N → ∞.
                                            |n|>N


The engine behind this is all attributed to orthogonality, and thus crucially relies on the
underlying Hilbert space structure. In general, the theory becomes much more complicated
once we leave this classical roam. Using Hilbert transforms, one can also show that
                            Z
                               |SN (f ) − f |p dm → 0,  N → ∞,
                            T

for any p > 1. For instance, see [50].
    We now consider the question of pointwise convergence of Fourier series. A simple and
classical framework is the so-called Wiener algebra A(T) of continuous functions f on T
satisfying                                  X
                                   ∥f ∥A :=   |fb(n)| < ∞.
                                               n∈Z

This condition and the Weierstrass M-test ensures that the Fourier partials sum SN (f )
converge to f uniformly on T. Following these discussions, one is naturally lead to the belief
that the Fourier partial sums of a continuous function always converge. However, this is
false.

Theorem 2.1 (du Bois–Reymond, 1870’s). For any point ζ0 ∈ T, there exists a continuous
function f on T, such that
                            sup |SN (f )(ζ0 )| = +∞.
                                     N ≥1

In particular, the Fourier series of a continuous function on T need not to converge at every
point on T.

   In initial proof was constructive, but in modern days, one can give a simple proof based
on the Banach–Steinhaus principle of uniform boundedness in functional analysis.

Proof. Fix a point ζ0 ∈ T, and assume that for any function f ∈ C(T) the Fourier partial
sums                                    X
                        SN (f )(ζ0 ) :=   fb(n)ζ0n , N = 1, 2, 3, . . .
                                            |n|≤N



                                                    11
converges. Regarding the family (SN )N as bounded linear functionals on the Banach space
C(T) of continuous functions on T, we must then have

                                         sup |SN (f )(ζ0 )| ≤ C(f ),
                                         N ≥0


for all f ∈ C(T), hence by the Banach–Steinhaus principle of uniform boundedness:

                                           sup ∥SN ∥C(T)∗ < ∞.
                                          N ≥1


Realizing the Fourier partial sums as convolution operators
                           Z
           SN (f )(eit ) =   f (eiτ )DN (ei(t−τ ) )dτ, eit ∈ T,                  N = 1, 2, 3, . . .
                            T

where DN denotes the Dirichlet kernel
                        X                sin((N + 1/2)t)
         DN (eit ) :=           eint =                   ,       eit ∈ T,          N = 1, 2, 3, . . .
                                             sin(t/2)
                        |n|≤N


By the Riesz representation theorem, each functional SN (·)(ζ0 ) may be identified with in-
tegration against the signed measure whose density is the translated Dirichlet kernel. In
particular,                                            Z
                         ∥SN ∥C(T)∗ = ∥DN (ζ0 ) dm∥M (T) =                 |DN | dm.
                                                                       T

Indeed, this follows by approximating sgnDN with functions of unit-norm in C(T). Now
the desired contradiction arises from the fact that a straightforward computation gives
                                    Z
                                       |DN | dm ≍ log N.
                                            T

In conclusion, there must exist a function f ∈ C(T), whose Fourier partials sums are un-
bounded at ζ0 ∈ T.

   Despite this fact, there is fortunately a condition on f , which is just slightly stronger
than mere continuity at a point, but on the upside, it is a local condition, which ensures
pointwise convergence of the Fourier series.

Proposition 2.2. (Dini’s criterion) Let f ∈ L1 (T) with the property that for some t0 ∈
[0, 2π):
                            Z 2π
                                  |f (eit ) − f (eit0 )|
                                                         dt < ∞.
                              0         |t − t0 |
Then
                                SN (f )(eit0 ) → f (eit0 ),      N → ∞.

Proof. Without loss of generality, one may take t0 = 0. Using the explicit expression for
                                       R
the Dirichlet kernel, and the fact that T DN dm = 1, one can write
                                            Z 2π
                                                                      f (eit ) − f (1) dt
                 SN (f )(1) − f (1) =               sin((N + 1/2)t)                       .
                                                0                        sin(t/2) 2π



                                                      12
Now the assumption on f ensures that the function

                                         f (eit ) − f (1)
                                            sin(t/2)

is integrable, hence the claim follows from the Riemann–Lebesgue Lemma 2.31, which is
proved later.

    In particular, it follows that the Fourier series of a Hölder continuous function f on T
converges to f at every point in T.
    Intrinsically, the convergence issues associated with the convergence of Fourier partials
sums are mainly caused by the fact that the Dirichlet kernels fail be uniformly bounded in
norm of L1 (T).
    Now despite the counterexample of du Bois–Reymond, it was still believed for a long
time that even if the Fourier series may diverge somewhere, these points must be rather
exceptional, and the Fourier series of an integrable function only fails to converge at a set
of Lebesgue measure zero. Then came A. Kolmogorov, and produced the wildest possible
counter-example.
Theorem 2.3 (Kolmogorov, 1926). There exists a function f ∈ L1 (T) whose Fourier series
diverges at every point in T.
    Kolmogorov’s sophisticated counter example shattered much of the remaining general
belief about the convergence problem of Fourier series. However, N. Lusin still conjectured
that convergence at dm-a.e point should hold for functions in L2 (T). Surprisingly, this was
later confirmed in the celebrated work of L. Carleson in [20].
Theorem 2.4 (Carleson, 1965). For any f ∈ L2 (T) the partial sums satisfy

                              SN (f )(ζ) → f (ζ),           N → ∞,

pointwise at dm-a.e ζ ∈ T.
    Not long after, Carleson’s Theorem was extended to all f ∈ Lp (T) for p > 1, by R. Hunt
in 1967. A complementary result showing the sharpness of Carleson Theorem was given by
J-P. Kahane and Y. Katznelson in [48]
Theorem 2.5 (Kahane–Katznelson). For any compact set E ⊂ T of Lebesgue measure zero,
there exists an f ∈ C(T) such that

                             sup |SN (f )(ζ)| = +∞,           ζ ∈ E.
                             N ≥1


    A simpler proof of this result, and an a.e version of Kolmogorov’s Theorem is contained
in [50, Ch. II, section 3]. Let us now include a short and elementary proof of Carleson’s
Theorem for functions of finite Dirichlet integral.
Proposition 2.6 (Mild Carleson). Let f ∈ L2 (T) with the additional property that
                                    X
                                     (1 + |n|)|fb(n)|2 < ∞.                               (2)
                                     n


Then the Fourier partial sums SN (f ) converge to f at dm-a.e on T.

                           ∥Sn − f ∥2L2   1 X b 2

                   X               X                X         1   X              1

Note that the assumption on Fourier coefficients of f enables us to choose εk ↓ 0 slow enough

zero, hence for dm-a.e ζ ∈ T, there exists an integer N = N (ζ) > 0 such that

                                   |SN (f )(ζ) − f (ζ)| ≤ εN .

This shows that SN (f ) → f pointwise dm-a.e on T.

    The label finite Dirichlet integral comes from the fact that the summability condition in
(2) is equivalent to                Z

                                       |z|<1

where P denotes the (harmonic) Poisson extension of f to the unit-disc. The intriguing
thing here is that functions having finite Dirichlet integral may still be very wild on T.
We mention yet another milder version of Carleson’s Theorem, which is also elementary
(though not as simple as the one above), but requires the stronger assumption that the
Fourier coefficients are ℓp -summable:
                                          X
                                                |fb(n)|p < ∞,
                                         n∈Z


for some 0 < p < 2. This is contained in the B. Simon [91, P. ? ].
    As previously mentioned, most of the convergence issues arise due to the fact that the
Dirichlet kernels fail to have an asymptotically bounded L1 -integral. To compensate for
their lack of integrability, analyst have long attempted to rectify the kernels by considering
averages of them. Recall that if a sequence of complex numbers sn → L, then so does its
arithmetic average:
                                      n
                                   1X
                                         sk → L,     n → ∞.
                                   n
                                       k=1

This means that averages can only improve the state of convergence, and one is therefore

It turns out the family of Fejér kernels are much better behaved than the Dirichlet kernels,
and satisfy the following conditions:
       R

These properties of the Fejér kernels ensure that the sequence of measures FN dm are ap-
proximates of the identity, that is, they converge in weak-star to the Dirac delta measure
dδ1 in the space M (T). The problem with the Dirichlet kernel is that it violates (ii), hence
it leaves any ball in the norm of M (T).
     With these properties at hand, we can show that the Fejér means σN (f ) of a continuous

                                Z 2π

trigonometric polynomials.

Theorem 2.7 (Weierstrass). The trigonometric polynomials are dense in C(T).

   One can also show that the Fejér means converge in Lp (T) for all p ≥ 1:
                                                   Z

Indeed, this follows from a standard 3ε-argument:

              ∥σN (f ) − f ∥Lp ≤ ∥g − f ∥Lp + ∥g − σN (g)∥L∞ + ∥σN (f − g)∥Lp .

Note that the last term can be estimated independent of N , using Minkowski’s integral

                              ∥σN (f − g)∥Lp ≤ sup ∥FN ∥L1 ∥f − g∥Lp .

Sending N → ∞, we obtain

                                         ∥σN (f ) − f ∥Lp ≤ C∥g − f ∥Lp ,

for any g ∈ C(T). Since C(T) is dense in Lp (T) by classical measure theory, we obtain the
desired conclusion.
    Another important summation method for Fourier series are the Abel–Poisson means,

One can also verify that the Abel–Poisson means satisfy the properties (i) − (iii) similar to

                                   

                                      P (f )(z) → f (ζ)

as z → ζ within any fixed cone with vertex at ζ contained in D. This result is one of the
fundamental cornerstones of Hardy space theory. See [33, Ch. II]

2.2    Classical uniqueness problems of Fourier series
In the previous subsection, we mainly discussed results on various representations involving
Fourier series, and the problems of convergence associated with such representations. Here,


                                                           17
attention to questions of uniqueness are consider, and guided by the following questions:
    1.) Can we uniquely reconstruct a signal f from its Fourier coefficients {fb(n)}n∈Z ?
    2.) If the Fourier series of f exhibits a certain behavior on a subset of T, does f
necessarily exhibit the same behavior there?
    It turns out that question 1.) admits a very satisfactory answer, which largely explains
the central role of Fourier analysis.

Corollary 2.8. Any µ ∈ M (T) is completely determined by its Fourier coefficients. In
other words, the map
                                 µ 7→ {b
                                       µ(n)}n∈Z

is injective.

Proof. By linearity, it suffices to consider µ ∈ M (T) such that
                                     Z
                            µ
                            b(n) =       ζ −n dµ(ζ) = 0,        n ∈ Z.
                                     T

By the Weierstrass approximation theorem, trigonometric polynomials are dense in C(T).
Hence µ annihilates all continuous functions on T, and therefore µ ≡ 0 by the duality
C(T)∗ ∼
      = M (T).

    In fact, the Weierstrass approximation theorem can be iterated to show that trigonomet-
ric polynomials are dense in the Banach space C k (T) of k-times continuously differentiable
functions on T, equipped with the norm

                                              k
                                              X
                                 ∥f ∥C k :=       sup |f (j) (ζ)|.
                                              j=0 ζ∈T


Indeed, once f ∈ C(T) can be approximated uniformly by trigonometric polynomials Tn , a
primitive of Tn (which is again a trigonometric polynomial) approximates a primitive of f
uniformly. Iterating this argument yields the desired density.
    As a consequence, any distribution S on T is uniquely determined by its Fourier coeffi-
cients
                                  := S eit 7→ e−int ,
                                                   
                             S(n)
                             b                           n ∈ Z.

   We now turn to question 2.). A first indication of the answer is provided by the following
localization principle.

Theorem 2.9 (Riemann’s localization principle). If f ∈ L1 (T) vanishes in a neighborhood
of a point ζ0 ∈ T, then
                           SN (f )(ζ0 ) → 0,    N → ∞.

   The proof is an immediate consequence of Dini’s criterion. An immediate consequence is
that if two functions f, g ∈ L1 (T) agree on an arc, then their Fourier series have essentially
the same behavior on any smaller subarc.
   We mention a classical uniqueness problem which has had a profound influence on the
development of real analysis. The problem was to understand which compact sets E ⊂ T
enjoy the following property: whenever (a(n))n∈Z is a bounded sequence such that the



                                               18
associated Fourier series satisfies
                               X
                   SN (ζ) :=           a(n)ζ n → 0,   N → ∞,     ζ ∈ T \ E,
                               |n|≤N


then necessarily a(n) = 0 for all n.
   Such sets are called sets of uniqueness. Note that the boundedness is necessary for the
Fourier series to converge anywhere on T.
   This problem came about from the work of B. Riemann, and was considered by G.
Cantor in the 1870s. Cantor proved that every closed countable set is a set of uniqueness.
Upon developing the Lebesgue integral, H. Lebesgue established the following results, which
are today regarded as fundamental in Fourier analysis.

Riemann–Lebesgue Lemma: If f ∈ L1 (T), then fb(n) → 0 as |n| → ∞.
Cantor–Lebesgue Lemma: If the partial sums |n|≤N an ζ n converge on a set of positive
                                          P

Lebesgue measure, then an → 0.

    We shall prove the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma in the next subsection. The Cantor–
Lebesgue lemma can be deduced from it and is left as an exercise.
    Lebesgue’s theory suggested that sets of measure zero should also be negligible for conver-
gence of Fourier series, and hence candidates for sets of uniqueness. However, this intuition
was disproved by D. Menshov, who constructed a compact set of Lebesgue measure zero
which is not a set of uniqueness. Phrased in modern terms, his result can be formulated as
follows:

Theorem 2.10 (Menshov). Let µ ∈ M (T) be non-trivial with µ
                                                          b(n) → 0 as |n| → ∞. Then
                               X
              SN (µ)(ζ) :=           b(n)ζ n → 0,
                                     µ                N → ∞,   ζ ∈ T \ supp(µ).
                             |n|≤N


In particular, any set containing supp(µ) cannot be a set of uniqueness.

    Here supp(µ) denotes the smallest compact set E ⊂ T such that |µ|(I) = 0 for every
open arc I ⊂ T \ E. This definition also coincides with the usual notion of support for
distributions.
    Combining the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma with Menshov’s theorem, we see that no set
of positive Lebesgue measure can be a set of uniqueness.
    This naturally leads to the study of sets that support singular measures µ whose Fourier
coefficients vanish at infinity. Such measures were systematically investigated by N. Bari and
A. Rajchman, who independently constructed examples supported on uncountable Cantor-
type sets. Further contributions were made by Ivashev-Musatov, Kahane, Rajchman, Rudin,
Piatetski-Shapiro, Salem, and Zygmund. A detailed historical account can be found in the
survey of T. W. Körner [61].


2.3    Some classical problems on translates
A recurring theme in harmonic analysis is the interplay between operators in the physical
domain and their counterparts on the Fourier side. A simple but fundamental model is the
relation between pointwise multiplication and translation of Fourier coefficients.


                                                 19
   Given f ∈ L1 (T), recall that multiplication by the monomial ζ n acts as a translation
operator on the Fourier coefficients:

                            ζn
                            df (m) = fb(m − n),                  m, n ∈ Z.

Thus the multiplication operator Mζ f (ζ) = ζf (ζ) induces translations on the Fourier side,
and its integer powers satisfy

                                  Mζn f (ζ) = ζ n f (ζ),         n ∈ Z,

where Mζ−1 f (ζ) = ζ f (ζ). More generally, if T is a trigonometric polynomial
                                            X
                                  T (ζ) =        Tb(n)ζ n ,       ζ ∈ T,
                                            n

then multiplication by T can be expressed as
                                         X
                         T (ζ)f (ζ) =           Tb(n) Mζn f (ζ),       ζ ∈ T.
                                            n

    In this subsection, we shall study subspaces of functions that are invariant under trans-
lations. To describe the general problem, let X be a Banach space of functions in L1 (T) for
which the multiplication operators Mζ , Mζ : X → X act continuously. The problem is to
describe the closed subspaces V of X which are invariant under translations:

                                    Mζ V ⊆ V,            Mζ V ⊆ V.

Phrased equivalently, we would like to classify the closed subspace V of X satisfying

                                                Mζ V = V.

In particular, we shall consider when a single function f ∈ X generates a dense invariant
subspace under this action, that is, when the linear span of the set

                     {ζ n f (ζ) : n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . . }           is dense in X.

These problems become particularly interesting in spaces X defined in terms of conditions
involving Fourier coefficients, or by restrictions on their Fourier support.
   We shall primarily be concerned with the problem in the most classical framework of
the Hilbert space L2 (T), normed by
                            Z                          X
                                  |f (ζ)|2 dm(ζ) =            |fb(n)|2 < ∞.
                              T                        n∈Z


    Our first result is a classical theorem of N. Wiener, which provides a complete classifi-
cation of closed subspaces of L2 (T) that are invariant under translation.

Theorem 2.11 (Wiener). Let V be a translation invariant closed subspace of L2 (T). Then




                                                    20
there exists a unique Lebesgue measurable subset S ⊂ T (modulo null-sets) such that

                        V = L2 (S) := {f ∈ L2 (T) : f = 0 dm-a.e off S}.

   It is worth to mention that Wiener’s Theorem also holds in the reflexive range of Lebesgue
spaces Lp (T) with 1 < p < ∞, but the proof needs to be slightly modified.

Proof. Let V be a non-trivial closed translation invariant subspace of L2 , and Π denote the
orthogonal projection from L2 onto V. Set f = Π(1) and note that 1 − f ∈ V ⊥ , hence
                   Z
                        (1 − f (ζ))f (ζ)ζ n dm(ζ) = 0,         n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .
                    T


But since the polynomials are dense in L2 , we conclude that f (1 − f ) = 0 dm-a.e on T,
hence f = |f |2 and therefore f = 1S for some Lebesgue measurable subset S ⊂ T, which
is uniquely determined up to sets of Lebesgue measure zero. Since V is closed, we clearly
have L2 (S) ⊆ V, hence it remains to prove that L2 (S) is dense in V. Let g ∈ V with the
property that g ⊥ L2 (S), which we may express as:
                         Z
                                g(ζ)ζ −n dm(ζ) = 0,       n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .
                            S


Then it follows that 1S g = 0 dm-a.e. But since 1T\S = 1 − f ∈ V ⊥ , we also have that
                        Z
                                 g(ζ)ζ −n dm(ζ) = 0,       n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .
                          T\S


hence g = 0 dm-a.e on T \ S, and we conclude that g = 0 dm-a.e on T. This proves that
L2 (S) = V, hence completing the proof.

    We now record the following corollary, which provides a complete answer to our question
in the classical setting of L2 (T).

Corollary 2.12. Let f ∈ L2 (T). Then the linear span of translates of f :

                                    {f (ζ)ζ n : n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . . }

is dense in L2 (T) if and only if f ̸= 0 dm-a.e on T.

   Next, we consider a unilateral version of Wiener’s theorem on translates in L2 (T), namely
the structure of closed subspaces V invariant under the multiplication operator Mζ :

                                              Mζ V ⊆ V.

A fundamental example of such a subspace is given by the celebrated Hardy space

                          H 2 (T) := {f ∈ L2 (T) : fb(n) = 0 for n < 0}.

    Since this space plays a crucial role, we will devote a few paragraphs explaining some
of its features. First, one can identify elements in H 2 (T) with analytic functions in the



                                                   21
unit-disc D having square summable Taylor coefficients centered at the origin. To explain
this connection, fix f ∈ H 2 (T) and consider its Poisson extension

                                          1 − |z|2
                                        Z
                         P (f )(z) :=             2
                                                    f (ζ) dm(ζ),         z ∈ D.
                                        T |ζ − z|

Using the identity

                     1 − |z|2       1        1
                             2
                               =        +        − 1,           ζ ∈ T,       z ∈ D,
                     |ζ − z|     1 − ζz   1 − ζz

the assumption on H 2 (T), and expanding into geometric series, one obtains
                                             X
                               P (f )(z) =         fb(n)z n ,    z ∈ D.
                                             n≥0


This shows that P (f ) is analytic in D, and by Parseval’s identity its Taylor coefficients are
square-summable. Conversely, every analytic function in D with square-summable Taylor
coefficients arises as the Poisson extension of a unique element f ∈ H 2 (T). Thus, H 2 (T) may
be identified isometrically with the Hilbert space of analytic functions on D with square-
summable Taylor coefficients, commonly denoted H 2 (D).
   In a similar way, one defines H ∞ (D) as the space of bounded analytic functions in D.
These are precisely the Poisson extensions of elements in

                          H ∞ (T) := {f ∈ L∞ (T) : fb(n) = 0, n < 0}.

In what follows, we allow the lax convention of not seriously distinguishing between these
different realizations of the Hardy spaces, allowing the context to clarify the interpretation,
and we often use the designation H 2 , and H ∞ .
    A key structural feature of H 2 is the inner–outer factorization: every nonzero f ∈ H 2
admits a unique (up to multiplication by unimodular constants) decomposition

                                  f (ζ) = Θ(ζ)F (ζ),            ζ∈T

where Θ ∈ H ∞ (T) is inner, meaning |Θ(ζ)| = 1 for dm-a.e ζ ∈ T, and F ∈ H 2 (T) is outer,
meaning that                               Z
                             log |F (0)| =
                                  b          log |F |dm > −∞,
                                                T

and is therefore uniquely determined by its boundary modulus |F | = |f |. Roughly speaking,
Θ is the argument of an H 2 -function, which captures the zero set and phase of f , while F
encodes its magnitude: |f | = |F | dm-a.e on T. These factorization have fairly explicit
expressions viewed as elements in H 2 (D). These details are deferred to the appendix, and
the reader is referred to [33, Ch. II] for a thorough treatment of Hardy space.

   The following celebrated structural Theorem by A. Beurling, gives a function theoretical
characterization of all closed subspace of H 2 which are invariant under Mζ .

Theorem 2.13 (Beurling, 1949). Let V be closed Mζ -invariant subspace of H 2 (T). Then




                                                    22
there exists a unique inner function Θ (up to a unimodular constant), such that

                                     V = ΘH 2 := {Θf : f ∈ H 2 }.

Proof. It follows that Mζ V is a closed subspace of V, and hence we have the orthogonal
decomposition
                                   V = Mζ V ⊕ (Mζ V)⊥ .

Suppose, for contradiction, that (Mζ V)⊥ = {0}, or equivalently that Mζ V = V. Then for
any f ∈ V with ∥f ∥H 2 = 1 and any integer n ≥ 1, there exists gn ∈ V with ∥gn ∥H 2 = 1 such
that
                                f (z) = z n gn (z),  z ∈ D.

Iterating, this forces f to vanish to arbitrarily high order at the origin, which is impossible
unless f ≡ 0. This contradiction shows that Mζ V ⊊ V. Consequently, there exists a nonzero
element f ∈ (Mζ V)⊥ . By definition, it follows that
             Z                            Z
                  |f (ζ)|2 ζ −n dm(ζ) =       f (ζ)f (ζ)ζ n dm(ζ) = 0,        n = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
              T                           T


Taking complex conjugates, we conclude that the non-zero Fourier coefficients of |f |2 are
zero, hence |f | = fb(0) ̸= 0 dm-a.e. Set Θ = f /fb(0), which by definition is an inner function.
Since V is closed it easily follows that ΘH 2 ⊆ V, hence it remains only to verify that ΘH 2
is dense in V. Let g ∈ V with the property that
                           Z
                              Θ(ζ)ζ n g(ζ)dm(ζ) = 0,     n = 0, 1, 2, . . .
                               T


Recall that since Θ ∈ (Mζ V)⊥ by definition, we also have that
                           Z
                                Θ(ζ)g(ζ)ζ n dm(ζ) = 0,         n = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
                            T

But these two conditions readily imply that Θg = 0, and since Θ is unimodular, we conclude
that g = 0 dm-a.e on T. It follows that V = ΘH 2 .

    We record the following consequence of Beurling’s Theorem, which will appear useful at
a later stage.

Corollary 2.14. For f ∈ H 2 , the linear span of {f (ζ)ζ n : n = 0, 1, 2, . . . } is dense in H 2 ,
if and only if f is outer.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Beurling’s Theorem and the uniqueness of
the inner–outer factorization in H 2 . If f is outer, then smallest Mζ -invariant subspace
containing f cannot be of the form ΘH 2 , unless Θ is a unimodular constant.

    Using Beurling’s Theorem as a stepping stone, we may now complete the characterization
of all closed subspace of L2 (T), which are invariant under Mζ .

Corollary 2.15 (Beurling–Wiener). Let V be a closed Mζ -invariant subspace of L2 (T). If




                                                     23
V = Mζ V, then there exists a unique Lebesgue measure subset S ⊂ T, such that

                                            V := L2 (S).

If Mζ V ⊊ V, then there exists a unique unimodular function U ∈ L∞ (T) (i.e |U | = 1 dm-a.e
on T) such that
                              V = U H 2 := {U f : f ∈ H 2 (T)}.

Proof. The first case V = Mζ V asserts that V is, in fact, translation invariant, and hence is
just a re-statement of Wiener’s Theorem.
    Moving forward, we thus assume that Mζ V ⊊ V. Arguing as in the proof of Beurling’s
Theorem, we can pick a non-trivial element f ∈ (Mζ V)⊥ , and deduce that f = cU , where
c ̸= 0 constant and U is a unimodular function on T. It is easy to see that U H 2 ⊆ V, hence
it only remains to show that U H 2 is dense in V. However, that remaining part of the proof
is again similar to Beurling’s Theorem, we omit the details.

   We can now describe all functions in L2 (T) which are cyclic wrt to Mζ in L2 (T). To this
end, we make use of the following fact: if f ∈ L2 (T) has integrable logarithm:
                                     Z
                                           log |f |dm > −∞,
                                       T


then there exists a function F ∈ H 2 such that |F | = |f | on T, and thus f = U F , for some
unimodular U ∈ L∞ (T).

Theorem 2.16. Let f ∈ L2 (T). Then the linear span of the set

                                  {ζ n f (ζ) : n = 0, 1, 2, . . . }

is dense in L2 (T) if and only if f ̸= 0 dm-a.e on T and
                                                                R
                                                                  T
                                                                      log |f |dm = −∞.

Proof. We denote by [f ] be the smallest closed Mζ -invariant subspace in L2 (T) containing
f . We first argue that the conditions on f are necessary. If f = 0 on a subset S of positive
Lebesgue measure in T, then it easily follows that [f ] ⊆ L2 (T\S), which is a proper subspace
of L2 (T), hence f cannot be cyclic. On the other hand, if
                                     Z
                                           log |f |dm > −∞,
                                       T


then an outer function F ∈ H 2 (T) can be constructed so that F = U f for some unimodular
function U ∈ L∞ (T). But then [f ] = [F ] ⊆ H 2 (T), which again is a proper subspace of
L2 (T).
    Conversely, assume that f ∈ L2 (T) with f ̸= 0 dm-a.e on T, and log |f | ∈
                                                                             / L1 (T). Now
the logarithmic divergence of f implies that [f ] cannot take the form U H 2 (T) for some
unimodular function U , since non-trivial elements in H 2 (T) have integrable logarithms.
Furthermore, since f vanishes almost nowhere on T, [f ] can neither be of the form L2 (S)
for some measurable subset S ⊂ T which does not have full Lebesgue measure in T. The
Beurling–Wiener Theorem then ensures that [f ] = L2 (T), which proves the claim.




                                                24
2.4    Ideals in the Wiener algebra
Moving forward, we shall now consider the problem of translations in the context of the
Wiener algebra A(T), which consists of functions f ∈ C(T) with absolutely summable
Fourier coefficients:                   X
                               ∥f ∥A :=    |fb(n)| < ∞.
                                             n∈Z

In other words, the space A(T) is isometrically isomorphic with the sequence space ℓ1 via
Fourier coefficients. The designation ”algebra” attached to the space A(T) arises from the
fact that A(T) is closed under pointwise multiplication, as demonstrated by the estimate
                                    X X
                       ∥f · g∥A =                 g (n − k) ≤ ∥f ∥A ∥g∥A .
                                             fb(k)b
                                    n    k

In fact, the Wiener algebra is one of most prominent example in which Harmonic analysis
meets the theory of (commutative) Banach algebras. In this more algebraic setting, closed
translation invariant subspace of A(T) are most commonly referred to as closed ideals.
Examples of closed ideals are given by

                     IE := {f ∈ A(T) : f = 0 on E}           E ⊆ T compact.                (3)

    A complete characterization of all closed ideals in A(T), in a similar spirit to Beurling’s
Theorem, is unfortunately not available here. However, one can still obtain a result which
is somewhat close to complete, as we shall see in ,
    Let us primarily attempt the simpler task of classifying the so-called maximal ideals in
A(T). Recall that an ideal M is said to be maximal, if whenever I is an ideal with

                                        M ⊆ I ⊆ A(T)

then wither I = M or I = A(T). Note that maximal ideal must necessarily be closed. The
principal result in this regime shows that all maximal ideals are of a fairly simple form.

Theorem 2.17 (Maximal ideals). Every maximal ideal in A(T) is of the form

                          Iζ0 = {f ∈ A(T) : f (ζ0 ) = 0}         ζ0 ∈ T.

   In modern times, this result is typically proved using Banach algebra techniques, notably
the so-called Gelfand transform. But we shall here remain within the realm of harmonic
analysis.
   The proof of this result relies on classical result of Wiener, which typically goes under
the name of Wiener’s 1/f -Theorem, and neatly characterizes all cyclic elements on A(T).

Theorem 2.18 (Wiener). For a function f ∈ A(T), the following conditions are all equiv-
alent:

  (i) f ̸= 0 on T.

 (ii) f is cyclic in A(T), that is, the closed linear span of

                                    {f (ζ)ζ n : n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . . }


                                                25
       is dense in A(T).

(iii) f is invertible in A(T), that is, 1/f ∈ A(T).

    We remark that the principal part of this Theorem is the statement (i) =⇒ (iii),
and modern proofs of this result typically appears in the literature of commutative Banach
algebras, which originates from the profound work of I. Gelfand. For instance, see [50, Ch.
VIII]. However, we shall below outline a simple, short and most importantly, a self-contained
proof by D. J. Newman in [80]. It essentially only requires the following simple lemma.

Lemma 2.19. Let W 1,∞ (T) denote the Sobolev space of essentially bounded functions f on
T whose distributional derivative is also essentially bounded on T, equipped with the norm:

                                  ∥f ∥W 1,∞ := ∥f ∥L∞ + ∥f ′ ∥L∞ .

Then there exists a numerical constant C > 0, such that the following norm inequalities
hold:
                    ∥f ∥L∞ ≤ ∥f ∥A ≤ C∥f ∥W 1,∞    ∀f ∈ W 1,∞ (T).

Proof. The proof is simple and principally based on the following observation that follows
from Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
                                         !1/2                         !1/2        Z                    1/2
 X                X                                 X        1                           2     ′ 2
      |fb(n)| ≤    (1 + |n|)2 |fb(n)|2          ·                            ≤C        |f | + |f | dm          .
  n                n                                n
                                                         (1 + |n|)2                T


We omit the simple details.

Proof of Theorem 2.18. Step 1: (iii) implies (ii):
Assume 1/f ∈ A(T) and approximate 1/f by trigonometric polynomials in the norm of
A(T). Now since A(T) is a Banach algebra closed under pointwise multiplication, which
corresponds to convolution on the Fourier side, multiplying by f we see that polynomial
multiples of f approximate 1 in the A(T)–norm. Now since the polynomials are dense, we
conclude that f is indeed cyclic.

Step 2: (ii) =⇒ (i)
Arguing by contraposition, we note that if f (ζ0 ) = 0, then the product f T with T being a
trigonometric polynomial can never uniformly approximate a function g with g(ζ0 ) ̸= 0 on
T.

Step 3: (i) =⇒ (iii)
Below, we shall outline a neat argument by D. J. Newman. Suppose f ̸= 0 on T and note
that by means of re-scaling f , we may assume that |f (ζ)| ≥ 1 on T. Fix a number 0 < ε < 1
to be specified later, and pick a trigonometric polynomials Pε such that ∥f − Pε ∥A ≤ ε.
Now using the following simple algebraic manipulation, it remains to show that the formal
series expansion

actually converges in the A(T)-norm. First, we should at least verify that 1/Pε ∈ C(T), but
this readily follows from the normalization of f :

          ε ≥ ∥f − Pε ∥A ≥ ∥f − Pε ∥L∞ ≥ |f (ζ)| − |Pε (ζ)| ≥ 1 − |Pε (ζ)|,                 ζ ∈ T,

and thus ∥1/Pε ∥L∞ ≤ (1 − ε)−1 . Furthermore, if M (ε) := ∥Pε′ ∥L∞ , then

    We mention the following remarkable extension of Wieners 1/f -Theorem, which was
originally established by P. Levy.

Theorem 2.20 (Levy–Wiener). If f ∈ A(T) and F is an analytic function in a neighborhood
of the range of f , then the composition F (f ) belongs to A(T).

Sketch of proof: One can imitate Newman’s argument in the proof of Wiener’s 1/f -Theorem,
now using the Taylor series expansion
                                                              ∞

                                                              n=0
                                                                      n!

where the trigonometric polynomial Pε are chosen so that ∥f − Pε ∥A is substantially smaller
than the radius of convergence of F . We leave the details to the interested reader.

   With Wiener’s 1/f -Theorem at our disposal, we proceed to characterize all maximal
ideals in the Wiener algebra.

   We now prove that the closed ideals in A(T) are essentially characterized by zero sets.

        JE := Clos{f ∈ A(T) : f = 0 in some open nbh of E},                    E ⊆ T compact.

    We conclude this section by presenting an alternative perspective on Wiener’s 1/f -
Theorem, viewing it as a Tauberian theorem. This interpretation is not immediately appar-
ent from the classical formulation. Broadly speaking, Tauberian theorems aim to identify
additional conditions under which a weaker mode of convergence can be strengthened to
a stronger one. Historically, A. Tauber showed that if a sequence of complex numbers cn
converges to 0 in the Cesàro sense and satisfies the Tauberian condition cn = o(1/n), then
in fact cn → 0. Subsequently, J. E. Littlewood proved that this condition can be weak-
ened to the corresponding big-O estimate. A general form of Wiener’s Tauberian theorem,
formulated in the setting of sequences, can be stated as follows.

Theorem 2.22 (Wiener Taburian Theorem). Let f ∈ A(T) with f ̸= 0 on T, and assume




                                                29
that (bn )n is a bounded sequence of complex numbers, for which the following limit exists:
                                     X                        X
                               lim        fb(n − k)bk = L           fb(k).
                              n→∞
                                      k                       k

Then the limit                            X                   X
                                  lim          an−k bk = L         ak ,
                                 n→∞
                                           k                  k

exists for all complex-valued sequences {an }n ∈ ℓ1 .

    In other words, the Wiener Tauberian Theorem asserts that if a bounded sequence
converges in certain mean with respect to a cyclic element in A(T), then it can be upgraded
to converge in any such mean. For instance, one can take f (ζ) = (1 − rζ)−1 with 0 < r < 1,

Proof of Theorem 2.22. Let I denote the set of all g ∈ A(T) with the property that the
limit exists                    X                   X
                            lim    gb(n − k)bk = L     gb(k).
                              n→∞
                                      k                       k

Note that I is a linear space, invariant under translation. Furthermore, it also closed in
A(T), hence it is a closed ideal, which by assumptions contains a function f ∈ A(T) with
f ̸= 0 on T. According to Wiener’s Theorem we conclude that I contains a cyclic element
f , but is easily seen to imply that I = A(T).


2.5    Lacunary Fourier series and Riesz products
General Fourier series can behave pretty badly and are somewhat too broad of a class to
study in some meaningful way. Here, we restriction our attention to special class of Fourier
series with very sparse support. We shall illustrate they exhibit more clear-cut dichotomies
in regards to various properties.
    Let Λ ⊂ Z is a subset of lacunary integers, that is, integers with are exponentially sparse:
                                                                         
                                          |n|
                        κ(Λ) := inf           : m, n ∈ Λ, |n| > |m|           > 1.
                                          |m|

We now consider an associated lacunary trigonometric series of the form
                                           X
                               F (ζ) :=          Fb(n)ζ n ,       ζ ∈ T.
                                           n∈Λ


In other words, Lacunary series are trigonometric series with exponentially sparse Fourier
non-zero Fourier coefficients. A concrete example of an exponentially sparse sequence is
{±3j }j , which gives rise to a lacunary trigonometric series (sometimes also called Hadamard
gap series).
   A key feature of lacunary series is that they exhibit a local-to-global phenomenon. This
can be viewed as a manifestation of the uncertainty principle:

Principle 2.23. If the Fourier spectrum is highly compressed (in the sense of large lacunary
spectral gaps), then the function’s spatial properties, whether good (regularity) or bad
(singularity), cannot remain localized. In fact, they must spread out maximally along the
unit circle.


                                                   30
   The following classical results clarifies one aspect of this point.

Theorem 2.24 (Ostrowski–Hadamard). Let {Nj }j be lacunary sequence of positive integers
and let F be an analytic function in the unit-disc D defined by
                                          X
                               F (z) :=          aj z Nj ,         z ∈ D.
                                             j


If F extends analytically across a single arc in T, then it extends analytically across T.

Theorem 2.25 (Hadamard–Ostrowski). Let (Nj ) be a lacunary sequence and
                                                   ∞
                                                   X
                                       F (z) =           aj z Nj
                                                   j=1


analytic in D. If F extends analytically across a point of T, then it extends analytically
across all of T.

   The proof presented below is a compressed version of Rudin’s proof in [88, Theorem
16.6].

Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that F extends analytically across a
neighborhood of ζ = 1. Since (Nj ) is lacunary, we may choose an integer λ ≥ 1 such that
                                    1
                          Nj+1 > 1 +    Nj ,                 j = 1, 2, 3, . . . .
                                     λ
Define the analytic polynomial

                            φ(w) := 12 wλ + wλ+1 ,
                                                
                                                                     w ∈ C,

and set
                                       G(z) := F (φ(z)).

   We claim that G extends analytically across the unit circle. To see this, we note that

                            |φ(w)| < 1       for w ∈ D,            φ(1) = 1,

and
                                   φ′ (1) = 12 (2λ + 1) ̸= 0,

it follows that φ is conformal near w = 1, and hence maps a neighborhood of 1 onto a
neighborhood of 1. Since F extends analytically across 1, the composition G = F ◦ φ is
therefore analytic in a neighborhood of the closed unit disc. In particular, there exists ε > 0
such that
                                    X∞
                             G(z) =      bm z m ,   |z| < 1 + ε.
                                     m=0

On the other hand, we also have by definition that
                                       X
                              G(z) =          aj φ(z)Nj ,           z ∈ D.
                                         j




                                                   31
For each j, a binomial expansion of the polynomial φ(z)Nj shows that it only contains
monomial terms with exponents in

                                      λNj ≤ m ≤ (λ + 1)Nj .

Invoking the lacunary assumption (λ + 1)Nj < λNj+1 , we get that these intervals are
pairwise disjoint, hence the following holds:
                   X                             X
                          ak φ(z)Nk =                      bm z m ,          j = 1, 2, 3, . . .
                  0≤k≤j                  0≤m≤(λ+1)Nj


As the right-hand side converges uniformly for |z| < 1 + ε, so does the partial sums of the
lacunary series. We conclude that F extends analytically to some disc |z| > 1.

    Moving forward, we now consider the behavior of lacunary trigonometric series. For
simplicity, we shall solely restrict our attention to lacunary sequences Λ with κ(Λ) ≥ 3, but
essentially all results presented here holds in general for κ(Λ) ≥ 1, but are more technical.
    We shall now further illustrate that lacunary series are completely determined by their
local behavior, where the notion of local depends on the rate of decay of the coefficients.

Theorem 2.26 (Zygmund). Let Λ ⊂ Z be a lacunary set of integers, and f ∈ L1 (T) with
Fourier spectrum in Λ. Then there exists a constant C(Λ) > 0 such that
                                                 !1/2            Z
                                X
                                             2
                                    |fb(n)|             ≤ C(Λ)           |f |dm.
                                n∈Λ                                  T


Furthermore, for any Lebesgue measurable subset E ⊂ T of positive Lebesgue measure, there
exists a constant C(Λ, E) > 0, and a number N (E) > 0 such that
                               X                                 Z
                                         |fb(n)|2 ≤ C(Λ, E)              |f |2 dm.                (4)
                                n∈Λ                               E
                             |n|>N (E)


   We now record the following important corollary.

Corollary 2.27. Let Λ ⊂ Z be lacunary integers and consider the lacunary series
                                           X
                                f (ζ) =           fb(n)ζ n ,      ζ ∈ T.
                                           n∈Λ


The the following statements hold:

  (i) If the series of f converges pointwise on a set of positive Lebesgue measure, then
      f ∈ L2 (T).

 (ii) If f ∈ L2 (T) vanishes on a set of positive Lebesgue measure, then it vanishes identically
      in T.

Proof. The proof of (ii) is immediate from the local estimate in (4). To see (i), assume
  / L2 (T) but yet its Fourier partials sums (Sn ) converge pointwise on a set E of positive
f ∈
Lebesgue measure in T. By means of slightly sacrificing the size of E, we may upon invoking

Egoroff’s Theorem assume that the (Sn ) converge uniformly on E. But this is in violating
with (4).

    Note that in view of Carleson’s Theorem on convergence of Fourier series, the converse
of (i) is also true. However, one can give a much simpler proof for lacunary Fourier series,
using Littlewood–Paley Theory. We shall not do it here, and refer the reader to Zygmund
in [97].
    Turning our attention to proving Zygmund’s Theorem 2.26, we shall need to introduce

reader may also attempt to deduce this claim from Zygmund’s Theorem 2.26.
    In summary, the Lebesgue decompositions of a Riesz product is either purely singular
wrt dm, or purely absolutely continuous and with a density in L2 (T).

    We mention on the passing that using the theory of Paley–Littlewood square functions,

    We shall now consider yet another result of A. Zygmund, which characterizes when
lacunary series are essential bounded. It turns out that every essentially bounded function
f with Fourier coefficients supported on a lacunary set is automatically continuous on T.

   We shall now record yet another manifestation of the classical uncertainty principle on
the simultaneous smallness of f and its Fourier coefficients. The following corollary can be
proved using a simple adaptation of the proof used to establish the second statement of
Theorem 2.28, and whose details are left to the reader.

Corollary 2.29 (Zygmund). Let µ ∈ M (T) with Fourier coefficients supported in a lacunary
subset of integers. If |µ|(I) = 0 for some arc I ⊂ T, then µ ≡ 0.

   In other words, measures with lacunary Fourier spectrum must have full support, while
measures with non-full support cannot have sparse Fourier spectrum.
   We now close this subsection by clarifying how smoothness properties of lacunary series
can also be encoded from their Fourier coefficients, thus we have yet another local-to-global
phenomenon is this regime.

Theorem 2.30 (Paley–Zygmund). Let {λk }k ⊂ Z be a lacunary set, and f ∈ L∞ with
Fourier coefficients supported in Λ. Then for any number 0 < α < 1, the following state-
ments are all equivalent:

  (i) f is α-Hölder continuous on T,

 (ii) f is α-Hölder continuous at a single point ζ0 ∈ T,

(iii) fb(λk ) = O(|λk |−α ), as |k| → ∞.




                                                         37
Proof. Let {λk }k be an increasing enumeration of Λ. We only need to prove (ii) =⇒ (iii)
and (iii) =⇒ (i).

rem 2.28 implies that f ∈ C(T) with the property that there exists C > 0 such that

                                     |f (ζ) − f (1)| ≤ C|ζ − 1|α ,          ζ ∈ T.

                          fb(n) = 0,         1 ≤ |n − λk | ≤ c|λk |,            k = 1, 2, 3, . . .

For sufficiently large |λk |, we can pick integers c|λk |/2 ≤ Nk < c|λk | and note that one may

                                                         38
   We remark that if f is a general α-Hölder continuous function on T, then we have the
Fourier decay
                             fb(n) = O(|n|−α ),    |n| → ∞.

However, the converse is far from true in this generality. There exists positive measures µ
in T supported in sets of Lebesgue measure zero, hence singular wrt dm, such that

                               b(n) = O(|n|−α ),
                               µ                       |n| → ∞.

In fact, the Ivashev–Musatov Theorem in Section 6 proves something much stronger.


2.6    Fourier coefficients of measures
We now turn to a systematic study of the behavior of Fourier coefficients of elements in
M (T), the space of finite complex Borel measures on T. Within this framework, our goal
is to explore the interplay between localization, as reflected in the smallness of the support,
and regularity, as captured by the decay of the Fourier coefficients.
    Recall that the classical Lebesgue decomposition from measure theory allows us to de-
compose any µ ∈ M (T) of the form:

                                    dµ = f dm + dµc + dµd

where f ∈ L1 (T) denotes the Radon-Nikodym derivative of µ wrt the arc length measure
dm, µc denotes the continuous singular part of µ, while µd denotes the discrete part, written
as a countable sum of atomic masses. As customary in the literature, f dm is the absolutely
continuous part of µ. The sum µs := µc +µd as the singular part of µ. Measures with trivial
discrete part µd , that is, of the form dµ = f dm + dµc are typically said to be continuous
measures.
    We shall start out with, perhaps, one of the most basic and classical observation in
Fourier analysis.

Lemma 2.31 (Riemann–Lebesgue). For any f ∈ L1 (T) we have

                                         lim fb(n) = 0.
                                        |n|→∞


   To prove the lemma, we shall use the following simple and observation: if f is k-times
continuously differentiable function on T, then repeated integration by parts gives:
                               Z
               f (n) = (−in)
               b             k
                                  f (k) (ζ)ζ −n dm(ζ) = O(|n|−k ),  |n| → ∞.
                                T


Proof. Approximate f by a smooth function fε in the norm of L1 (T), for instance by taking
convolution of f with an approximate of the identity. Integrating the Fourier coefficients of
fε by parts (once is enough) and using the triangle inequality, we get

                  |fb(n)| ≤ |fbε (n)| + ∥f − fε ∥L1 ≤ C(ε)|n|−1 + ∥f − fε ∥L1 .

The claim now follows by sending |n| → ∞ first, and then ε → 0+.



                                                39
   Note that if f ∈ L2 (T) then its Fourier coefficients must tend to zero, in view of Parseval’s
identity:                                   Z
                             X
                                 |f (n)| = |f (ζ)|2 dm(ζ) < ∞.
                                  b     2

                                    n                T

This simple observation, in conjunction with L2 (T) being a dense subset of L1 (T), yields
yet another (though principally similar) proof of the Riemann–Lebesgue Lemma. Now it is
natural to ask whether the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma can be improved in some quantitative
way. The following result refutes this in the strongest way possible.

Theorem 2.32. For any positive numbers (w(n))∞

below is much simpler, but it still requires some level of clear-headedness.

   Next, we shall investigate the Fourier coefficients of measures with a non-trivial discrete
component. The following lemma quantifies how a single point mass can be detected via

  As an immediate consequence, we obtain the following corollary, which characterizes

Corollary 2.34 (Wiener). Let µ ∈ M (T). Then
                                  X                                        X

                                     1     X

   Roughly speaking, Wiener’s Theorem asserts that for any ε > 0, the amplitudes |b
                                                                                  µ(n)| ≥
ε have zero density:
                               # {|n| ≤ N : |b
