                          lim                            = 0.
                         N →∞          2N + 1
However, it turns out that the Fourier coefficients of a continuous measure do not have to
be asymptotically small.

Proposition 2.35 (A continuous measure with non-vanishing amplitudes). There are con-
tinuous measures µ ∈ M (T) with µ
                                b(n) ↛ 0 as |n| → ∞.

Proof. We construct a general recipe using Riesz products. Let (ak )k be positive numbers
in (0, 1] consider an associated Riesz product of type (R1 ):

                                              N                          
                                              Y                         j
                        dµ(ζ) := lim                       1 + aj Re(ζ 3 ) ,              ζ ∈ T,
                                    N →∞
                                              j=1


interpreted as the weak-star cluster point in M (T). Now if we impose the condition
                                                   X
                                                            a2j = ∞,
                                                       j


then a classical result of A. Zygmund asserts that µ is singular wrt dm on T. For instance,
see [97]. Now to see that µ is continuous, we use the remark prior to Lemma 2.33:

                      X                           k
                                                  Y
               3−k             µ(n)|2 ≤ 3−k                1 + a2j ≤ 3−k 2k → 0,
                                                                  
                              |b                                                               k → ∞,
                     |n|≤3k                       j=1


hence we conclude that µ is a continuous singular probability measure on T. Now a successive
expansion of the associated Riesz partial product PN imply that for each integer N ≥ 1:
                                                                           
                                                  PN                                     2         N
                                                                          aj  ζ (3ε1 +3 ε2 +···+3 εN )
                                  X                             Y
                                              −    j=1 |εj |
                PN (ζ) = 1 +             2
                                  ε̸=0                         j:εj ̸=0


where the sum is taken over all non-trivial tuples ε := (ε1 , ε2 , . . . , εN ) ∈ {−1, 0, 1}N . Now
if we impose that aj → 0, then we see that µ b(n) → 0 as |n| → ∞, while if we pick aj ≡ 1,
then lim supn→∞ µb(n) > 0.



                                                             42
    Nevertheless, it turns out that there is a rigidity phenomenon for Fourier decay of mea-
sures. Roughly speaking, if the positive Fourier coefficients of a measure are asymptotically
small, then the same must hold for the negative coefficients. This is made precise by the
following result.

Theorem 2.36 (Rajchman, Milicer–Gruzewska). If a measure µ ∈ M (T) satisfies

                                            lim µ
                                                b(n) = 0,
                                          n→+∞


then it automatically also satisfies

                                            lim µ
                                                b(n) = 0.
                                          n→−∞


   Measure with the property µ  b(n) → 0 as |n| → ∞ are called Rajchman measures, and
have historically played a tremendous role in studying uniqueness problems of Fourier series.

Proof of Theorem 2.36. Let µ ∈ M (T) with the property that µb(n) → 0 as n → ∞. Then
for any trigonometric polynomial T , we have also have
                 X                    Z
                          µ(n − k) =
                     Tb(k)b             T (ζ)ζ −n dµ(ζ) → 0,   n → ∞,
                    k                       T


hence measures of the form dµT := T dµ also satisfy µc     T (n) → ∞ as n → ∞. The principal
idea is to show that the Fourier coefficients of the total-variation |µ| of µ also tends to zero as
n → ∞, by means of approximating |µ| by measures of the form µT in total variation-norm.
Once that has been accomplished, we simply utilize the fact that since |µ| is real-valued,
thus
                             |µ|(n)
                              c     = |µ|(−n)
                                        c        → 0,       n → ∞,

for which it easily follows that limn→−∞ µ b(n) = 0.
    To this end, recall that there exists a bounded Borel measurable function ϕ on T with
|ϕ| = 1 µ-a.e and such that d|µ| = ϕdµ. This is essentially the polar decomposition of a
measure.
    Now, a classical Theorem of Lusin in measure theory asserts that for any ε > 0, there
exists ψε ∈ C(T) such that supT |ψε | ≤ 1 and

                                     |µ| ({ζ ∈ T : ϕ ̸= ψε }) ≤ ε.

Passing to appropriate Fejér means of ψε , we can may exhibit trigonometric polynomials Tε
such ∥ψε − Tε ∥∞ ≤ ε. It follows that
                          Z                     Z
         ∥|µ| − µTε ∥ ≤       |ϕ − Tε |d|µ| ≤              |ϕ − ψε |d|µ| + ∥µ∥ε ≤ (2 + ∥µ∥) ε.
                                                {ϕ̸=ψε }


With this estimate in mind, we obtain

                  ||µ|(n)|
                   c       ≤ ∥|µ| − µTε ∥ + µ
                                           |dTε (n)| ≤ (2 + ∥µ∥)ε + µ
                                                                   |dTε (n)|.


Sending n → ∞ first, and then ε → 0+, the claim follows.


                                                    43
  Actually, a simple refinement of the above argument shows that the class of Rajchman
measures are stable under absolute continuity.

Corollary 2.37 (Rajchman, Milicer-Gruzewska). Let µ, ν ∈ M (T), with ν << µ. Then

                           lim µ
                               b(n) = 0            =⇒    lim νb(n) = 0.
                         |n|→∞                          |n|→∞


    For further quantitative refinements of the above results, we refer the reader to the work
of de Leeuw and Katznelson in [28].
    On the contrary, it is natural to ask whether there corresponds to any sequence of
complex numbers {cn }n with lim|n|→∞ cn = 0, as measure µ ∈ M (T) such that

                                    µ
                                    b(n) = cn ,         n ∈ Z.

It turns out that not all sequences tending to zero arise as the amplitudes of a measure.

Proposition 2.38. There exists a sequence of positive numbers {cn }n with lim|n|→∞ cn = 0,
which is not the Fourier coefficients of a complex finite Borel measure on T.

Proof. Step 1: Trigonometric polynomials with large mass and small amplitudes:

   It turns out that can much further and prove the following remarkable result, initially
proved in [82].

Theorem 2.39 (Piatetski–Shapiro). There exists a compact set E ⊂ T of Lebesgue measure
zero, which satisfies the following properties:

  (i) E support no non-trivial Rajchman measure,

 (ii) E supports a non-trivial distribution S with

                                          S(n)
                                          b    → 0,          |n| → ∞.


   A neat and modern proof of this theorem was later given by J-P. Kahane, and a carefully
outlined argument similar to that is contained in the PhD-Thesis of N. Lev, see [67, Ch. 1].


2.7    Further results
There is a general principle linking pointwise almost everywhere convergence to weak-type
estimates for an associated maximal operator. Suppose {Tα }α is a family of linear operators
acting on a Lebesgue space Lp (X, µ), where (X, µ) is a measure space. Define the associated
maximal (sublinear) operator by

                                T ∗ f (x) := sup |Tα f (x)|,         x ∈ X.
                                                 α

If T ∗ satisfies a weak-type estimate of the form

                                                         ∥f ∥pLp
                         µ {x ∈ X : |T ∗ f (x)| > λ} ≤ C
                                                    
                                                                 ,                λ > 0,
                                                           λp




                                                      45
then the set
                                                                                      
                           p
           A :=       f ∈ L (X, µ) :    lim Tα f (x) = f (x) exists for µ-a.e. x ∈ X
                                       α→α(0)


is closed in Lp (X, µ). The proof is simple, and can be found in [29, P. 27].
    If, in addition, one knows that limα→α(0) Tα f (x) = f (x) holds µ-a.e. for all f in a dense
subset of Lp (X, µ), it follows that A = Lp (X, µ).
    This principle underlies the proof of Carleson’s theorem. In that setting, one studies the
maximal partial sum operator

                        S ∗ f (ζ) := sup |SN f (ζ)|,   ζ ∈ T,    f ∈ L2 (T).
                                    N ≥1


Since pointwise convergence is immediate for smooth functions on T, which form a dense
subset of L2 (T), the central difficulty lies in establishing the corresponding weak-type esti-
mate for the associated maximal operator S ∗ .

    We note that Beurling’s theorem on Mζ -invariant subspaces extends to the Hardy spaces
  p
H for all p > 0, with the same description. In the case p = ∞, one must soften the topology
on H ∞ (to make it separable) and consider weak-star closed Mζ -invariant subspaces.
    Beurling’s theorem naturally raises the question of whether an analogous result holds
in other spaces of analytic functions, most notably the Bergman space L2a (D) consisting of
analytic functions f on D with finite area integral
                                   Z
                                       |f (z)|2 dA(z) < ∞.
                                           D


In this setting, the correspondence with boundary values available in the Hardy space H 2 (D)
is lost, and one no longer has access to a simple inner–outer factorization. Nevertheless,
H. Hedenmalm in [43] and B. Korenblum in [56], showed that there are some forms of
factorizations that still persist.
    In analogy with Beurling’s theorem, one studies closed Mz -invariant subspaces of L2a (D),
where Mz f (z) = zf (z). It was shown by C. Apostol, H. Bercovici, C. Foias, , and C. Pearcy
that there exist closed Mz -invariant subspaces V of L2a (D) for which the wandering subspace

                                       V ⊖ Mz V := V ∩ (Mz V)⊥

is infinite dimensional. In particular, Mz -invariant subspaces of L2a (D) are far from being
generated by a single function, in contrast with Hardy space.
    Despite this, Aleman, Richter, and Sundberg proved that a meaningful analogue of
Beurling’s theorem still holds in L2a (D), see [3]. More precisely, every closed Mz -invariant
subspace V is wandering in the sense of Halmos, that is, V is the smallest Mz -invariant
subspace containing V ⊖ Mz V.
    We also mention the influential work of H. Hedenmalm on singly generated and zero-
based Mz -invariant subspaces [38], which laid much of the foundation for modern Bergman
space theory. For further background on Bergman spaces, see [40].
    Given a subset of integers Λ, we denote by CΛ (T) the closed subspace of continuous



                                                  46
functions f on T whose Fourier coefficients are supported in the frequencies Λ:

                       CΛ (T) := {f ∈ C(T) : fb(n) = 0,                  n ̸= Λ}.

We say that Λ is a Sidon set if there exists a constant C(Λ) > 0 such that
                       X
                            |fb(n)| ≤ C(Λ) sup |f (ζ)|,              ∀f ∈ CΛ (T).
                        n                    ζ∈T


As we have seen, lacunary sequences Λ = (Nk )k satisfying inf k Nk+1 /Nk > 1, are Sidon
sets. A striking arithmetic characterization of Sidon sets was obtained by G. Pisier in [83].
    For an integer n, let R(n, Λ) denote the number of representations of n as a finite sum
                                                  X
                                           n=            εj λj ,
                                                    j


where λj ∈ Λ and εj ∈ {−1, 0, 1}. Pisier showed that Λ is a Sidon set if and only if there
exists 0 < γ < 1 such that, for every finite subset Γ ⊂ Λ,

                                      sup R(n, Γ) ≤ 3γ|Γ| .
                                      n∈Z


    In other words, additive relations among elements of Λ must be exponentially sparse.
J. Bourgain also proved some intimate connections to associated Riesz products, in [16]. See
also Kronecker’s theorem on independent sets in Chapter VI of [50].

   A very deep result which extends Theorem 2.25 of Hadamard and Ostrowski on natural
boundaries for lacunary Taylor series, is the following result:

Theorem 2.40 (Fabry’s gap Theorem). Let {Nj }∞
                                             j=1 be an increasing sequence of positive
integers satisfying
                                 lim Nj /j = ∞.
                                        j→∞

If R > 0 denotes the radius of convergence of the analytic power series
                                           ∞
                                           X
                                F (z) :=         aj z Nj ,         |z| < R
                                           j=1


then F does not extend analytically across any point in |z| = R.

    It turns out that the converse of Fabry’s Theorem is also true, and is a Theorem of
Pólya. There are several proofs of Fabry’s gap Theorem in [36], where one is based on
Turan’s lemma, on estimating the supremum norm of polynomials on T in terms of the
supremum norm on subarcs. There is also an analogue of Fabry’s gap Theorem for Dirichlet
series due to Carlson-Landau, see [36, Ch. 4, §7].

    In connection with the Poisson summation formula, a natural problem is to characterize
discrete infinite measures µ on R whose Fourier transform µ
                                                          b is of a similar form, with both
measures supported on uniformly discrete sets, that is,

                                        inf |λ − λ′ | > 0.
                                       λ̸=λ′



                                                    47
Such measures are necessarily of the form
                                  X                               X
                            µ=           a(λ) δλ ,         µ
                                                           b=           µ
                                                                        b(ξ) δξ ,
                                  λ∈Λ                             ξ∈Ξ


where Λ and Ξ are uniformly discrete subsets of R. Here we regard these objects as tempered
distributions. Measures of this type are called crystalline measures, and at first glance, one
might expect the uncertainty principle to obstruct the existence of such objects. However,
a basic example, arising from the Poisson summation formula, is given by the Dirac comb
                                                     X
                                               µ=          δn .
                                                     n∈Z


It is therefore of interest to understand crystalline measures and the structure of the sets
that support them. These questions are closely related to the theory of quasicrystals, devel-
oped to a large extent by Y. Meyer, whose mathematical framework was later instrumental
in interpreting some experimental discoveries of the Nobel Laureate in Chemistry D. Shecht-
man. We refer the reader to the survey of P. Kurasov [66] and to Meyer’s work [1] for further
background.


3     Logarithmic integrals on the unit-circle
In this section, we shall explore the convergence of the logarithmic integral
                                   Z
                                     log |f (ζ)|dm(ζ) > −∞
                                     T

and determine its governing role in several approximation and uniqueness problems arising
in harmonic analysis and complex function theory.


3.1    The F. & M. Riesz Theorem
We shall begin this story with a theorem of the brothers F. and M. Riesz, on the character-
ization of measures whose Fourier coefficients are supported on positive frequencies.

Theorem 3.1 (F. and M. Riesz). Let µ be a non-trivial complex finite Borel measure on T
with the property that  Z
                                  ζ n dµ(ζ) = 0,            n = 0, 1, 2, . . .
                              T

Then µ is absolutely continuous wrt to dm on T, and its Radon–Nikodym derivative f :=
dµ/dm satisfies                   Z
                                              log |f |dm > −∞.
                                          T

   In the literature, the F. and M. Riesz Theorem is often regarded as a principal result
within the theory of Hardy spaces, hence it is commonly phrased in complex analytic terms.
To clarify this point, closed subspace of L1 (T):

                         H 1 (T) := {f ∈ L1 (T) : fb(n) = 0, n < 0},



                                                     48
One may regard H 1 (T) as the trace space of analytic functions F in the analytic Hardy
space H 1 (D):                      Z
                                   sup            |F (rζ)|dm(ζ) < ∞.
                                  0<r<1       T

More precise, every F ∈ H 1 (D) has finite non-tangential limits equal to f ∈ H 1 (T), while
the Poisson extension to D of any f ∈ H 1 (T) belongs to H 1 (D). See appendix on Hardy
spaces. In complex analytic terms, the F. and M. Riesz Theorem implies that any measure
with vanishing negative Fourier coefficients is actually absolutely continuous wrt dm, with
Radon-Nikodym derivative in H 1 (T).
   Clearly, the conclusion of the F. and M. Riesz Theorem holds if we only assume that the
                     b(n) = 0 for n > M or n < M , for some M ∈ R. In fact, the F. and.
Fourier coefficients µ
M. Riesz is yet another manifestation uncertainty principle in harmonic analysis.

Principle 3.2. If the Fourier coefficients of a measure does not meet some neighborhood
of either ±∞, then the measure is absolutely continuous with logarithmic integral Radon-
Nikodym derivative on T.

    Neighborhoods at infinity are sets of the form (−∞, M ) and/or (M, ∞), where M ∈ R.
The proof presented below will be based on two key lemmas, which are both important in
their own right. At then end, we shall also give the short proof bases on Poisson exten-
sions and boundary behavior of Hardy space functions. Our first lemma is about uniform
approximation by holomorphic polynomials on sets of Lebesgue measure zero, whose utility
will re-surface on several occasions later.

Lemma 3.3 (Uniform approximation on small sets). Let E ⊂ T be a compact set of Lebesgue
measure zero, and let g be a continuous function on T with supζ∈T |g(ζ)| = 1. For any ε > 0,
there exists analytic polynomials (Qn )n satisfying:

              sup |Qn (z)| ≤ 1,       lim sup|Qn − g| = 0,             lim sup |Qn | = 0,
              |z|≤1                       n   E                         n T\E
                                                                             ε



where Eε := {ζ ∈ T : dist(ζ, E) ≤ ε} denotes ε-neighborhood of E.

    It is important to remark that the hypothesis m(E) = 0 is crucial, otherwise taking
g ≡ 1 we would be able to show that the Hardy space H 1 (D) contains a non-trivial indicator
function 1E , which violates the fact that such functions are logarithmically integrable on T,
see Appendix. The proof of this lemma will require a cocktail of several classical techniques
from real analysis.

    The next lemma is typically referred to as Jensen’s lemma. The reader should not confuse
it with Jensen’s inequality for convex functions, although they are of similar flavor.

Lemma 3.5 (Jensen’s lemma). For any f ∈ H 1 (T), we have
                                                 Z
                                  log|fb(0)| ≤           log |f |dm
                                                 T

   We remark that in view of Jensen’s inequality for convex functions, this is essentially an
improvement of the triangle inequality:
                                             Z
                                    |fb(0)| ≤ |f |dm,
                                                     T


indicative of the fact that elements in the subspace H 1 (T) exhibit much better behavior than
all of L1 (T). Jensen’s lemma is typically proved using Hardy space theory, for instance, see
[33], but we shall below give a simple self-contained proof, as in [36].

Proof of Lemma 3.5. Step 1: Jensen’s lemma for polynomials:
We shall first argue that Jensen’s lemma holds for all analytic polynomials Q. In that case,
log |Q| is a subharmonic function on C, and since b
                                                  Q(0) = Q(0) Jensen’s lemma follows from
the sub-mean inequality.

We shall now upgrade the estimate to all function in H 1 (T). To this end, fix an arbitrary
f ∈ H 1 (R) and consider its Fejér means fj ∈ H 1 (T), which converge to f in L1 (T), by
Fejér’s Theorem. Now for any ε > 0, the step 1 of proof implies that

                                              T                         T

It now only remains to send ε → 0+ and invoking the monotone convergence Theorem.


   We now prove the main result in this subsection.

                                 P (µ)(z) = 2               ,        z ∈ D,
                                                   T 1 − ζz
which defines an analytic function in D. Furthermore, it follows from Fubini’s Theorem that

                                           (1 − r2 )
       Z                         Z Z

which implies that P (µ) ∈ H 1 (D). It follows from the appendix on Hardy spaces that
P (µ) has radial limit dm-a.e on T equals to an L1 (T)-function f with fb(n) = 0 for n < 0,
i.e. f ∈ H 1 (T), and furthermore P (µ)(z) = P (f )(z). It follows by uniqueness of Poisson
integrals that dµ = f dm with f ∈ H 1 (T).


   Let us conclude this subsection with a simple corollary of the F. and M. Riesz theorem.

Corollary 3.6. For any compact set E ⊊ T, the analytic polynomials P are dense in C(E).

Proof. Suppose that P is not dense in C(E). Then, by duality (Hahn–Banach and Riesz
representation Theorem), there exists a non-trivial measure µ ∈ M (E) such that
                             Z
                                     ζ n dµ(ζ) = 0,        n = 0, 1, 2, . . . .
                                 T


By the F. and M. Riesz theorem, there exists f ∈ L1 (T) such that dµ = f dm, and
                                           Z
                                                log |f | dm > −∞.
                                            T

Since supp(µ) ⊆ E ⊊ T, the function f vanishes on a set of positive Lebesgue measure. This
contradicts the logarithmic integrability of f , and the result follows.


3.2    The Rudin–Carleson Theorem
Here, we consider a result attributed to the independent efforts of W. Rudin and L. Carleson,
which somewhat highlights the sharpness of the brothers Riesz Theorem. To this end, we
shall need a classical construction which goes back to the work of Fatou, asserting that any
compact set of Lebesgue measure zero in T is an admissible zero set for a function f ∈ H 1 (T)
which has continuous extension to T.

Theorem 3.7 (Fatou). For any compact set E ⊂ T of Lebesgue measure zero, there exists
a non-trivial function f which is continuous on T with

                                           fb(n) = 0,       n<0

such that f = 0 on E.


                                                      54
   The following proof is essentially a modification of an argument due to B. Korenblum,
outlined in [40]. For a slightly different proof, we refer the reader to P. Koosis in [54].

Proof of Theorem 3.7. Let {In }n denote the connected components of the open set T\E. To
each arc In , we perform a so-called Whitney decomposition {In,j }j , which has the following
property
                                                            1
                              dist(In,j , E) = |In,j | =          |In |.                  (8)
                                                         3 · 2|j|
In other words, their length are comparable to their distance to E. We now re-label the
joint collection of all Whitney decompositions of {Ik }k by {Jn }n . Let ξn denote the center
of each Whitney arc Jn , set rn = |Jn | + 1. Now consider the functions

                                           ξn |Jn |
                               hn (z) =             ,         z ∈ D.                        (9)
                                          rn ξn − z

We now record the following properties of (9), whose verification are left to the reader:
  (i) each hn is analytic in a neighborhood of D,

 (ii) Re hn ≥ 0,

(iii) there exists an numerical constant c > 0, such that Re hn (ζ) ≥ c for ζ ∈ Jn ,
Now pick a increasing sequence of positive numbers (λn )n with λn ↑ +∞, such that
                                      X
                                              λn |Jn | < ∞,
                                        n


and consider the (outer) function
                                                          !
                                            X
                         f (z) = exp −            λn hn (z) ,        z ∈ D.
                                              n


It follows from the property (ii) of hn that f : D → D is analytic, and since the {ξn } only
accumulate to the compact set E, we see that f extends analytically across each compact
subarc of T ⊂ E. For instance, this can easily be verified using Morera’s Theorem on a small
disc centered at an arbitrary point in T \ E. We shall now verify the continuity property
of f and its vanishing on the set E. To this end, fix a point ζ ∈ T \ E, and let Jn be the
Whitney subarc that contains ζ. It follows from property (iii) of hn that
                                                              !
                                        X
                      |f (ζ)| ≤ exp −         λn Re hn (ζ)        ≤ exp(−cλn )
                                          n


Now a key observation is that in order for dist(ζ, E) → 0, it must pass through infinitely
many Whitney arcs {Jn }n , since their lengths are always comparable to their distance to
E. Now this observation in conjunction with the fact that λn ↑ ∞, implies that f (ζ) → 0
as dist(ζ, E) → 0. We conclude that f is continuous on T, vanishes precisely on E, and
satisfies fb(n) = 0 for n < 0, since it is a bounded analytic function in D.


   With this lemma at hand, we shall now turn to the proof of the Rudin-Carleson Theorem,
phrased as a problem on boundary interpolation with analytic functions in D.


                                                  55
Theorem 3.8 (Rudin-Carleson). Let E ⊂ T be a compact set of Lebesgue measure zero.
Then for any g ∈ C(T) and any ε > 0, there exists φε ∈ H ∞ (D) ∩ C(T) such that φε = g
on E, and
                           sup|φε (ζ)| ≤ (1 + ε) sup |g(ζ)|.
                                  ζ∈T                          ζ∈E

   Here, we denote by H ∞ (D) the Banach space of bounded analytic functions in D,
equipped with the supremum sup|z|<1 |f (z)|. The word ”peak” comes from the fact that
the interpolating function φε on E, roughly attains its ”peak value” on that set, that is, its
supremum norm is essentially smaller elsewhere on T. We shall also see in the proof how
the notion of peak naturally occurs. The proof below is constructive, and essentially due
to L. Carleson, which is outlined on P.129 in [33]. There are simpler proofs by means of
duality, which are far from constructive, see [33] and references therein.

Proof of Theorem 3.8. Step 1: Constructing a peak function:
Fix an arbitrary compact set E ⊂ T of Lebesgue measure zero and our goal is first to exhibit
a so-called peak function for E. To this end, consider the holomorphic function h = log f on
D, where f is as in Theorem 3.7. Note that Re h is a strictly negative on T, with Re h = −∞
precisely on E and h ∈ C ∞ (D \ E). Now consider the following conformal transformation
of h;
                                           h(z)
                                H(z) :=           ,     z ∈ D,
                                         h(z) − 1
which intrinsically gives rise to a holomorphic self-map H : D → D, which extends contin-
uously to T, satisfies H = 1 on E, while |H| < 1 on D \ E. This gives our desired peak
function on E.

Step 2: Approximation scheme:
Now fix an arbitrary continuous function g on T, and choose a large integer n0 > 0, so that
the continuous function g0 := H n0 · g attains its maximum on E:

                                        sup|g0 (ζ)| = sup |g(ζ)|.
                                        ζ∈T              ζ∈E


Invoking Lemma 3.3, we can find holomorphic polynomials (pj )j , and open neighborhoods
(Ej )j of E such that pj → g0 uniformly on E and

                       sup|pj+1 − pj | ≤ 2−j ,            sup|pj | ≤ sup |g(ζ)|.
                        Ej                                 T            ζ∈E


Next, we pick positive integers (Nj )j so large that

            sup |H Nj (ζ)||pj+1 (ζ) − pj (ζ)| ≤ 2−j ,                sup |H Nj (ζ)pj (ζ)| ≤ 2−j
          ζ∈T\Ej                                                 ζ∈T\Ej


This can indeed be arranged since supT\Ej |H| < 1, for all fixed j. Now consider the
functions
                                 X
    φj (z) := H Nj (z)pj (z) +         H Nk (z) (pk+1 (z) − pk (z)) ,         z ∈ D,     j = 1, 2, . . .
                                 k≥j




                                                    56
The Weierstrass M -test readily implies that each φj extends continuously to T. Since H = 1
on E, and pk → g uniformly on E, we have φj = g on E for each j. Furthermore,
                                                 X
                         |φj | ≤ |H Nj pj | +          2−k ≤ sup |g(ζ)| + 21−j .
                                                              ζ∈E
                                                 k≥j


The proof is complete.

   Invoking a more careful argument, one can actually show that the norm estimate also
holds with ε = 0, but this is not important for our further developments.
   We complete this section by mentioning that the initial proof of the brothers Riesz
Theorem was essentially based on the above construction of peak sets, and the argument is
carefully outlined in the book of P. Koosis [54].


3.3    Mean polynomial approximation on the unit-circle
Here we consider the problem of mean polynomial approximation on T. Given a positive
finite Borel measure µ on T, we ask when are the analytic polynomials dense in the space
L2 (µ). Fortunately, this result has a very satisfactory answer which goes back to the work
of G. Szegö on orthogonal polynomials.

Theorem 3.9 (Szegö). Let µ be a positive finite Borel measure on T with Lebesgue decom-
position dµ = wdm + dµs . Then
                                Z                               Z                
                                             2
                          inf       |q(ζ) − ζ| dµ(ζ) = exp              log wdm
                         q∈P    T                                   T

where P denotes the set of analytic polynomials.

   As an immediate corollary of Szegö’s Theorem, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.10. The analytic polynomials are dense in L2 (dµ) if and only if

                                                  / L1 (T).

    Note that the singular part of µ is irrelevant. However, we shall below list a short proof
of Corollary 3.10, under the assumption that µ is absolutely continuous wrt dm, which does
not require the Szegö’s beautiful distance formula, and only relies on a duality argument
and the F. and M. Riesz Theorem.

Proof of Corollary 3.10. If log w ∈ L1 (T), then we can define a an outer function W ∈ H 1 (T)
such that |W | = w a.e on T, and hence W = U w for some unimodular function U ∈ L∞ (T).
But then U0 (ζ) = U (ζ)ζ is a non-trivial element in the Hilbert space L2 (wdm) with the
property
    Z                          Z

    We now re-direct our attention to the proof of Theorem 3.9 below, which will be based
on an Beurling’s Theorem, and an approximation lemma on small sets, which allows us to
dispense with the singular part of µ. The approximation lemma in question is roughly a
slight extension of Lemma 3.3, with some added real analysis flavor.
Lemma 3.11. Let µ be a positive finite Borel measure on T which is singular wrt dm. Then
there exists analytic polynomials (pn )n such that

            (i) sup|pn (z)| ≤ 1,      (ii) pn → ζ dµ − a.e,         (iii) pn → 0, dm − a.e.
                 z∈D


Proof. Since finite Borel measures are Radon measure, we can find compacts subsets {En }n
of T with En ⊆ En+1 , each of Lebesgue measure zero, such that

                                          µ(T) ≤ µ(En ) + 1/n.

Now applying Corollary 3.4, we can find analytic polynomials (Qn,j )j , such that for each n:

       sup|Qn,j (z)| ≤ 1,          sup |Qn,j (ζ) − ζ| → 0,       Qn,j → 0,         dm-a.e on T,
       z∈D                         ζ∈En


as j → ∞. Now using the uniform convergence on En and the estimates
                                                                           
                 µ {|Qn,j − ζ| > ε} ≤ µ(T \ En ) + µ En ∩ {|Qn,j − ζ| > ε/2} ,

we may for each n find an integer jn ≥ 1, such that Qn,jn → ζ in measure µ. By means
of modifying Qn,jn further, if necessary, we may using a similar argument also assume that
Qn,jn → 0 in measure dm. Now letting (pn )n be an appropriate subsequence of (Qn,jn )n ,
we make pn → ζ dµ-a.e and pn → 0 dm-a.e, which proves the claim.


   We are now ready to prove Szegö’s Theorem.

whenever q is, we have

Corollary 3.12. Let w ∈ L1 (T) non-negative with T log wdm = −∞. Then whenever

Proof. Let f ∈ L1 (T) be a function satisfying the above properties. Note that we can

    We shall briefly mention an interesting consequence of Szegö’s Theorem to the theory
of orthogonal polynomials. To this end, let µ be a probability measure on T and construct
orthogonal (analytic) polynomials {φn }∞        2
                                        n=0 in L (µ) using the Gram-Schmidt procedure of
        2
{1, ζ, ζ , . . . } such that   Z

                                    T

where δm,n are the usual Kronecker deltas. It is not difficult to see that the φn ’s can be
chosen monic, and more surprisingly, these orthogonal polynomials satisfy the following
so-called Szegö recursion:

                        φn+1 (z) = zφn (z) − αn z n φn (1/z),       z∈C

where {αn }n is the so-called Geronimus-Verblunsky sequence of µ. Now a key structural
result in the theory is that every probability measure µ gives rise to a unique Geronimus-
Verblunsky sequence {αn }n with |αn | < 1. Conversely, every sequence of complex numbers
{αn }n with |αn | < 1 gives rise to a unique probability measure, for which it is a Verblunsky
off. Now if dµ = wdm + dµs , then one can show using Szegö’s Theorem that
                             ∞
                             Y                         Z          
                                   (1 − |αn |) = exp        log wdm ,
                             n=0                        T


and the later sum is easily seen to converge if and only if {αn }n ∈ ℓ2 . For great brief survey
on these matters, and more, we refer the reader to B. Simon in [90].
   We complete this subsection by highlighting a classical result from 1941 due to A. Kol-

                                                           61
3.4    Simultaneous polynomial approximation in the disc
In this final part of the section, we shall delve into certain aspects of the problem on
polynomial approximation the the closed disc D. A natural extension of Szegö’s problem in
this context goes as follows:
    Let µ be a positive finite Borel measures µ on D. When are the analytic polynomials P
dense in L2 (µ)?
    It turns out that this problem is immensely rich and connects to various other inter-
esting problems in complex analysis, harmonic analysis, and operator theory, and several
known figures, such as J. Brennan, S. Khrushchev, T. Kriete, A. Volberg, and others have
contributed a fair share to this subject. In broad generality, J. Thomson in [94] found a
characterization expressed in terms of analyticity. If P 2 (µ) denotes the closure of P in L2 (µ)
then it was proved that P 2 (µ) = L2 (µ) if and only if no point-evaluation defines a bounded
linear functional on P 2 (µ). Now one direction is rather simple, thus if λ ∈ D is a bounded
point-evaluation functional on P 2 (µ), then this means that there exists a constant C(λ) > 0
such that                                 Z
                           |p(λ)|2 ≤ C(λ) |p(z)|2 dµ(z).        p∈P
                                             D

But this and a classical normal family argument implies that all functions in P 2 (µ) extend
analytically in a neighborhood of λ, hence P 2 (µ) ⊊ L2 (µ).
     Even in the case when P 2 (µ) has a non-trivial bounded point-evaluation functional, it is
still of interest to understand the space P 2 (µ). A particularly interesting case is when every
point in D is assume to be a bounded point-evaluation functional on P 2 (µ). J. Thomson in
[94] proved the following remarkable decomposition: there exists a Borel set E ⊆ T∩supp(µ)
of
                              P 2 (µ) ∼
                                      = P 2 (µ|supp(µ)\E ) ⊕ L2 (µ|E ),

interpreted in the sense isometric isomorphism of Hilbert spaces. Here the space

                                         P 2 (µ|supp(µ)\E )

is irreduciblein the sense that it contains no non-trivial characteristic function, which roughly
means that it is a genuine space of analytic functions on the part of µ restricted to supp(µ) \
E, while you can approximate any L2 -function on the set supp(µ)∩E. This result highlights
the occurrence of a certain simultaneous approximation phenomenon, which we shall get
more aquatinted with. To clarify this point, Thomson’s result implies that there exists
analytic polynomials (pn )n with the properties
                          Z                            Z
                                    2
                                 |pn | dµ → 0,             |pn − 1|2 dµ → 0.
                           D\E                         E


With this perspective in mind, we continue the story from an uncertainty principle perspec-
tive, by asking a slightly broader question: Suppose we have a Banach space X of analytic
functions in D which contains P as a dense subset. Can we classify the compact subsets
E ⊂ T, for which there exists analytic polynomials {Qn }n so that the following simultaneous
approximation phenomenon occurs:

                         Qn → 0 in X,            Qn → 1 uniformly on E?


                                                  62
   The following classical results gives essentially puts this question into two different cat-
egories.

Theorem 3.14 (Khinchin-Ostrowski). Let E ⊂ T be a set of positive Lebesgue measure
and let (Qn )n be analytic polynomials with the property that there exists a constant C > 0
such that               Z
                   sup     log+ |Qn (rζ)|dm(ζ) ≤ C,     Qn → 0 a.e on E.
                      0<r<1   T

Then (Qn )n converges uniformly to 0 on compacts subsets of D.

   The Khinchin–Ostrowksi Theorem roughly asserts that if functions in X have finite
Nevanlinna characteristic
                               Z
                          sup    log+ |f (rζ)|dm(ζ) < ∞,
                                  0<r<1     T

then simultaneous approximation can only occur on subsets E ⊂ T of Lebesgue measure
zero. As we saw earlier from the peak construction of the Rudin-Carleson Theorem, for any
compact set E ⊂ T of zero Lebesgue measure, there exists analytic functions (fn )n which
converge uniformly to 1 on E and uniformly to zero in D. It is not difficult from here to
pass to analytic polynomials and show that any compact set of Lebesgue measure zero gives
rise to a simultaneous approximation phenomenon on spaces X whose topology is weaker
than uniform convergence in D.

Proof of Theorem 3.14. By means of sacrificing arbitrary small but positive Lebesgue mea-
sure of E, we may by means of invoking Egoroff’s Theorem assume that limn Qn = 0
uniformly on E. Note that by the conformal Jensen lemma (see Theorem 8.3 in appendix),
we have for any z ∈ D:
                  Z
  log|Qn (z)| ≤        log|Qn (ζ)|Pz (ζ)dm(ζ)
                  T
                                                                        1 + |z|
                                                     Z                              Z
                                                   ≤   log |Qn |Pz dm +                 log+ |Qn |dm.
                                                     E                  1 − |z|     T

Since Qn (rζ) → Qn (ζ) as r → 1−, an immediate consequence of Fatou’s Lemma yields

                                                              1 + |z|
                                       Z
                       log|Qn (z)| ≤       log |Qn |Pz dm +           C,   z ∈ D.
                                       E                      1 − |z|

Sending n → ∞ and using the assumption that Qn → 0 uniformly on E, we conclude that
Qn → 0 uniformly on compact subsets of D.

    Note that the assumption on E having positive Lebesgue measure was crucial. For a
slightly stronger statement, we refer to [36, P. 280].
    We shall now move our attention to class of analytic functions X, which do not have
finite Nevanlinna characteristic, and exhibit simultaneous approximation on sets of positive
Lebesgue measure. In order to maintain our initial perspective of mean polynomial approx-
imation, a convenient setting to phase this problem within is the class of Bergman spaces




                                                    63
L2a (dAα ) which consists of analytic functions f in D satisfying
                                     Z
                                            |f (z)|2 dAα (z) < ∞,
                                        D


where dAα (z) = (1 − |z|)α−1 dA(z) denotes a weighted Lebesgue area measure on D, wrt to
the parameter range α > 0 to ensure that it is a finite measure.
    The following remarkable result will be the principal focus of this section, which not
only provides a geometric characterization of simultaneous approximation in L2a (dAα ), but
in addition connects it to a unilateral Fourier uniqueness problem.
Theorem 3.15 (Khrushchev). Let K ⊂ T be a compact set of positive Lebesgue measure.
Then the following statements are equivalent:
  (i) Unilateral uniqueness. K supports no non-trivial µ ∈ M (T) with
                                         X
                                              (1 + n)α |b
                                                        µ(n)|2 < ∞,
                                         n≥0


     for some/any α > 0.

 (ii) Simultaneous approximation. For some/any α > 0, there exist analytic polyno-
      mials (Qn )n such that
                             Z
                                  |Qn − 1|2 dAα → 0,                sup |Qn | → 0.
                              D                                     K


(iii) Entropic content. K contains no compact subset of positive Lebesgue measure which
      has finite Beurling–Carleson entropy:
                                             X                 1
                                                 |In | log         < ∞,
                                                             |In |

     where {In }n are the connected components of T \ K.
    Note that the characterization in (iii) gives a geometric characterization of such compact
sets E. Notably, it is independent of α > 0, hence clarifying that if the statements in (i)
and (ii) hold for some α > 0, then also hold for any α > 0.
    We note that the condition in (i) is unilateral, that is, we only require Fourier decay
of positive Fourier frequencies. To phrase it differently, it is intrinsically a uniqueness
phenomenon for the Cauchy integral in the analytic Dirichlet-Sobolev space ℓ2,α     a . To see
this, note that if µ ∈ M (T), then
                                    Z            ∞
                                       dµ(ζ)    X
                       K(µ)(z) :=             =     b(n)z n ,
                                                    µ                      z ∈ D.
                                     T 1 − ζz   n=0


Therefore, the condition (i) can be reformulated as E supports a measure complex finite
Borel measure µ for which its Cauchy integral K(µ) defines a non-trivial element in the
fractional Dirichlet-Sobolev space
                 Z                                    X
                    |K(µ)′ (z)|2 (1 − |z|)α−1 dA(z) ≍   (1 + n)α |b
                                                                  µ(n)|2 < ∞.
                  D                                           n≥0


                                                    64
In contrast, the bilateral uniqueness problem on which sets E supports a complex finite Borel
measure with {b µ(n)}n∈Z ∈ ℓ2,α , the problem in the range α > 1 has a simple solution due to
the classical Sobolev embeddings (Murray’s Theorem). Such compact sets must have non-
empty interior, whereas Khrushchev’s Theorem implies that there are totally disconnected
sets E, which can support a measure whose Cauchy integral is C α (T), for any desirable
α > 0. In the range α = 1, the bilateral problem is related to removable sets for analytic
Sobolev functions W 1,2 (C), which initially appear in the work of L. Ahlfors and A. Beurling
in [2]. On the other hand, the simultaneous approximation phenomenon in (ii) can formally
be translated to assertion that the following Thomson decomposition holds:

                          P 2 (dA + dm|K ) ∼
                                           = P 2 (dA) ⊕ L2 (dm|K ).

Below, we show how using a simple Cantor-constructions, one can exhibit sets of finite
Beurling–Carleson entropy.

Example 3.16 (Fractal sets which lack entropic content). Let (αn )∞      n=0 be positive real

from each connected component of En the middle open arcs of length αn /2n . This gives a

    However, in the statement (iii) of Khrushchev’s Theorem, the claim is that K should con-

    Having established the equivalence between (i) and (ii) in Khrushchev’s Theorem, we
shall now turn to proving the necessity and sufficiency of the entropic content in (iii).
Khrushchev’s original proof of the necessity goes via (i) =⇒ (ii), and involves a confor-
mal invariant version of classical Khinchin-Ostrowski Theorem, on special Stolz-Privalov
domains associated with E. Since this approach slightly deviates from the theme in this
notes, we shall give a constructive and direct proof of (i) =⇒ (iii). Our proof is principally
based on construction, dating back to the work of L. Carleson in [19], and recently appeared
in [71].

    To this end, we shall need rephrase the property in (iii) involving a quantitative metric.
Given a continuous increasing function κ : [0, 1] → [0, 1] with κ(0) = 0, we define the
κ-Hausdorff content of a Borel set K ⊂ T as

          Monotonicity:        Hκ (K1 ) ≤ Hκ (K2 )                             if K1 ⊆ K2 ,

Lemma 3.19 (Makarov’s lemma). Let E ⊊ T be a compact set of positive Lebesgue measure
and set
                                            e
                              κ(t) := t log ,     0 < t ≤ 1.
                                            t
Then E contains no subset of finite Beurling-Carleson entropy of positive Lebesgue measure
if and only if
                                    Hκ (I \ E) = Hκ (I),

for any arc I ⊂ T. If E satisfies the above condition, then there exists for any arc I a
positive finite Borel measure νI supported in I \ E, such that

                             1
                   ∥νI ∥ =     κ (|I ∩ E|) ,               νI (J) ≤ Cκ(|J|),   J ⊂ T,
                             2
where C > 0 is a numerical constant independent of I.

  Note that the second statement is essentially a localized quantitative version of Frost-
man’s mass distribution principle.

Proof. Step 1: Necessity:
Note that Hκ (I) = κ(|I|) for any arc I ⊂ T. Suppose that Hκ (I \ E) < κ(|I|), for some
closed arc I. This implies that we can pick a cover (Ik )k of open arcs of I \ E, such that
                                              X

This implies that K := I \ ∪k Ik ⊆ E is a compact set of positive Lebesgue measure, which

K ⊆ E of positive Lebesgue measure, and let {Jk }k≥0 be the connected components of
                              P

                                       X

                                      k>k0

Step 3: Frostman measure:
Recall that Frostman’s Lemma (for instance, see Theorem ? in [21]) asserts that if a compact
subset K ⊂ T satisfies Hκ (K) > 0, then there exists a probability measure µ ∈ M (K) such
that µ(I) ≤ Cκ(|I|) for any arc I ⊂ T. It now just remains to choose such a Frostman
measure µI supported in a compact subset of I ⊂ E and simple re-scale it to ∥νI ∥ =
1
2 κ (|I ∩ E|), while using the boundedness of κ to ensure that we still maintain µ(I) ≤
Cκ(|I|) for any arc I ⊂ T, with possibly a slightly larger numerical constant.

   As reader may have noticed, the above argument works equally well for a general in-
creasing gauge-function κ : [0, 1] → [0, 1], which vanishes at zero.
   We now complete the proof of the remaining part of Khrushchev’s Theorem.

                                                      72
3.5    Further results
We saw in subsection 3.1 that Jensen’s inequality implies that if f ∈ H 1 (T) then
                                         Z
                                             log |f |dm > −∞.
                                         T


Conversely, it is also natural to ask which f ∈ L1 (T) satisfy have convergent logarithmic
integral. This problem was solved by J. Bourgain, who proved the following beautiful
Theorem.

Theorem 3.20 (J. Bourgain, [17]). A function f ∈ L1 (T) has finite logarithmic integral on
T if and only if there exists g, h ∈ H 1 (T), such that f = gh dm-a.e on T.

    The proof is based on a deep result on the factorization of unimodular functions on T,
expressed in terms of quotient of Blaschke products.
    We note that there are smooth version of Fatou’s Theorem, which essentially dates back
to the work of L. Carleson in [21] in the setting of C k (T) for all finite k > 0, and which was
later extended to C ∞ (T) by B. Taylor and D. Williams [93].

Theorem 3.21 (Carleson, 1967, Taylor-Williams, 1970). Let E ⊂ T be a compact set of
Lebesgue measure zero, which has finite Beurling–Carleson entropy. There exists an analytic
function f : D → D which extends to C ∞ (T) and satisfies
                                   ∞ n
                                   \                       o
                                      ζ ∈ T : f (n) (ζ) = 0 ⊆ E.
                                   n=0

    The proof of Theorem 3.21 is essentially outlined in our proof Lemma 3.18, but differs
from original one in [93]. It is worth to remark that the condition of finite Beurling–Carleson
entropy is also necessary. Indeed, if f is an analytic function in D which extends to function
of class C 1 (T) (or even of Hölder class), and vanishes on E, then

                             |f (ζ)| ≤ sup|f ′ | · dist(ζ, E),      ζ ∈ T.
                                         T


Combining this with the fact that log |f | is integrable on T, one concludes that
                      Z                                Z
                                      1
                           log              dm(ζ) ≲        |log |f ||dm + 1 < ∞.
                       T         dist(ζ, E)            T

Now the integral on the left hand side is easily shown to be comparable to the Beurling–
Carleson entropy of E:
                                              Z
                         X             1                  1
                            |Ik | log       ≍    log            dm(ζ)
                                      |Ik |    T     dist(ζ, E)
                             k


where {Ik }k are the connected components of T \ E. With these discussion, we conclude
that the boundary zero sets of analytic functions in D, with smooth extensions to T are
characterized as the compact sets of Lebesgue measure zero having finite Beurling–Carleson
entropy. These sets were also recently shown to play crucial role in the theory of model
spaces, see [30], [72], and references therein.


                                                  73
   Another refinement of Szegö’s problem on mean polynomial approximation in T, is to
consider the angle between the analytic polynomials P0 of average zero, and the non-analytic
polynomials P (analytic polynomials in the variables ζ). In the framework of stochastic
process, this problem has the amusing interpretation of measuring angle between ”the past
and the future”. More precisely, the result goes as follows.

Theorem 3.22 (Helson-Szegö). Let dµ = wdm + dµs be a positive finite Borel measure on
T and consider                                    Z
                      cos(θ) :=        inf           f gdµ .
                                                  g∈P0 ,f ∈P             T
                                             ∥g∥L2 (µ) =∥f ∥L2 (µ) =1

Then there exists real-valued functions u, v ∈ L∞ (T) with ∥v∥L∞ (T) < π/2, such that

                                             log w = u + H(v),

if and only if θ = π/2.

    The above so-called Helson-Szegö decomposition of the weight log w is much stronger
merely requiring log w to be integrable on T. In fact, it turns out that it is also equivalent
to the following so-called Muckenhoupt A2 -condition:
                                        Z         Z        
                                   1              1
                       sup                 wdm          w−1 dm < ∞,
                       I⊂T        m(I)   I       m(I) I

where the supremum is taken over all arcs I ⊆ T. The Muckenhoupt A2 -condition plays a
crucial role in harmonic analysis, as it characterizes the boundedness on L2 (wdm) of the
Hilbert transform

                                                 f (eit )
                                        Z
                    H(f )(eiθ ) := lim                    dt,   eiθ ∈ T
                                  ε→0+ |t−θ|>ε eit − eiθ


and other classical singular integral operators of Calderón-Zygmund type. Another curious
fact is that there exists no known direct proof showing that either the boundedness of H
on L2 (wdm) or the Muckenhoupt A2 -condition implies the Helson-Szegö decomposition of
log w. Indeed, all known proofs go through via the angle between subspaces. Partial progress
and some neat insights to this problems were announced in the short paper [25]. For further
details on these matters, we refer the reader to the excellent book of J. Garnett in [33].
    A natural way to generalize Khrushchev’s problem is to ask for which positive integrable
functions w on T, can we find polynomials (Qn )n for which
                          Z                                   Z
                              |Qn − 1|2 dA → 0,                     |Qn |2 wdm → 0.      (13)
                          D                                     T

Phrased differently, we are asking for which weights w on T does the following splitting
phenomenon in the Thomson decomposition occur:

                              P 2 (dA + wdm) ∼
                                             = P 2 (dA) ⊕ L2 (wdm).

A. Volberg showed in [37, Ch. 12, p. 82] that there exists a weight with full support, which
is small enough so that the above holds. It was known among experts that if there exists a


                                                        74
set E ⊂ T (of positive Lebesgue measure) of finite Beurling–Carleson entropy for which
                                          Z
                                                  log wdm > −∞,
                                              E

then it is impossible to exhibit such polynomials. In fact, it was conjectured by T. Kriete
and B. MacCluer in [64] that simultaneous approximation phenomenon in (13) occurs if and
only if                              Z
                                                  log wdm = −∞
                                              E

for all sets of positive Lebesgue measure which have finite Beurling–Carleson entropy. This
conjecture was indeed recently confirmed by L. Bergqvist and B. Malman in [10], and may
essentially be regarded as a local version of Szegö’s Theorem.


4     Fourier uniqueness problems in L2 (R)
Here, we shall visit several manifestations of the uncertainty principle in the most classical
framework of L2 (R). We start with a brief crash-course on the Fourier transform, and
immediately move on to some of its most classical results, and continue further specialized
Theorems.


4.1     The Fourier transform on the real line
The Fourier transform of an integrable function f ∈ L1 (R) is defined as
                                          Z
                               fb(ξ) :=           f (x)e−ixξ dx,   ξ ∈ R.
                                             R

Some authors adopt different normalizations, but this will not play any essential role for
our purposes. Before turning to convergence issues, let us record some basic structural
properties, which hold whenever both sides are well-defined:

    • Linearity: af
                  \ + bg = afb + bb
                                  g.

    • Translation: f (·\
                       − x0 )(ξ) = e−ix0 ξ fb(ξ).

    • Modulation: eixξ
                    \              b(ξ − ξ0 ).
                      0 f (x)(ξ) = f



    • Convolution: f[
                    ∗ g = fbgb.

    • Differentiation: fb′ (ξ) = iξ fb(ξ).

    In contrast with Fourier coefficients, the Fourier transform is defined on an unbounded
set, and thus requires integrability at infinity in order to be a well-defined function.
    A particularly convenient setting for studying the Fourier transform is the Schwartz class
S(R), consisting of all smooth functions f on R such that

                                   sup(1 + |x|)M |f (N ) (x)| < ∞,
                                   x∈R


for all integers M, N ≥ 0. In other words, functions in S(R), together with all their deriva-
tives, decay faster than any polynomial at infinity.


                                                       75
    The Schwartz class enjoys a number of remarkable stability properties: it is closed under
differentiation and multiplication, and the Fourier transform preserves it. More precisely,
one can show that
                                f ∈ S(R) ⇐⇒ fb ∈ S(R),

so that the Fourier transform defines a bijective linear map on S(R). In this setting, the
Fourier inversion formula takes the form
                                       Z
                                    1
                           f (x) =       fb(ξ)eixξ dξ,    x ∈ R.
                                   2π R

   Moreover, for f, g ∈ S(R), one readily verifies the polarized Plancherel identity
                               Z                            Z
                                                        1
                                    f (x) g(x) dx =              fb(ξ) gb(ξ) dξ.
                                R                      2π    R

In particular, taking f = g yields the analogue of Parseval’s identity:
                       Z                   Z
                                         1
                          |f (x)|2 dx =       |fb(ξ)|2 dξ,  f ∈ S(R).
                        R               2π  R


   Since S(R) is dense in L2 (R), Plancherel’s identity allows us to extend the Fourier
transform uniquely to a continuous linear operator on L2 (R). The above identities continue
to hold for all f ∈ L2 (R), with convergence understood in the L2 -sense. Details of this
extension can be found in standard references such as [50, 62, 88].

   We now turn to a remarkable identity that connects a function with its Fourier transform
through discrete sampling.

Theorem 4.1 (Poisson summation formula). For any f ∈ S(R), we have
                                           X                 X
                                      2π         f (2πn) =           fb(n).
                                           n∈Z               n∈Z


Proof. Consider the 2π-periodic function
                                             X

                                             k∈Z


We may regard ϕ as a function on T. Since f ∈ S(R), the series defining ϕ converges
absolutely and uniformly, and ϕ is continuous. Moreover,
                              Z                 Z
                                 |ϕ(ζ)| dm(ζ) ≤   |f (x)| dx,
                                     T                       R


so ϕ ∈ L1 (T).
   We now compute its Fourier coefficients:
                          Z 2π                            X Z 2π
                 ϕ(n) =
                 b                 ϕ(t)e−int dt = 2π                   f (t + 2πk)e−int dt.
                           0                              k∈Z    0

                                                  Z
                                   ϕ(n)
                                   b    = 2π           f (t)e−int dt = fb(n).
                                                   R


Thus the Fourier coefficients of ϕ coincide with the values of fb at the integers.
  Since ϕ is smooth and periodic, its Fourier series converges uniformly, and therefore
                                                  X               X

                                                  n∈Z             n∈Z


On the other hand,                                       X

                                                         n∈Z

which completes the proof.

    We note that the Poisson summation formula continues to hold under weaker assump-
tions, provided the expressions involved are interpreted in a suitable sense.


4.2    The Heisenberg-Uncertainty principle
We begin with perhaps the most classical result in the theory, attributed to the physicist
Heisenberg and forming one of the cornerstones of quantum mechanics.
   Below, we state the result for functions in the Schwartz class. Although it suffices to
assume f, f ′ ∈ L2 (R), we work in S(R) to avoid technicalities. In this framework, the
uncertainty principle takes the following form.

Theorem 4.2 (Heisenberg’s UP). For any x0 , ξ0 ∈ R, we have
                    Z                            1/2 Z                               1/2
      ∥f ∥2L2 ≤ 2        (x − x0 )2 |f (x)|2 dx                (ξ − ξ0 )2 |fb(ξ)|2 dξ          ,   f ∈ S(R).
                     R                                     R

Proof. By means of translating f , we may assume that x0 = 0. Likewise, we may also
re-scale f and assume that ξ0 = 0. According to Plancharel’s Theorem, the Heisenberg
inequality can be rephrased as the following estimate:

                                       ∥f ∥2L2 ≤ 2∥M f ∥L2 ∥Df ∥L2

where M f (x) = xf and Df (x) = −if ′ (x), regarded as linear operators. Consider the
commutator regarded as the linear map defined by [M, D] = M D − DM . Note that a
straightforward calculation gives

         [M, D]f (x) = −ixf ′ (x) + i(xf (x))′ = −ixf ′ (x) + if (x) + ixf ′ (x) = if (x).

Furthermore, viewed as a densely defined linear operators on L2 , one can easily show that




                                                         77
it is self-adjoint. It follows from these observations that

              1                   1
  ∥f ∥2L2 =     ⟨[M, D]f, f ⟩L2 = (⟨M Df, f ⟩L2 − ⟨DM f, f ⟩L2 ) =
              i                   i
                    1
                       (⟨Df, M f ⟩L2 − ⟨M f, Df ⟩L2 ) = 2 Im⟨Df, M f ⟩L2 ≤ 2∥M f ∥L2 ∥Df ∥L2 .
                     i



    More generally, the same argument proves the following claim, which typically appears
in quantum physics.

Corollary 4.3 (UP for commutators). Let H be a complex Hilbert space and let A, B be
self-adjoint densely defined linear operators on H. If C = [A, B] denotes the commutator,
then
                        ∥Cu∥2H ≤ 2∥Au∥H ∥Bu∥H ,        u ∈ DA ∩ DB .

Here DA and DB denotes the domains of A and B, respectively.

   Of course, this estimate becomes more interesting if the commutator C is related to the
identity operator, in some natural way.


4.3    Functions with compactly supported Fourier transform
Next, we shall look a yet another classical manifestation of the uncertainty principle, which
stems from the Paley–Wiener Theorem on the relation between compactly supported func-
tions and their Fourier transform.
    An entire analytic function f is said to be of exponential-type a with a ≥ 0, if for any
ε > 0, there exists a constant C(ε) > 0 such that

                            |f (z)| ≤ C(ε) exp ((a + ε)|z|) ,   z ∈ C.

We denote this set of functions by Ea , and declare that an entire analytic function is of
(finite) exponential-type, if f ∈ ∪a≥0 Ea . The following classical result characterizes a large
class of entire functions of exponential-type in terms of their Fourier transform.

Theorem 4.4 (Paley-Wiener). Let a > 0 and f ∈ L2 (R). Then supp(fb) ⊆ [−a, a] if and
only if there exists F ∈ Ea such that F = f a.e on R. In particular, f extends to a function
in Ea , whose restriction to the real line belongs to L2 (R).

   Note that the statement is symmetric in the sense that we can swap the roles of f, fb.
   In order to prove the Paley-Wiener Theorem, we shall need generalization of the max-
imum principle to unbounded domain. Results of this type go under the name of the
Phragmén-Lindelöf principle, but we shall below phrase half-space version of it, suitable for
our purposes.

Theorem 4.5 (Phragmén-Lindelöf, halfplane). Let f be a bounded continuous function in
C+ which is analytic in C+ . Suppose that there exists a number 0 < α < 1 and a constant
C > 0 such that
                            |f (z)| ≤ C exp ( |z|α )   z ∈ C+ .



                                                 78
Then f belongs to H ∞ (C+ ) with

                                    sup |f (z)| = sup |f (x)|.
                                   z∈C+               x∈R


   We note that the growth restriction on f in C+ cannot be relaxed to include α = 1, as
seen from the example f (z) = e−iz . In similar way, the same conclusion also holds for the
lower half-plane C− .

Proof. Set M := supx∈R |f (x)|, and without loss of generality we may assume that M = 1.
Fix 0 < α < β < 1. For ε > 0, consider the perturb analytic functions

                         fε (z) := exp −ε(−iz)β f (z),
                                               
                                                                    z ∈ C+ .

Let R > 0 number to be specified in a moment, and consider the semi-circle in C+ defined
by
                            CR := {z = Reit : 0 ≤ t ≤ π}.

Note that
                                           π
             Re ε(−iReit )β = εRβ cos(β(t − )) ≥ c(β)εRβ ,
                           
                                                                               0 ≤ t ≤ π,
                                           2
for some constant c(β) > 0. With this at hand, we obtain the estimate

            |fε (Reit )| ≤ |f (Reit )| exp −c(β)εRβ ≤ C exp Rα − c(β)εRβ ≤ 1,
                                                                       


whenever R > 0 sufficiently large. If DR denotes the semi-disc in C+ with boundary CR ∪R,
then it follows that
                                       sup |fε (z)| ≤ 1.
                                      z∈C+ \DR

Now since
                               |fε (x)| = |f (x)| ≤ 1,        x ∈ R,

the maximum principle also ensures that

                                        sup |fε (z)| ≤ 1.
                                       z∈D R


Therefore, we conclude that
                                        sup |fε (z)| ≤ 1.
                                       z∈C+

Since the bound is independent on ε > 0, we can let ε → 0+ in order to conclude that

                                          sup |f (z)| ≤ 1.
                                        z∈C+


This proves the claim.

Corollary 4.6 (Phragmén-Lindelöf, Quadrants). Consider a rotated quadrant
                                                             π
                         Qθ := {z ∈ C : | arg(z) − θ| <        },      θ ∈ R.
                                                             4


                                                 79
Let f be an analytic function in Qθ which extends continuously to Qθ , and satisfies the
growth estimate:
                           |f (z)| ≤ C exp ( |z|α ) , z ∈ Qθ ,

for some 0 < α < 2, and constant C > 0. Then

                                        sup |f (z)| = sup |f (z)|.
                                       z∈∂Qθ              z∈Qθ


Proof. The proof is an immediate consequence of the Phragmén–Lindelöf Theorem for half-
spaces, by means of mapping C+ conformally onto Qθ via the map
                                                  √
                                      φ(z) := eiθ0 z           z ∈ C+

with θ0 = θ − π4 . Now let f : Qθ → C analytic with continuous extension to Qθ and satisfies
the growth condition
                              |f (z)| ≤ C exp ( |z|α ) , z ∈ Qθ ,

for some 0 < α < 2. Then the composition g = f ◦ ϕ defined an analytic function in C+ ,
which extends to continuously up to the boundary R and satisfies:
                                                   
                              |g(z)| ≤ C exp |z|α/2                 z ∈ C+ .

It follows from Theorem 4.5 that

                     sup |f (z)| = sup |g(z)| = sup |g(z)| = sup |f (z)|.
                   z∈∂Qθ                z∈∂C+              z∈C+          z∈Qθ




   We now turn to the proof the Paley–Wiener Theorem, which is fortunately not too long.

Proof of the Paley-Wiener Theorem. Step 1: Necessity:
First, we note that if f ∈ L2 (R) with supp(fb) ⊆ [−a, a] implies that fb ∈ L1 (R). Using the
inverse formula                                     Z a
                                        f (x) = c         fb(ξ)eixξ dξ
                                                     −a

for some irrelevant normalizing constant c ̸= 0, we may invoke Morera’s Theorem to conclude
that f extends to an entire analytic function. The claim on f being of exponential-type a
follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:
                              Z a
                  |f (z)| ≤         |fb(x)|ex Im(z) dx ≤ ∥fb∥L1 ea| Im z| ,      z ∈ C.
                               −a

Step 2: Sufficiency for bounded f :
We shall primarily assume that f ∈ L∞ (R) and continuous on R with the property that there
exists F ∈ Ea with F = f a.e on R. For any b > a, consider the functions Gb (z) = F (z)eibz ,
which are bounded and continuous on R. Furthermore, on the imaginary axis, we have

                    |Gb (iy)| = |F (iy)|e−by ≤ C(ε)e−y(b−a−ε) ,                 y > 0,



                                                     80
whenever 0 < ε < b − a. Furthermore, Gb also satisfies the following estimate on C+ :

                         |Gb (z)| = |F (z)|e−by ≤ C(ε) exp ((α + ε)|z|) .

Applying the Phragmen–Lindelöf principle for rotated quadrants in Corollary 4.6 on both
the first and the second quadrants, we conclude that Gb ∈ H ∞ (C+ ). Therefore, it follows
that supp(G cb ) ⊆ [0, ∞), and hence supp(fb) ⊆ [−b, ∞). A similar argument applied to G−b
on the lower-half plane C− also implies that G−b ∈ H ∞ (C− ), hence supp(fb) ⊆ (−∞, b]. We
therefore conclude that supp(fb) ⊆ [−b, b] for all b > a. Taking intersects we conclude that
the Fourier spectrum of f is contained in [−a, a].
Step 3: General case:
Let f ∈ L2 (R) and assume there exists a function F ∈ Ea with F = f a.e on R. Pick a
smooth family of compactly supported approximates of identity φε on R, and consider the
function                         Z
                       Fε (z) :=   F (z − x)φε (x)dx,   z ∈ C.
                                         R

If fε denotes the restriction of Fε to the real line, then it is evident that fε = f ∗ φε ∈ Cb (R).
Using a Phragmen-Lindelöf argument similar as in step 2, we also deduce that Fε ∈ Ea .
According to the second step, it therefore follows that

                                         supp(fbε ) ⊆ [−a, a],

for all ε > 0. But since fε → f ∈ L2 (R), it easily follows from Plancherel’s Theorem that
supp(fb) ⊆ [−a, a]. This completes the proof.

   We shall now record two interesting consequences of the Paley–Wiener Theorem with a
uncertainty flavor.
Corollary 4.7 (UP via Paley–Wiener). Let f ∈ L2 (R) non-trivial with compact Fourier
spectrum supp(fb). Then f cannot vanish on a set accumulation points in C, and it satisfies
the growth restriction
                              log |f (z)| ≤ A|z|   |z| > 1,

for some A > 0.
    In other words, elements in L2 (R) with compactly supported Fourier spectrum can nei-
ther vanish too much, nor grow too fast at infinity.
    Our next observation is a more qualitative and discrete refinement of the above obser-
vation, and is attributed to father of information theory, B. Shannon.
Theorem 4.8 (Shannon sampling formula). Let f be a smooth function in R with supp(f ) ⊆
[−a, a] for some a > 0. Then the following sampling formula holds

                                   X  πn  sin(ax − πn)
                         fb(ξ) =    fb                   ,             ξ ∈ R.

                                   n∈Z

                                        Z π

                                         −π

Now regarding f as a smooth 2π-periodic function on R, we see that the Fourier coefficients

                                  Z π

   Note that we do not actually need f to be smooth, it suffices only to require that its

   A consequence of Shannon’s sampling formula is that the Fourier transform of a com-
pactly supported f ∈ L2 (R) cannot vanishes on a dense enough lattice, unless it vanishes
identically.

Corollary 4.9. If 0 < a ≤ b and f ∈ L2 (R) with

                                         sin(εξ) sin(bξ)

supported in [−a, a].


4.4    Measures with spectral gaps
Here, we shall consider investigate some properties of measures with spectral gaps. Let
M (R) denote the space of finite complex Borel measures in R. We say that µ ∈ M (R) has


                                                    82
a spectral gap, if
                                            µ) ∩ I = ∅,
                                       supp(b

for some finite interval I ⊂ R. The following simple observation illustrates a clear manifes-
tation of the uncertainty principle.

Corollary 4.10 (UP via spectral gaps). If µ is a compactly supported complex Borel measure
in R, whose Fourier µ
                    b has a spectral gap, that is,

                                            µ) ∩ I = ∅,
                                       supp(b

for some finite interval I ⊂ R, then µ ≡ 0.

Proof. If supp(µ) ⊆ [−a, a] for some a > 0, then the Fourier transform
                                       Z a
                             µ
                             b(ξ) :=         e−iξx dµ(x)      ξ ∈ C,
                                        −a

extends to an entire analytic function in C. For instance, this can be proved using Morera’s
Theorem. It follows that µb cannot vanish in an arc in R, unless it vanishes identically.


   Moving forward, we shall investigate further properties of measures with spectral gaps.
Given a number a > 0, we denote

                      Ma (R) = {µ ∈ M (R) : supp(b
                                                 µ) ∩ [−a, a] = ∅} ,

which is easily shown to define a closed subspace of M (R), equipped with the usual total
variation norm on R. Define the Bernstein space

                        Ba (R) := {f ∈ L∞ (R) : f ∈ Ea } ,          a>0

equipped with the supremum norm in R. Note that Ba (R) consists of bounded continuous
functions in R with extend to entire analytic functions of exponential type a.
    Our main concern in this subsection is to unravel the following result, and a few of its
far-reaching consequences.

Theorem 4.11 (Pollard). Let a > 0 and µ ∈ M (R), and consider define the associated
Pollard function:
                                                     Z                
              Pµ,a (z) := sup |f (z)| : f ∈ Ba (R),        |f |d|µ| ≤ 1 ,   z ∈ C.
                                                       R

Then whenever µ ∈ Ma (R), we have for any σ ∈ R that

                                   log+ Pµ,a (x + iσ)
                                 Z
                                                      dx < ∞.
                                 R      1 + x2

    Roughly speaking, the Pollard function Pµ,a measures, in a rather indirect way, how
large functions in Ba (R) can become at a given point z ∈ C, under a normalization imposed
by the measure µ. There is no reason to expect that the supremum is attained by a single


                                                83
function f ∈ Ba (R), and in general no such maximizer exists. The Pollard function should
therefore be viewed as a nonlinear envelope capturing the collective behavior of all admissible
functions.
   Pollard’s Theorem reveals a subtle phenomenon, namely that the presence of a spectral
gap imposes a strong global growth restriction on functions in Ba (R) along horizontal lines.
   In order to prove Pollard’s Theorem, we shall need two lemmas. The first lemma links
the Bernstein spaces to measures with spectral gaps.

Lemma 4.12. For any a > 0 we have the equality of sets
                                                    Z                                       
         Ma (R) = Ba (R)⊥ :=           µ ∈ M (R) :        f (x)dµ(x) = 0,         ∀f ∈ Ba (R) .

perturbed functions
                                             sin(εz)

   With Pollard’s Theorem at hand, we shall now record deep result of Beurling, on the
incompatibility between measure having a spectral gap and thin support.

    The condition (17) roughly measures how thin the closed set S is at neighborhoods of
infinity, and the Beurling’s Theorem asserts that measures supported in such thin sets S
must be full Fourier spectrum in R. For instance, it is not difficult to show that {Jk }k is a
disjoint union of open finite Whitney intervals in R, that is, they satisfy

                                        |Jk | ≍ dist(Jk , S)
