    One can actually show that Theorem 4.14 still holds under the weaker assumption that
log W is merely uniformly continuous, but the required modifications of ψx0 become slightly
more tedious, at the costing of making the main ideas behind the proof less transparent.
The curious readers may attempt to strengthen Theorem 4.14 on their own, or consult [36,
P. 215].


4.5    Problems related to uniqueness pairs in L2 (R)
We continue the study of compatibility conditions between the support of a measure on R
and its Fourier spectrum. In this subsection, we restrict our attention to functions in L2 (R)
and address the problem of when one can exhibit non-trivial functions with prescribed
support and Fourier spectrum.
   A function f ∈ L1loc (R) is said to be essentially supported in a Lebesgue measurable
subset E of R, if f = 0 a.e on R \ E. We designate this property by ess supp(f ) ⊆ E.
Given two sets E, F ⊂ R, the pair (E, F ) is a declared to be a uniqueness pair if whenever
f ∈ L2 (R) with
                     ess supp(f ) ⊆ E,     ess supp(fb) ⊆ F =⇒ f ≡ 0.

Note the Plancherel’s Theorem ensures that the notion of uniqueness pairs is symmetric in
the sense that (E, F ) is a uniqueness pair if and only if (F, E) is. Recall that the Paley–


                                             90
Wiener Corollary 4.7 implies that a pair of closed sets (E, F ) is a uniqueness pair if whenever
E ⊊ R and F is a bounded set, and vice versa. Below, we shall investigate further results
of this type. The first principle result goes as follows.

Theorem 4.15 (Amrein–Berthier). Let f ∈ L2 (R) and E, F ⊂ R be sets of finite Lebesgue
measure. Then there exists a constant C(E, F ) > 0, such that
                                                                       
                          ∥f ∥2L2 ≤ C(E, F ) ∥1E c f ∥2L2 + ∥1F c fb∥2L2 .                       (19)

In particular, if both f and fb are essentially supported on sets of finite Lebesgue measure,
then f = 0.

    A pair of Lebesgue measurable subsets (E, F ) is declared to be a strong uniqueness pair
if (19) holds, hence every strong uniqueness pair is clearly a uniqueness pair. The Amrein–
Berthier Theorem asserts that (E, F ) is a strong uniqueness pair if they both have finite
Lebesgue measure. We remark that M. Benedicks proved a weaker result in [8], namely
that (E, F ) is a uniqueness pair if E, F both have finite Lebesgue measure. His proof only
involves the Poisson summation formula and does require the estimate in (19).

Proof of Theorem 4.15. The proof consists of a nice blend of operator theoretical arguments,
conveniently divided into 3 steps.

Step 1: Norm of the localization operators:
Consider the localization operator T f = 1E F −1 (1F Ff ) which is a bounded linear operator
from L2 (R) → L2 (R) with ∥T ∥L2 →L2 ≤ 1. We claim that if α := ∥T ∥L2 →L2 < 1, then the
proof follows. Indeed, set PF (f ) = F −1 (1F Ff ) and note that f = PF (f ) + PF c (f ), and
T (f ) = 1E PF (f ), which implies that

  ∥f ∥L2 = ∥1E f ∥L2 + ∥1E c f ∥L2 ≤ ∥T (f )∥L2 (E) + ∥1E PF c (f )∥L2 + ∥1E c f ∥L2
                                                         ≤ α∥f ∥L2 + ∥fb∥L2 (F c ) + ∥f ∥L2 (E c ) .

Since α ∈ (0, 1), the result follows with C = (1 − α)−1 .

Step 2: Compactness:
We primarily note that L = T ◦ F is actually Hilbert-Schmidt operator,
                                         Z
                       L(f )(x) = 1E (x)   f (y)eixy dy,  x ∈ R,
                                               F


with Hilbert-Schmidt kernel K(x, y) := 1E (x)1F (y)eixy for x, y ∈ R, hence L is compact,
and thus T is compact on T. Now if ∥T ∥L2 →L2 = 1, then by weak compactness of the
unit-ball of L2 and compactness of T , there exists a non-trivial element f ∈ L2 (R) with
∥f ∥L2 →L2 ≤ 1, such that
                                     ∥T (f )∥L2 = 1.

In fact, since ∥T ∥L2 →L2 ≤ 1, we actually also assume that ∥f ∥L2 = 1, hence by Plancherel’s
Theorem                      Z                       Z
                                  −1
                                |F (1F f )(x)| dx = |fb(ξ)|2 dξ.
                                         b     2
                                E                          R




                                                   91
It follows that ess supp(fb) ⊆ F and ess supp(f ) ⊆ E, which is equivalent to the equation
T (f ) = f , that is, 1 is an eigenvalue of T with eigenvector f . It is well-known that each
eigenspace of T is finite dimensional, hence we will reach the desired contradiction once we
can produce an infinitude of linearly independent elements in the eigenspace E corresponding
to the eigenvalue 1.
Step 3: Shifting measurable sets:
Here we shall prove that for any E0 ⊂ E with m(E0 ) > 0 and any 0 < ε < m(E0 ), there
exists a number a ∈ R such that m(E) + ε/2 ≤ m(E ∪ (E0 + a)) ≤ m(E) + ε. To this end,
consider the positive function
                                                     Z
          T (a) := m(E ∪ (E0 + a)) = m(E) + m(E0 ) −   1E (t + a)dt,  a ∈ R,
                                                            E0


which is easily seen to be continuous on R since the translations of L1 -functions are contin-
uous in the L1 -norm. Furthermore, we have T (0) = m(E) while T (a) → m(E) + m(E0 ) as
|a| → ∞, hence the claim readily follows from the intermediate value theorem. With this
lemma at hand, we shall now produce an eigenspace of a localization operator with infinitely
linearly independent vectors. Set E1 = ess supp(f ) ⊂ E, let k0 ≥ 0 with 2−k0 < m(E1 ),
and pick a1 ∈ R such that m(E) + 2−k0 −1 ≤ m(E ∪ (E1 + a1 )) ≤ m(E) + 2−k0 . Recursively
and by means of induction, we can find real numbers (an )∞ n=0 such that for all n = 1, 2, . . . :

          Xn

It therefore follows that (fn )n all satisfy T ′ (fn ) = fn , where T ′ (f )(x) = 1E ∗ F −1 (1F fb)
again is a compact operator on L2 (R). Furthermore, the (fn )n are linearly independent
since Sn+1 \ Sn have positive Lebesgue measure by construction. But this contradicts the
fact that eigenspaces of compact operators are finite dimensional. We therefore conclude
that ∥T ∥L2 →L2 < 1, and proof of the Amrein-Berthier Theorem follows.


   The above proof of the Amrein–Berthier Theorem was almost purely operator theoretical,
and this is no coincidence. In fact, it is equivalent to the following statements on projections.

Proposition 4.16. Let (E, F ) be a pair of Lebesgue measurable subsets of R and consider
the associated orthogonal projections on L2 (R) defined by

                                                  −1
                 PE (f ) = 1E f,       F (f ) = F
                                      Pc             (1F F(f )),     f ∈ L2 (R).

Then the following statements are all equivalent:

  (i) (E, F ) is a strong uniqueness pair.

           F ∥L2 →L2 < 1.

                                            Z                  Z

     for all f ∈ L2 (R) with ess supp(fb) ⊆ F .

with ess supp(f ) ⊆ E and ess supp(fb) ⊆ F , which is in contradiction with (19).

Step 2: (ii) =⇒ (iii):
This is simple, if ∥T ∥L2 →L2 < 1, then I − T is invertible on L2 (R) with inverse given by the
Von Neumann series k≥0 T k .
                       P

                                                   93
    The following quantitative manifestation of the uncertainty principle is worthy of record-
ing.
Corollary 4.17 (UP-Uniqueness Pairs). If there exists a non-trivial function f ∈ L2 (R)
with ess supp(f ) ⊆ E and ess supp(fb) ⊆ F , then |E||F | ≥ 1.
Proof. Let T = PE PcF be the localization operator and note that a straightforward calcula-
tion gives the norm estimate
                                    ∥T ∥L2 →L2 ≤ |E||F |.

Now if |E||F | < 1, then I − T is invertible with the inverse given by a Von Neumann series
as demonstrated in Proposition 4.16, and thus we conclude from the same Proposition
that (E, F ) is a strong uniqueness pair. This implies that there cannot exists a non-trivial
function f ∈ L2 (R) with ess supp(f ) ⊆ E and ess supp(fb) ⊆ F .

   It turns out that strong uniqueness pairs (E, F ) give rise to the following independence
phenomenon: the behavior of f on E has no interference with the behavior of its Fourier
transform fb on F , and vice versa. To make this formal, we record the following corollary of
the Amrein–Berthier Theorem, which allows one to solve the following system of equations.
Corollary 4.18. Let (E, F ) be a strong uniqueness pair. Then for any pair of g, h ∈ L2 (R),
there exists f ∈ L2 (R) such that

                            f = g a.e on E,        fb = h a.e on F.

                                                                   ∗
Proof. Set T = PE Pc F , and note that the adjoint operator is T = PF PE , and we have the
                                                                        c
simple identities I − PE = PE c and I − PcF = PF c . If (E, F ) is a strong uniqueness pair the
                                               d
Proposition 4.16 ensures that I − T is invertible on L2 (R), hence it remains only to verify
that the L2 -function

                                            −1
                      f := (I − Pc
                                 F )(I − T )   g + (I − PE )(I − T ∗ )−1 h

satisfies the properties PE f = g and Pc
                                       F f = h. However, this is essentially pure algebra, as
demonstrated below:

                                 −1
   PE (f ) = PE (I − Pc
                      F )(I − T )   g = (PE − T )(I − T )−1 g = −PE c (1 − T )−1 g + g = g.

In the last step, we use that PE c (1−T )−1 = 0, which readily follows from the Von Neumann
series expansion of T . Similarly Pc F f = h, we omit the details.



   One can also establish periodic versions of the Amrein–Berthier Theorem, which involv-
ing Fourier coefficients. Theorems of these type are attributed to Mikheyev, and turn our
to have some interesting connections to lacunary Fourier series. However, this is beyond
the scope of these notes, and we refer the reader to [36, P. 102] for further details on these
matters.
   As a consequence of the Paley-Wiener Theorem, and as noted in the beginning of the
subsection, any compact set E ⊂ R and closed proper set F ⊊ R gives rise to a uniqueness
pair (E, F ). The following example shows that strong uniqueness pairs with large Fourier
spectrum can also arise.


                                              94
Proposition 4.19. There exists a strong uniqueness pair (E, F ), where E has finite Lebesgue
measure, while F has infinite Lebesgue measure.

we can ensure that the intervals Iλ with λ ∈ ΛN := Λ ∩ {|λ| > N } are all disjoint. Now, set

    Restricting our attention to case of bounded spectrum, our last principal result in this
section gives a metric characterization of measurable sets E ⊂ R, which give rise to strong
uniqueness pairs (E, F ), for any bounded subset F .

Theorem 4.20 (Logvinenko-Sereda). Let E be a measurable subset of R. Then (E, F ) is
a strong uniqueness pair for any bounded measurable set F ⊂ R if and only if, there exists
a number r > 0 such that
                                                  |I \ E|
                                δr (E c ) := inf          > 0,
                                            |I|=r    |I|
where the infimum is taken of over all intervals I ⊂ R of length r.

   In the literature, the above condition on E c is commonly referred to as E c being relatively
r-dense in R. The proof presented here will principally rely on reformulating the notion of
r-density in terms of harmonic measure, in order to deduce a ”two-constant” Theorem.

Lemma 4.21 (Two-constant Lemma). A measurable set S ⊂ R is relatively r-dense for
some r > 0 if and only if the harmonic measure on C+ := {Im z > 0} restricted to the
horizontal line y = 1:
                                                  Z

is bounded below: γ := inf x∈R ωx+i (S) > 0. In that case, the following two-constant type
estimate holds:
             Z                         Z        γ

                |P (f )(x + i)| dx ≤ 2    |f | dx ∥f ∥L2     ,     f ∈ H 2 (R).
                       R                              Ec

Proof. The first statement is just a straightforward estimate involving the Poisson kernel,
whose verification is left to the reader, and we therefore only verify the second claim. Sup-

   There are also different proofs of the Logvinenko-Sereda Theorem which essentially only
involves real-analysis, based on Bernstein-type estimates, and where essentially sharp con-
stants are obtain. For instance, see the recent work of O. Kovrijkine in [63]. Furthermore,
there is also an Lp -version of the ”two-constant” Theorem, which naturally allows one to
generalize the Logvinenko-Sereda to this setting, as well. See P. 115 in [36].


4.6    Further results
Let f be a measurable function on R with the properties that

                         f (x) = O(exp(−ax2 )),              fb(ξ) = O(exp(−bξ 2 ))

for some positive numbers a, b > 0. Now if one considers the dilatation fδ (x) = f (δx) with
δ > 0, then we have

                     fδ (x) = O(exp(−aδ 2 x2 )),             fb(ξ) = O(exp(−bξ 2 /δ 2 ),

Hence such an attempt of improving the decay of f at infinity, worsens the decay of fb, and
vice versa. This suggests the manifestation of an underlying uncertainty principle, which
can formally be stated as follows.




                                                        98
Theorem 4.22 (Hardy’s uncertainty principle). Let f measurable function on R with the
property that there exists positive numbers α, β > 0, and an integer N ≥ 0, such that
                                                      2                                            2
                |f (x)| ≤ C(1 + |x|)N e−απx ,                      |fb(ξ)| ≤ C(1 + |ξ|)N e−βπξ

for some constant C > 0. Then αβ = 1, unless f ≡ 0. Furthermore, there exists a
                                                                2
polynomials P of degree at most N , such that f (x) = P (x)e−απx .

    The proof of Hardy’s uncertainty principal mainly relies on clever applications of the
celebrated Phragmén-Lindelöf Theorem in complex analysis. For instance, a sketch of proof
is outlined by T. Tao in his blog, see [?]. In a similar fashion, we also mention the following
result of A. Beurling, which is less precise, but whose simplicity makes it rather attractive.

Theorem 4.23 (Beurling). If f ∈ L1 (R) with
                                  Z Z
                                              |f (x)||fb(ξ)|e−|x·ξ| dξdx < ∞,
                                      R   R

then f ≡ 0.

    On a historical note, Beurling’s result appeared in his collected works in [12], but the
proof was unfortunately not preserved. In 1990, L. Hörmander successfully recovered the
proof from old notes taken during his conversations with A. Beurling between 1964-1968,
where they spent time together at the Institute for Advanced studies. See [45]. We also
refer to the more recent work of H. Hedenmalm in [39], which gives a different take on the
matter.
    We shall now visit two extensions of the classical Heisenberg uncertainty principle. The
first result is due to M. Cowling and J. F. Price, and is essentially an Lp -version.

Theorem 4.24 (Cowling-Price). Let 1 ≤ p, q ≤ ∞, 0 < α < 1, θ, ϕ > 0 which satisfy the
relationship                                          
                            1 1                    1 1
                    α θ+ −          = (1 − α) ϕ + −        .
                            p 2                    q   2
Then there exists a constant C > 0, possibly depending on all these parameters, such that
for any Schwartz function f on R:
                             Z                        α/p Z                   (1−α)/q
              ∥f ∥L2 ≤ C              |x|θp |f (x)|p dx        |ξ|ϕq |fb(ξ)|q dξ          .
                                 R                                    R

    For further details and other results of similar flavor, we refer the reader to [26]. Next,
we mention an entropic version of the Heisenberg uncertainty principle. The entropic un-
certainty principle was initially conjectured in 1957 by I. Hirschman in [44] and later proved
by W. Beckner in 1975, see [5]. The result goes as follows.

Theorem 4.25 (Entropic UP). For any f ∈ L2 (R) the following inequality holds:
                 Z                                        Z
                                          1                                    1              e
                       |f (x)|2 log            dx +           |fb(ξ)|2 log            dξ ≥ log .
                   R                  |f (x)|2            R                  |fb(ξ)|2         2

   This theorem is rather deep, and hinges on the sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality, re-
garding the operatornorm of the Fourier transform F : Lp (R) → Lq (R), where 1 < p < 2


                                                              99
and q = p/(p − 1). See [5].
    Theorems of two-constant type are classical in analytic function theory, and may some-
what be regarded as integrable version of Hadamards three circle/line Theorem, quantitative
versions of the Phragmén–Lindelöf principle, exploiting convexity. We highlight the follow-
ing classical two-constant Theorem, whose proof can readily be extracted from the argument
displayed the two-constant lemma.

Theorem 4.26 (Two-constant Theorem). Let Ω ⊂ C be Jordan domain. Suppose there
exists a closed set E ⊂ ∂Ω and closed curve Γ ⊂ Ω the property that the harmonic measure
on Ω, evaluated at E wrt reference points in Γ satisfies:

                                    α := inf ω(z, E, Ω) ∈ (0, 1).
                                         z∈Γ


Then for any f ∈ C(∂Ω), the following uniform estimate holds for the harmonic extension
uf to Ω:
                          sup|uf (λ)| ≤ C sup|f |α sup |f |1−α
                              λ∈Γ                    E        ∂Ω\E

where C > 0 is a numerical constant.

    More refined and technical versions of the Logvinenko-Sereda Theorem were established
by C. Shubin, R. Vakilian and T. Wolff in [89, Theorem 2.1]. In the related field of time-
frequency analysis, there is also a version of the Amrein–Berthier Theorem for the generator
of a Gabor system. For instance, see [81] and references therein.
    We complete this chapter by including some remarks in relation recent works on anni-
hilating pairs. In [79], F. Nazarov obtained a quantitative estimate of the Amrein–Berthier
constant
                                     C(E, F ) ≤ CeC|E|·|F |

for any sets E, F of finite Lebesgue measure and where C > 0 is an absolute constant. A
neat proof is contained in [36, Ch. 4, § 11] More recently, A. Kulikov, F. Nazarov, M. Sodin
in [65] considered uniqueness pairs where both sets (Λ, Σ) where discrete subsets of R. To
give a flavor of their result, we only state a mild version of it.
    Let Λ = {λj }j and Σ = {σj }j be real numbers arranged in increasing order and with
limj→±∞ λj = limj→±∞ σj = ±∞. We say that a pair (Λ, Σ) is super critical if

                                               1                                         1
                lim sup |λj |(λj+1 − λj ) <      ,         lim sup |σj |(σj+1 − σj ) <     ,
                |j|→∞                          2           |j|→∞                         2

an we call the pair (Λ, Σ) subcritical if

                                               1                                         1
                lim sup |λj |(λj+1 − λj ) >      ,         lim sup |σj |(σj+1 − σj ) >     ,
                |j|→∞                          2           |j|→∞                         2

A version of their result goes as follows.

Theorem 4.27 (Kulikov–Nazarov–Sodin). Any supercritical pair (Λ, Σ) is a uniqueness
pair for the Schwartz class S(R):

                         f ∈ S(R)        f |Λ = fb|Σ = 0           =⇒ f ≡ 0.


                                                     100
Any subcritical pair (Λ, Σ) is a non-uniqueness pair for S(R).

    The interesting point regarding their work is that their condition of supercritical pairs
naturally arises from the a sharp version of the classical Wirtinger’s inequality, that is, the
one-dimensional analogue of the Poincaré-inequality. Prior, Fourier uniqueness pairs where
consider by M. Viazovska and D. Radchenko, and special applications to the sphere-packing
problem, see [86, 96]. However, these works were based on modular forms, and a great survey
on the connection between Fourier interpolation formulas and optimal tilings of spheres in
a Euclidean space can be found in [24].
    On a related note, we also mention the work of H. Hedenmalm, A. Montes-Rodriguez,
A. Bakan, on Heisenberg uniqueness problems in the setting of hyperbolic Fourier series,
see [41, 42].

Passing to the setting of the real line, we now highlight the role of the global size condition

This identity and standard properties of the Poisson kernel imply that |F (x)| = |f (x)| for
a.e x ∈ R. Now if f ∈ Lp (R) for 1 ≤ p < ∞, then applying Jensen’s inequality to the
identity in (23) yields
           Z                          Z Z                                        Z Z
                                                     y                   dx
                 |F (x + iy)|p dx ≤             2 + (x − t)2
                                                             |f (t)|p dt    =               |f (t)|p dt,
             R                        R     R y                          π         R    R

hence F ∈ H p (C+ ). The case f ∈ L∞ (T) is even simpler.

   We mention that the identity in (23) actually characterizes outer functions F (up to
multiplication with a unimodular constant), and asserts that log |F | is the Poisson extension


                                                       103
of an integrable function in L1 (dP ).


5.2    Bernstein’s problem on weighted approximation
Most of the content in this section is based and intimately related to Bernstein’s problem on
weighted approximation on the real line. Recall that Weierstrass Theorem ensures that any
continuous function on a finite interval of the real line can be approximated by polynomials,
and this cannot naively be extend to yield an uniform approximation in all of R.
   However, a natural way to modify the problem is to introduce a (non-negative) weight-
function W on R, which is large at infinity, and measures the proximity by

                                        |f (x) − p(x)|W (x) ≤ ε,

where p are polynomials. Given a weight W on R, we denote by C0 (W ) the space of
continuous functions f on R with lim|x|→∞ W (x)f (x) = 0 and normed by

                                     ∥f ∥C0 (W ) := sup W (x)|f (x)|.
                                                      x∈R


Now for C0 (W ) to contain the set of all polynomials P, we need to require that W decays
rapidly at infinity:
                           lim W (x)|x|n = 0,      n = 0, 1, 2, . . .
                           |x|→∞

Polynomials approximation:
The celebrated Bernstein problem asks to characterize the weights W such that the poly-
nomials P are dense in C0 (W ):

                           inf ∥f − p∥C0 (W ) = 0,           ∀f ∈ C0 (W ).
                           p∈P


It is useful to dualize the approximation problem using functional analysis. To this end,
we denote by M (W ) the space of locally finite complex Borel measures on R, which are
summable wrt W −1 . That is, we consider the Banach space M (W ) equipped with the norm
                                               Z
                            ||µ||M (W ) :=           W −1 (x)d|µ|(x) < ∞.
                                                 R


Now it is not difficult to see that C0 (W )∗ ∼
                                             = M (W ) in the usual L2 -pairing in R:
                             Z
                                     f (x)dµ(x) ≤ ∥f ∥C0 (W ) ∥µ∥M (W ) .
                                 R

Moment problem:
Now given a rapidly decaying weight W at infinity, we have by the Hahn-Banach theorem
that the polynomials are not dense in C0 (W ) if and only if there exists a non-trivial µ ∈
M (W ) such that its moments satisfy
                                       Z
                        mn (µ) :=           xn dµ(x) = 0,      n = 0, 1, 2, . . .
                                        R

In other words, the dual space M (W ) lacks the property that its elements are uniquely


                                                     104
determined by their moment sequences {mn (µ)}n≥0 . Consider the Fourier transform
                                          Z
                               µ
                               b(ξ) :=         e−ixξ dµ(x),          ξ∈R
                                           R

and note that µ ∈ M (W ) is uniquely determined to its moment sequence if and only if

                          b(n) (0) = 0,
                          µ                     n = 0, 1, 2, · · · =⇒ µ ≡ 0.

Quasi-analyticity:
In other words, the polynomials are dense in C0 (W ) if and only if the class of functions

                                  FM (W ) := {b
                                              µ : µ ∈ M (W )}

is quasi-analytic in R, that is, if f ∈ FM (W ) with f (n) (x0 ) = 0 for all n = 0, 1, 2, . . . and
some point x0 ∈ R, then f ≡ 0.
    We have thus rephrased Bernstein’s problem in three different ways, and it is desirable
to identify reasonably explicit conditions on W , under which these phenomena occur. The
main goal of this subsection is to resolve Bernstein’s problem for a sufficiently rich class of
weights. A complete solution lies beyond the scope of these notes, and further comments
are deferred to the subsection on Further results. The first theorem applies to a broad yet
concrete class of weights. Let P denote the set of polynomials.

Theorem 5.3. Let W be an even weight which decays rapidly at ∞, and is of the form
                                               Z |x|          !
                                                       w(t)
                          W (x) = exp −                     dt ,          |x| ≥ c
                                                 c      t

where c > 0 is a constant and w is a positive non-decreasing function on [c, ∞) with
limx→+∞ w(x) = +∞. Then P is dense in C0 (W ) if
                                      Z
                                         log W (x)
                                                2
                                                   dx = −∞.
                                        R 1+x

    We remark that if W is assumed to be differentiable, then the above specific form of W
is equivalent to the condition that

                                     xW ′ (x)
                                 −            ↑ +∞,            x → ∞.
                                      W (x)

Examples of such eligible weights are, for instance, W (x) = e−k|x| with k > 0, but the
theorem becomes simple for such weights as the Fourier transform of elements in M (W )
extends analytically in a neighborhood of the origin. A more interesting class of weights
consists of those decaying at the rate
                                                                               
                                                 k|x|
            W (x) ≍ exp −                                                           ,   |x| → ∞,
                              log |x| log log |x| . . . log log . . . log |x|

for any finite iterations of logarithms. It is natural to wonder whether the logarithmic
integral divergence is sharp. The following result asserts that it is indeed also necessary.


                                                     105
Theorem 5.4. Let W ∈ L1 (R) be a weight with log W ∈ L1 (dP ). Then there exists a
non-trivial measure µ ∈ M (W ) with identically vanishing moment sequence.

    The take-away from Theorem 5.3 in conjunction with Theorem 5.4 is that for sufficiently
regular weights W , the density of P in C0 (W ) is equivalent to the lack of (global) logarithmic
integrability:                      Z

                                              2
                                                  dx = −∞.
                                     R 1+x

The proof of Theorem 5.4 is short and self-contained, hence we give it first.

                                                         1/2

   In order to prove Theorem 5.3, we shall need a lemma, which extracts the precise way

   We complete this section by recording the following result, which summarizes our dis-
cussion in this subsection.

Corollary 5.6. Let W be a weight satisfying the hypothesis of Theorem 5.3. Then there
exists a non-trivial measure µ ∈ M (W ) with mn (µ) = 0 for n = 0, 1, 2, . . . if and only if
log W ∈ L1 (dP ).

   We remark this corollary is essentially a real-line analogue of Corollary 3.12, which was
derived from Szegö’s Theorem in the unit-circle.




                                                     108
5.3    Problems on determining majorants
A slight modification of Bernstein’s problem on weighted polynomial approximation, rephrased
as a problem on quasi-analyticicity, goes as follows. Describe the weights W on R, for which
there exists a non-trivial function f ∈ L1 (R) with the following properties:

 (1.) |f (x)| ≤ W (x), for all |x| ≥ R sufficiently large,

 (2.) The Fourier transform fb has a zero of infinite order at the origin:

                                      fb(ξ) = O(|ξ|n ),      ξ → 0.

      for all integers n ≥ 0.

    We declare W to be a d-majorant (determining majorant) if there exists no such non-
trivial function f ∈ L1 (R). In other words, we are interested in exhibiting a non-trivial
absolutely continuous measure f dm ∈ M (W ). The main result in this subsection goes as
follows.

Theorem 5.7. Let W be a weight in L1 (R). For W to be a d-majorant it is necessary
that log W ∈/ L1 (dP ). If W additionally satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem 5.3, then the
condition log W ∈/ L1 (dP ) is also sufficient.

Proof. Step 1: Necessity:
The proof is very similar to that of Theorem 5.4, we only sketch it. Arguing by contra-
position, assume that log W ∈ L1 (dP ). Set w(x) := W (x) exp(−|x|1/2 ) and note that
log w ∈ L1 (dP ) and w ∈ L1 (R). We can therefore form the outer function f ∈ H 1 (R) such
that |f | = w a.e on R. Now since xn w(x) is integrable for all integers n ≥ 0, we conclude
that fb ∈ C ∞ (R), and since supp(fb) ⊆ [0, ∞), we must have that fb(n) (0) = 0 for all integers
n = 0, 1, 2, . . . . Since w ≤ W , we conclude that W is not a d-majorant.

Step 2: Sufficiency:
                                                                     / L1 (dP ). Let
Now suppose that W satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem 5.3 and log W ∈

        (i) |f (x)| ≤ W (x),     |x| > R      (ii) fb(ξ) = O(|ξ|n ),    ξ → 0,         n ≥ 0.

Since fb is continuous on R we have fb(0) = 0, and inductively, we can deduce that fb is
infinitely differentiable at ξ = 0 with fb(n) (0) = 0 for n = 0, 1, 2, . . . . Now since W decreases
rapidly at infinity, we can differentiate inside the integral to conclude that fb ∈ C ∞ (R), hence
the moments of f : mn (f dm) = 0 for all n = 0, 1, 2, . . . . Now consider F (z) := K(f )(z + i)

   We shall now consider a unilateral version of the problem on d-majorants. Suppose we
have a weight W and a non-trivial function f ∈ L1 (R) with the property that

                                 |f (x)| ≤ W (x),            x > c.                     (26)

Let us first examine the simplest scenario when W ≡ 0. In that case, the right translation
of fc of f by c is supported in (−∞, 0], hence by the F. and M. Riesz Theorem we have

                                      log |fb(ξ)|
                                     Z
                                              2
                                                  dx > −∞.
                                     R 1+x


This implies that fb cannot vanish on a set of positive measure in R. On the contrary, the
complex conjugate of any H 1 (R)-function satisfies such an estimate, hence one can generally
not find any better restriction on the zero set of fb. However, one can easily construct a
non-trivial f ∈ H 1 (R), which is smooth on R and flat at 0 ∈ R:

                               f (k) (0) = 0,         k = 0, 1, 2, . . .

This implies that the unilateral condition is much weaker than the two-sided. However, it
               R
turns out that R log W dP = −∞ still imposes a restriction on how small fb can be.
                                                                              R
Theorem 5.8 (Unilateral d-majorant). Let W be an even weight which satisfies R log W dP =
−∞. Then whenever f ∈ L1 (R) is a non-trivial function satisfying the unilateral

                                  |f (x)| ≤ W (x),           x>c

then its Fourier transform fb cannot vanish on a set of positive Lebesgue measure on R.

   We now turn to the proof of Beurling’s Theorem, which requires a bit more work.

Proof of Theorem 5.9. We may without loss of generality assume that ∥µ∥M (R) ≤ 1. Con-

5.5    A problem with many faces - Completeness, Moments and Ze-
       ros
We close this chapter by considering a more classical moment-type problem, with the prin-
cipal intent of illustrating how it relates to several others classical problems.
    Let Λ be a sequence of positive real numbers. We ask for which Λ does the following
moment-type uniqueness property for measures supported in a finite compact interval I ⊂
[0, ∞): any complex finite Borel measure µ supported in I satisfies the uniqueness property:
                           Z
                                tλ dµ(t) = 0,            ∀λ ∈ Λ =⇒ µ ≡ 0.
                            I

Denote by C(I) the Banach space of continuous functions on I equipped with the usual
supremum norm. Using that the fact that the dual space of C(I) consists of complex finite
Borel measures µ on I, we see that our moment-type uniqueness problem is equivalent to
the completeness problem of asking for which sequences Λ the linear manifold

                      P(Λ) := Span{tλ : λ ∈ Λ}                  dense in      C(I).

For simplicity, and without loss of generality, we may assume that I = [0, 1]. These problems
dates back to the work of H. Müntz and O. Szász, and have the following satisfactory answer.

Theorem 5.14 (Müntz-Szász). P(Λ) is dense in C(I) if and only if 1 ∈ Λ and

tems for L2 (I), which are extremely sensitive to removal of a single, the system of monomials
tλ with λ ∈ Λ is rather insensible. As we shall see from the proof below, the completeness
result also holds in the framework of Lebesgue spaces Lp (I) for 1 ≤ p < ∞. In the proof be-
low, we shall illustrate that the condition (27) intrinsically arises from the characterization
of zero sets of functions in the Hardy spaces.

5.6    Further results
There is an improvement of the Beurling-Cartwright-Levinson Theorems by A. Volberg,
which goes as follows.
                                                                                 √
Theorem 5.15 (Volberg). Let M : [1, ∞) → [0, ∞) be a concave function with M (t)/ t
increasing in a neighborhood at infinity, and such that
                                    Z ∞
                                           M (t)
                                                 dt = +∞.
                                     1      t2

Then any non-trivial f ∈ L1 (T) with fb(n) = O(e−M (|n|) ), as n → −∞, must satisfy
                                    Z
                                          log |f |dm > −∞.
                                      T

    We remark that proof involves a mixture of very intricate potential theory and E. M.
Dyn’kin’s work on pseudo-analytic continuations of functions. A detailed proof can be found
in [36] and also P. Koosis [55]. Further refinements and continuations of such results were
also carried out by A. Borichev, which also be located in [36]. Now there are local versions
of the above results, for M which decay slower than that of Volberg. The following result
is recent and due to B. Malman, and shows that when the spectral decay is slightly slower,
then one retrieves a local version of Volberg’s Theorem.




                                                118
Theorem 5.16 (Malman, 2024). Let µ ∈ M (T) whose Fourier coefficients satisfy the fol-
lowing rapid one-sided decay:

                              b(n) = exp(−cn1/2 ),
                              µ                            n → ∞.

Then dµ = f dm with f ∈ L1 (T), and the ess supp(f ) can be chosen to be an open set U ,
with the property that for any ζ ∈ U , there exists an arc I(ζ) such that
                                    Z
                                            log |f |dm > −∞.
                                     I(ζ)


    We note that the above result may also be regarded as a generalizing the brothers
Riesz Theorem. The proof of Malman’s Theorem in [76] involves an interesting problem
on polynomial approximation in the unit-disc and extends upon the former work of S.
Khrushchev in [51].
    A more detailed treatment of these problems can be found in [55] by P. Koosis, with
many interesting historical notes. It is worth mentioning that L. de Branges introduced a
different perspective to the Bernstein problem, which later led to several improvements. we
only mention a few great series of recent works in this direction: the work of A. Borichev
and M. Sodin in [15], and also by A. Poltoratski in [85].


6     Small support and amplitudes
6.1    Fourier majorants
Recall that the classical Parseval Theorem implies that if S is a non-trivial distribution on
the unit-circle T with Fourier coefficients:

                         S(n)
                         b    := S(ζ 7→ ζ −n ),       n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .

satisfying                              X
                                          S(n)|2 < ∞,
                                         |b
                                        n

then S must be an L2 -function on T, and thus be supported on a set of positive Lebesgue
measure. From this point of view, one encounters yet another manifestation of the uncer-
tainty principle, asserting that if we can to construct a distribution or measure with small
support in the sense of Lebesgue measure, then their Fourier coefficients are bounded to have
large ℓ2 -mass. It is thus natural to further investigate a qualitative threshold for this phe-
nomenon. For instance, we may ask if any positive sequence (w(n))∞                      2
                                                                              P
                                                                     n=0 with    n w(n) := ∞
an admissible Fourier majorant for a distribution S with support on a set of zero Lebesgue
measure in T? In other words, does there exists a distribution S on T, supported on a set
of Lebesgue measure zero, such that

                            |S(n)|
                             b     ≤ w(|n|),         n = ±1, ±2, . . .

The following remarkable result originates for the profound work of Ivashev-Musatov [46],
which was later refined and improved by T. W. Körner in [57, 58, 59].


                                               119
Theorem 6.1 (Ivashev-Musatov, Körner). Let (w(n))∞
                                                  n=0 be a sequence of positive numbers
which satisfy the following properties:
               2
     P
 (i)    n w(n) = +∞,

 (ii) there exists a constant C > 1 such that for any integer n ≥ 1:

                          C −1 w(n) ≤ w(k) ≤ Cw(n),             n ≤ k ≤ 2n.

There there exists a probability measure µ supported on a set of Lebesgue measure zero on
T, such that
                           |b
                            µ(n)| ≤ w(|n|),    n = ±1, ±2, . . . .

   Roughly speaking, the theorem asserts that square divergent sequences whose oscillation
between n and 2n is controlled are admissible Fourier majorants for measure with small
support. The initial proof of Ivashev–Musatov required more restrictive regularity conditions
on (w(n))n , but several of these we successively removed by Körner.
   Now it is now natural to ask whether the additional regularity condition in (ii) is neces-
sary. Although, it is well-known that the condition (ii) may likely be relaxed, the following
result demonstrates that it cannot simply be removed.

Theorem 6.2 (Körner). There exists decreasing sequence of positive numbers (w(n))n with
the property n w2 (n) = ∞, but any non-trivial distribution S on T with
            P


                          |S(n)|
                           b     ≤ Cw(|n|),         n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .

for some constant C > 0, must have full support in T.

   Note that Theorem 6.2 has the additional property that no compact subset E ⊊ T can
support a non-trivial distribution S with

                          |b
                           S(n)| ≤ Cw(|n|),         n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .

In the proceeding sections, we shall also prove a one-sided analogue of the above result. It
will be deduced from the Cartwright–Levinson Theorem. With these perspectives in mind,
the following complementary result was recently obtained by the author in [68].

Theorem 6.3 (Limani). Let (w(n))∞  n=0 be a sequence of positive real numbers with w(n) ↓ 0,
and 0 < δ < 1 be number. Then there exists a compact set E = E(δ) ⊂ T with m(E) > 1 − δ
such that any non-trivial µ ∈ M (E) satisfies with

                                         |b
                                          µ(n)|
                                     sup        = +∞.
                                     n≥0 w(n)


   Note that w cannot even be the unilateral Fourier majorant of a measure µ ∈ M (E).


6.2    The Ivashev-Musatov Theorem
This section presents the principal lemma underlying the proof of the Ivashev-Musatov
Theorem. The lemma involves the construction of smooth functions that are both highly
localized and possess uniformly small amplitudes, which may be of independent interest.


                                              120
Theorem 6.4 (Principal IM-Lemma). Let {w(n)}∞     n=0 positive numbers satisfying the hy-
pothesis (i) − (ii) of the IM-Theorem. Then for any 0 < ε < 1, there exists ψε ∈ C ∞ (T),
such that

 (I) ψε ≥ 0 on T,
     R
(II) T ψε dm = 1,

(III) m(supp(ψε dm)) ≤ ε,

(IV) |ψ
      cε (n)| ≤ w(|n|), for all n ̸= 0.

   Note that the regularity condition (ii) of {w(n)}n implies that there exists a number
M = M (w) > 1 such that w(1)n−M ≤ w(n) for all n ≥ 1. This observation implies that any
f ∈ C ∞ (T) automatically satisfies the condition (IV ) for large enough n, and therefore the
lemma is intrinsically a statement about the existence of smooth functions with uniformly
small amplitudes of ”lower” frequencies. With the Principal lemma at hand, one can easily
obtain the Ivashev-Musatov Theorem as an immediate corollary, as we demonstrate below.

Proof of the Ivashev-Musatov Theorem. Consider the family of probability measures dµε :=
ψε dm appearing in the prinicap IM-Lemma, and apply the Helly selection Theorem in order
to extract a weak-star cluster point µ, which again is a probability µ on T by (I) and (II),
and according to (III) must be supported on a set of Lebesgue measure zero. At last, the
uniform bound in (IV ) in conjunction with the weak-star convergence readily gives

                        |b
                         µ(n)| ≤ lim sup|ψ
                                         cε (n)| ≤ w(|n|),     ∀n ̸= 0.
                                   ε→0+




   We shall now comment on the conventional approach in proving the Ivashev-Musatov
Theorem, which involves oscillatory integrals. First, observe that if f ∈ C ∞ (T) and note
that fN (ζ) = f (ζ N ) where N > 0 is a large integer, then supp(fN ) = supp(f ) remains
small, since composition with ζ N constitutes to spreading it out the support of supp(f )
among N uniformly spread arcs. Meanwhile, the frequencies of fc N are now only supported
on integer multiples of N , with
                                        
                                        fb(k)   , n = Nk
                               fc
                                N (n) =
                                        0      ,n ∈/ N Z.

In other words, this procedure annihilates frequencies at a fixed arithmetic scale N , and
shifts forward the amplitudes of f . However, this procedure does not shrink the lower order
Fourier coefficients of f , thus a natural idea is to substitute ζ N by a suitable unimodular
function U (ζ), with the intent further decreasing the amplitudes of small frequencies. More
specifically, let U be a unimodular function: |U | = 1 on T, and consider the composition
fU (ζ) := f (U (ζ)). If we further impose that U has a holomorphic extension to {|z| < 1} i.e
it is an inner function, the supp(U b ) ⊆ [0, ∞), hence means of multiplying U by ζ, we may
                    R
wlog assume that T U (ζ)dm(ζ) = 0. It follows that fU non-negative if f is, fc    U (0) = f (0),
                                                                                          b
and then Löwner’s lemma on harmonic measures ensures that m(supp(fU )) = m(supp(f )).
In case U is not inner, then one needs to make sure that pre-images of U do not distort the


                                              121
measure too much. Now, assuming such arrangements have been made, we note that since
f is smooth on T, the Fourier coefficients of fU can be computed via
                                     X        Z
                          fU (n) =
                           c             f (k) U k (ζ)ζ −n dm(ζ)
                                         b
                                      k        T


which indicated how the framework of oscillatory integrals and stationary phase arguments
enter the picture. Now, to cook-up a unimodular function U which ”cancels” out the
large amplitudes of the lower frequencies f , is arguably where the crucial difficulty lies,
and cannot comprehensibly be outlined as the previous steps. This is roughly the initial
approach taken by Ivashev-Musatov in [46], and was further simplified by T. W. Körner in
[57, 58], but required stronger additional regularity assumptions on the Fourier majorant
{w(n)}n . Below, we shall follow a different route suggested by T. W. Körner in his further
work in [59], which involves carefully construction of smooth functions with huge oscillations
designed to cancel out lower order frequencies.
    The following lemma summarizes how the hypothesis (ii) of Theorem 6.1 will be used
throughout. By means of substituting w(n) with min(w(n), 1), we may from now on, and
onward and without loss of generality, always assume that w(n) ≤ 1 for all n.

Lemma 6.5. Let {w(n)}n≥0 be positive real numbers satisfying the hypothesis (ii) of the
IM-Theorem. Then there exists a number M = M (w) > 1 such that:
                                      m n M
                        w(m) ≤ min      ,     w(n),         m, n ≥ 0.
                                       n m
   In particular, the above lemma implies that {w(n)}n has at most polynomial decay/growth.

Proof. Let C(w) > 1 be the constant appearing in the hypothesis (ii) of the IM-Theorem.
Repeatedly using the assumption (ii) on {w(n)}n , we get

with M := log2 C(w). The lemma now follows by switching the roles of m, n ≥ 1 in the
above argument.

   Our principal tool for proving the principal lemma will be based on this fine building-
block by T. W. Körner.

Lemma 6.6 (T. W. Körner, Lemma 2.2 in [59]). For any integer M > 1, there exists a
constant C(M ) > 0, such that the following statement holds. For any closed arc I ⊆ T

    This lemma is quite sophisticated and basically allowed T. W. Körner to circumvent
the previous rather cumbersome approach involving oscillatory integrals. Its proof is not
difficult, albeit technical and deviates from the general theme presented here. We do not
include it.
    Our first step towards the principal lemma will require the following stepping-stone,
which crucially makes use of the assumptions (i) − (ii) of the IM-Theorem.

Lemma 6.7. Let (w(n))∞    n=0 be positive real numbers satisfying the hypothesis (i)−(ii) in the

                            w(2m ) ≤ 2−M |m−l| w(2l ),        m, l ≥ 0.

6.3    The indispensable regularity condition
Here prove that the condition (ii) in the IM-Theorem cannot simply be removed. To this
end, we shall need the following manifestation of the uncertainty principle, which asserts
that if a (pseudo)measure µ attains a substantial portion of its mass from Fourier coefficients
up to order N , and its coefficients tremendously drop in value, the the support of µ is bound
to spread around T.

Lemma 6.8 (Körner-Meyer). For any γ, δ, N > 0, there exists ε = ε(γ, δ, N ) > 0, such that
the following statement holds: whenever S is a distribution on T with
                                    X
                                                  2
                              (i)           |S(n)|
                                             b      ≥ γ,         (ii) sup |S(n)|
                                                                           b     ≤ ε,
                                                                     |n|>N
                                    |n|≤N


then supp(S) meets every δ-neighborhood of T, i.e, it is δ-dense.

Proof. The proof is neat argument by contradiction, which Körner attributes to Y. Meyer.
Suppose the conclusion fails for some triple (γ, δ, N ), hence we can find distributions (Sk )k on
T and numbers εk ↓ 0, where each Sk satisfies (i)−(ii) with εk , but supζ∈T dist(supp(Sk ), ζ) ≥
δ. Considering a finite partition {Ij }j of T into arcs of length δ/10, say, we see by the pigeon
hole principle that there exists at least one arc I0 , for which

                                                 supp(Sk ) ∩ I0 = ∅,

for infinitely many k’s. Therefore, by means of passing to such a subsequence, we may


                                                           126
assume that
                X
                       ck (n)|2 ≥ γ,
                      |S                    sup |S
                                                 ck (n)| ≤ εk ,            supp(Sk ) ∩ I0 = ∅.
                                           |n|>N
              |n|≤N


Now since (Sb( n))n with k = 1, 2, defines a uniformly bounded sequence in ℓ∞ , we can apply
Helly’s selection Theorem in order to extract a weak-star cluster point in ℓ∞ , which gives
rise to a distributions S on T with these pre-scribed Fourier coefficients. Since εk → 0, and
N > 0 is fixed, we obtain
              X
                      S(n)|2 ≥ γ,
                     |b                 S(n) = 0,
                                        b                  |n| > N,           supp(S) ∩ I0 = ∅.
             |n|≤N


But this forces S to be a trigonometric polynomials which vanishes on an arc I0 . Recall the
simple observation that if S is a trigonometric polynomial of order N , then Q(z) := z N S(z)
extends to an analytic polynomial on z ∈ C, which vanishes at the same points as S does.
We therefore conclude that S vanishes identically, which immediately contradicts that a
portion of its ℓ2 -mass exceeded γ > 0.

   We are now ready to complete the proof of Körner’s Theorem.

6.4    Supporting sets for Fourier majorants

    Under additional regularity condition on w, this theorem can essentially be proved using

is far from enough.

such that any non-trivial distribution in T with Fourier majorant w has full support. How-

    Another beautiful result due to T. W. Körner is a topological analogue of the Ivashev–
Musatov Theorem, where the notion ”small” in the sense of zero Lebesgue measure is
substituted by small in topological sense.
Theorem 6.13 (Körner). Let (w(n))∞
                                  n=0 be a sequence of positive numbers which satisfy
the following properties:
      P
  (i)    n w(n) = +∞,

There there exists a positive function f ∈ L∞ (T) such that supp(f dm) has no interior, but

                            |fb(n)| ≤ w(|n|),           n = ±1, ±2, . . . .

    Note again that in view of the Körner-Meyer Theorem, the additional regularity condi-
tion in (ii) cannot simple be removed. The topological Ivashev-Musatov Theorem essentially
touches on the problem of finding the threshold of Fourier majorants (w(n))n , for which not
every function f with |fb(n)| ≤ w(|n|) is continuous on T. Conversely, this begs the question
of how wild the Fourier majorants of continuous functions can be. It turns out that this
question can be settled in a surprisingly sharp way.
Theorem 6.14 (de Leeuw–Kahane–Katznelson). Let (an )n be a sequence of complex num-
bers with n |an |2 < ∞. Then there exists f ∈ C(T) such that
         P


                              |fb(n)| ≥ |an |,      n = 0, ±1, ±2, . . .

   For a short beautiful proof of this result, which differs from its original one in [27], and
involves probabilistic techniques, we refer the reader to Y. Katznelson in [50].


                                                  131
7     The Beurling–Malliavin Theorem(s)
Here we consider a line of work originating in the profound contributions of A. Beurling
and P. Malliavin, which may justifiably be regarded as among the deepest developments in
twentieth-century harmonic analysis. The theory is both subtle and far-reaching, and its
influence is reflected, for instance, in the extensive treatment given by P. Koosis in his two
volumes devoted to the subject, [55, 53]. In these notes, we shall primarily focus on the
so-called Beurling–Malliavin multiplier theorem. As is customary, and for brevity, we will
refer to it simply as the BM-1 theorem.


7.1    The Beurling-Malliavin multiplier Theorem
Let w be a bounded weight function on R and a > 0 be a real number. We say that the
weight w is a-admissible if there exists a non-trivial element f ∈ L1 (R) such that

                 (a.) diam supp(fb) < 2a,     (b.) |f (x)| ≤ w(x),    x ∈ R.             (33)

In other words, a-admissible weights are majorants of integrable functions in R with Fourier
spectrum contained on an interval of length 2a. If w is a-admissible for some a > 0, we
simple refer to it as a BM-majorant, short for Beurling–Malliavin majorant. The problem is
to find ”optimal” conditions of w so that it becomes a BM-majorant. The first observation
is that the logarithmic integrability
                                    Z
                                      log w(x)
                                            2
                                               dx > −∞
                                     R 1+x

is necessary in order for w to be a BM-majorant. Indeed, this essentially a consequence
of the F. and M. Riesz in the upper-half plane, which characterizes majorants of functions
with semi-bounded Fourier spectra.

Proposition 7.1 (F. and M. Riesz Theorem). There exists a non-trivial f ∈ L1 (R) with
                                                        R
semi-bounded spectrum satisfying |f | ≤ w if and only if R log wdP > −∞.

Proof. First, if w ∈ L∞ (R) and log w ∈ L1 (dP ), then we can form the outer function
f ∈ H ∞ (C+ ) with |f | = w a.e on R. Conversely, if there exists a non-trivial f ∈ L1 (R)
with semi-bounded spectrum and such that |f | ≤ w a.e on R. Then there exists exists a
translate of f which agree with boundary values of an H 1 (R)-function a.e on R. But then
by the F. and M. Riesz Theorem, we have
                            Z            Z
                               log wdP ≥    log |f |dP > −∞.
                              R              R




   However, it turns out that the log-integrability of w is far from sufficient in order for
w to be a BM-majorant. Indeed, the Paley-Wiener Theorem implies that if the Fourier
transform fb is compactly supported, then f defines an entire analytic function in C, thus
any BM-majorant w cannot vanish along a sequence of real numbers which accumulate, nor
can it have a zero of infinite order.



                                             132
    On the other hand, it is not difficult to construct admissible majorants for measures with
semi-bounded spectrum that have these properties. Roughly speaking, these observations
indicate that BM-majorants cannot be zero too often, but the main result essentially asserts
that the principal enemy is the oscillation of w. One of the cleanest forms (i.e involving a
quantitative condition) of the Beurling–Malliavin multiplier Theorem can phrased as follows.
                                                                        R
Theorem 7.2 (Strong BM1). Let w be a bounded weight on R with R log wdP > −∞ and
log w is uniformly continuous. Then w is a-admissible for any a > 0.

   In the work of V. Havin, J. Mashreghi, F. Nazarov in [77] the BM-theorem is phrased
under the weaker and more quantitative assumption that

                                              w(x)
                                        sup        < ∞.
                                      |x−y|≤1 w(y)


This roughly asserts that log w has uniformly bounded oscillation on intervals of fixed length.
In particular, the condition is satisfied if log w is uniformly continuous in R. More commonly,
the BM-Theorem is stated under the hypothesis that log w is Lipschitz continuous on R.
However, Theorem 7.2 can readily be deduced from that assumption, by means of smoothing
the majorant with an appropriate approximate of the identity.
    It is well-known that the BM1 Theorems are close to sharp in several respects, and it
is therefore not completely surprising that their proofs are quite difficult and long, despite
both joint and individual efforts of several mathematicians, see [36, 77, 55] and references
therein. In this section, we shall prove certain milder forms of the BM1-Theorem, under
other slightly different regularity conditions on w, and briefly explain how the strong BM1
Theorem can be attained from its milder counterparts.


7.2    Non-admissible BM-majorants and a mild BM1 Theorem
As mentioned in the previous subsection, the Paley-Wiener Theorem infers some additional
restrictions for bounded and log-integrable weights to be admissible BM-majorants. Our
main objective in this subsection is to prove a mild version of the BM1-Theorem, under the
hypothesis that we remove the ability for w to oscillate. In order to increase the readers
appetite for these results, we shall strategically first examine two instructive scenarios that
illustrate how certain sophisticated oscillatory behavior of w can prevent it from being a
BM -majorant. Our first example involves a weight which decreases way too fast at infinity.

Example 7.3 (Rapid unilateral decay at infinity). Fix a positive integer n ≥ 1 and let
(tnj )j≥1 be an enumeration of the dyadic points πk2−n for integers k ≥ n2n located on the
positive real-axis (0, ∞). Pick any increasing positive function θ on [0, ∞) with
                                        ∞
                                        X   θ(j)
                                                 = +∞.
                                        j=1
                                             j2

Define a bounded weight w on R which interpolates the values

                              w(tnj ) = e−θ(j) ,     j = 1, 2, 3, . . .

and such that the graph of w looks like a zig-zag function with spikes at the points (tnj ), in


                                               133
such a way that the we maintain finite logarithmic integral:
                                   Z
                                      log w dP > −∞.
                                              R

We leave it to the reader so verify that such a construction is possible, recalling that we
have the freedom to even further specify θ. Now suppose f ∈ L1 (R) with the properties that
supp(fb) ⊆ [−2n , 2n ] and such that |f | ≤ W a.e on R. Then fb is a continuous compactly
supported function on R, thus f is also continuous on R. In particular, this implies that
µ ∈ M (R) defined by dµ = fbdx satisfies
                                                                    

By the Cartwright–Levinson Theorem, we must have that µ ≡ 0, hence w cannot be 2n -
admissible. However, nothing forbids w from being a-admissible for some a > 2n . This
example illustrates that BM -majorants may suddenly fail to be a-admissible at smaller
scales of a > 0.

    Our next example involves the construction of bounded continuous weights with wild
oscillations, yet are log-integrable wrt dP , but are non-admissible at any scale.

Example 7.4 (Wild oscillations). We shall construct a positive bounded continuous weight
w on R with log w ∈ L1 (dP ), which is not a BM-majorant. We primarily note that if
f ∈ L1 (R) with supp(fb) ⊆ [−a, a], then f ′ ∈ L∞ (R) with

         |f (x)| ≤ |f (xk )| + L(a)(xk+1 − xk ) ≤ w(xk ) + L(a)ℓk ,              x ∈ [xk , xk+1 ]

so that R log wdP > −∞. One should think w being very oscillatory, whose graph roughly

dP , hence f ≡ 0 by the F. and M. Riesz Theorem. Therefore such oscillatory w can never be


                                                    134
BM-majorants, even though they are positive bounded continuous weights, logarithmically
integrable wrt dP .

   With the above examples in mind, we now prove that log-integrability in conjunction
with complete lack of oscillation ensures admissible BM-majorants.

Theorem 7.5 (Mild BM1). Let w be an even function on R which is decreasing on [0, ∞),
             R
and satisfies R log wdP > −∞. Then w is a-admissible for any a > 0.
                                          1/2
Proof. Set W (x) = cw(2x)e−|2x| , where c > 0 constant to be determined momentarily.
Since log w is Poisson-integrable, so is log W , and the rapid-decay also ensures that W ∈
L1 (R). We can therefore find an outer function F ∈ H 1 (R), such that |F | = W a.e on R.
For any given a > 0, we may by means of multiplying F with eiσx ∈ H ∞ (R) for some σ > 0,
we may assume that Fb(a) ̸= 0. Now put

                                  g(x) = Fb(a − x)Fb(a + x),         x ∈ R,

which satisfies g(0) = (Fb)2 (a) ̸= 0, supp(gdm) ⊆ [−a, a], and g ∈ C ∞ (R). Now, a straight-

              Z                       Z

                      t≤ξ/2       t>ξ/2


provided c > 0 small enough. This proves that w is a-admissible majorant for any given

    Note that the above proof was fairly simple and required no additional tools than the
definition of an outer function. However, rather strong regularity assumptions on W were
imposed, which on the upside are fairly practical to verify.


7.3    The BM-Theorem via subharmoncicity and the ∂-equation
Here, we shall prove yet another version of the Beurling–Malliavin multiplier Theorem,
which is more accessible than the strong BM1–Theorem. Here, we follow a slightly different
route than the proofs presented in [36], and also in [77]. Instead, we shall here carry out
a proof which was recently suggested in the work of A. Cohen in [23], whose cental idea is
attributed to J. Bourgain. Here we recall if µ ∈ M (R) the Hilbert transform of µ is defined
as                                         Z
                                         1         dµ(t)
                       H(µ)(x) := lim                    ,    x ∈ R.
                                   ε→0+ π |t−x|>ε t − x

Our principal effort in this subsection will be to prove the following Theorem.
                                                                        R
Theorem 7.6 (Medium BM1). Let w be a bounded weight on R with R log wdP > −∞. If
log w is uniformly Lipschitz continuous on R and if

                                            H((log w)′ ) ∈ L∞ (R),


                                                     135
then w is a-admissible for all a > ∥H((log w)′ )∥L∞ (R) .

   The proof proceeds in three principal steps:

  1.) Reformulate the problem as an obstacle-type problem in complex analysis,

  2.) Solve a subharmonic version of the BM1-Theorem,

  3.) Upgrade the solution using Hörmander’s ∂-method.

In the present setting, the first step is straightforward, owing to the availability of the Pa-
ley–Wiener Theorem. However, in attempting to extend the Beurling–Malliavin Theorem to
contexts where no Theorem of Paley–Wiener-type is available, one encounters a substantial
obstruction. This roughly explain why such generalizations are notoriously difficult beyond
the classical framework of functions of finite bandwidth. We return to this point in the
subsection on further discussions. Once the problem has been recast via the Paley–Wiener
Theorem, the second step becomes almost canonical. Variants of this approach already
appear in [55]. Having established a subharmonic version of the BM1-Theorem, it is then
natural to seek a holomorphic solution via ∂-methods. The implementation of Hörmander’s
∂-theory, however, requires some care and constitutes the main technical difficulty.
Step 1: Reformulation with Paley-Wiener:
To thid end, let us invoke the Paley-Wiener Theorem, in order to reformulate the BM1-
Theorem. For any number a > 0, we would like to find an entire analytic function fa which
satisfies the following properties:

  (i) |fa | ≤ w on R,

 (ii) |fa (x + iy)| ≤ Cea|y| for some C > 0 and all x, y ∈ R,

(iii) fa (0) ̸= 0.

Note that the third condition ensures that fa is non-trivial, and is indeed by a simple
translation argument seen to be both necessary and sufficient. Technically, the first condition
only needs to hold a.e on R, but since fa is continuous and we can apply a convolution of
w with an appropriate smooth approximate of the identity, in order to pass to a smooth
weight w, which is pointwise comparable to w.
Step 2: A subharmonic BM -Theorem:
Note that the above growth conditions on fa readily translate to the corresponding sub-
harmonic function ua := log|fa | on C. This makes it rather natural to consider the fol-
lowing weaker problem on subharmonic functions, often referred to as the subharmonic
BM-problem. More precisely, we would like to find a subharmonic function ua on C with
the following properties:

  (i) |ua | ≤ log w on R,

 (ii) |ua (x + iy)| ≤ C + a|y| for some C > 0 and all x, y ∈ R,

(iii) ua (0) > −∞.

It turns out that this problem can be solved rather exactly, under certain additional regu-
larity assumption(s) on w.


                                              136
Proposition 7.7 (Exact subharmonic BM-Problem). Let w be a bounded weight on R with
    log wdP > −∞. Assume that log w is Lipschitz continuous on R and H((log w)′ ) ∈
R
  R
L (R). Then for any a > ∥H((log w)′ )∥L∞ , there exists a subharmonic function ua on C,
  ∞

which is Lipschitz continuous in C, and satisfies

                    u|R = log w,         u(x + iy) ≤ C + a|y|,         x, y ∈ R.

Proof. The proof is essentially a matter of verification, once a suitable candidate for ua has
been found. Indeed, we shall look for a solutions of the form

                                                          |y|
                                     Z
                                                                    dt
  u(z) := P|y| (log w)(x) + C|y| =        log w(t)                     + C|y|,        z = x + iy ∈ C,
                                      R              (x − t)2 + y 2 π

where Py denotes the Poisson kernel in C± and C > 0 is a constant to be determined later.
Note that P|y| (log w)(x) is well-defined since log w ∈ L1 (dP ), and it is harmonic in C \ R.
The perturbed term C|y| is also trivially harmonic in C \ R, hence u is harmonic on C \ R.
Furthermore, since P|y| (log w)(x) is symmetric about the real line, extends continuously to
C and equals to log w on R, we conclude that u is continuous on C. We can therefore define
its Laplacian in the sense of distributions on C. Note that supp(∆u) ⊆ R. Now pairing
with test functions and applying Green’s Theorem, one can show that
                                                           
                            ∆u = 2 ∂y P|y| (log w)|y=0+ + C δR .

A noteworthy remark related to term appearing above is that the map

                                          ω 7→ ∂y E(ω)|y=0+

is in the language of PDE typically refereed to as the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator on
C\R, as it transforms Dirichlet data for the harmonic extensions to Neumann data. Moving
forward, we simply utilize the Cauchy Riemann equations in order to rewrite

                            ∂y E(log w)|y=0+ = −H((log w)′ )(x).                                   (34)

Therefore, we obtain
                               ∆u = 2 (−H((log w)′ ) + C) δR .

Now this where the subtle assumption H((log w)′ ) ∈ L∞ (R) enters the picture, allowing us
to choose the number C > 0 so that it exceed its supremum norm, and thus ensuring that
u is subharmonic on C with

              u = log w on R         and       u(x + iy) ≤ a|y| + A,             x, y ∈ R,

whenever a exceeds the supremum norm of H((log w)′ ) in R, and with A = supR log w, since
w is assumed to be bounded. To see that u is Lipschitz, it suffices to show that the partial
derivatives
                       ∂x P|y| (log w)(x), ∂y P|y| (log w)(x) ∈ L∞ (C).




                                                 137
To this end, an integration by parts yields

                  ∂x P|y| (log w)(x) = P|y| ((log w)′ ) (x),         z = x + iy ∈ C

while the same calculation as in (34) shows

              ∂y P|y| (log w)(x) = −P|y| (H((log w)′ )) (x),           z = x + iy ∈ C.

Since both (log w)′ and its Hilbert transform H((log w)′ ) belong to L∞ (R), it follows that
E(log w) has uniformly bounded gradient, thus u is uniformly Lipschitz continuous in C.

    Let us now gather a few remarks. The good thing with the above approach is that
we could actually solve the subharmonic BM-problem exactly, using a fairly explicit ansatz,
instead of merely finding a sub-solution: u ≤ log w on R. However, a drawback is that we had
to crucially rely on the rather obscure and impractical additional assumption H((log w)′ ) ∈
L∞ (R), but which appears rather intrinsically. In classical works of Beurling and Malliavin
[13], and Koosis [55], a sub-solution to the subharmonic BM-problem was found. Beurling–
Malliavin used a variational argument similar to the solution of the Dirichlet problem, while
Koosis developed a Perron-type method to exhibit a sub-solution. In the next subsection,
we shall provide a different proof of the Medium BM1-Theorem following the work of Havin-
Mashreghi-Nazarov in [77], and see that the same condition H((log w)′ ) ∈ L∞ (R) enters the
picture, but from a different perspective.

Step 3: Upgrading subharmonic BM1:
Having solved a subharmonic BM-problem, it now essentially remains to find an entire ana-
lytic function fa such that log|fa | ≤ ua +C, on C with fa (0) = 1, say. Historically, problems
of this type have been approached by looking for f expressed as am infinite Weierstrass
product formula over its zeros, which gives
                                          X
                          log|f (x)| =            ck log(ak − z),    z ∈ C.
                                              k


Now since our u already satisfies ∆u = µ with data µ = (−H((log w)′ ) + C)δR , we can write
                                      Z
                             u(z) =           log |t − z|dµ(t),     z ∈ C.
                                          R

These observations suggest that one may attempt to approximate µ by a sum of Dirac
deltas. Indeed, it turns out that one can carry out such an approach in detail, as was done
in [?].
    However, we shall here instead follow a different route which was suggested by J. Bour-
gain, mentioned in [23]. The proof will instead rely on the following solution to the ∂-
equation in weighted L2 -spaces by L. Hörmander.

Theorem 7.8 (Hörmander). Let f ∈ L2loc (C) and let φ : C → R be a continuous function
which is strictly subharmonic function in supp(f ), and satisfies

                                                     e−φ(z)
                                      Z
                               A :=       |f (z)|2          dA(z) < ∞.
                                      C              ∆φ(z)


                                                     138
Then there exists F ∈ L2loc (C) which solves ∂F = f in distributional sense on C, and
satisfies                     Z
                                         |F (z)|2 e−φ(z) dA(z) ≤ 4A.
                                     C

   A surprising fact is that the above result seems to have been noticed first only in several
complex variables. For a detailed proof, and additional historical remarks on Hörmander’s
Theorem, we refer the reader to the excellent notes of B. Berndtsson [11].
   We are now ready to solve the final piece of the problem.

Proposition 7.9 (Upgrading to an analytic solution). Let u : C → R be a non-trivial sub-
harmonic function satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition 7.7. Then there exist a constant
A > 0 and an entire function f such that

                        log |f (z)| ≤ A + u(z),            z ∈ C,         f (0) = 1.

Proof. The proof is based on solving a non-homogeneous ∂-equation and applying Hörman-
der’s L2 -method. Our data will be conveniently expressed in terms of a smooth compactly
supported function ψ ∈ C ∞ (C) which satisfies

                     ψ≡1        on         {|z| ≤ 1},         supp(ψ) ⊆ {|z| ≤ 2}.

Below, we shall form simplicity allow the constants to change from line to line.

Step 1: A non-homogeneous ∂-equation:
Consider a smooth compactly supported function ψ on C with

                                  ∆χ(z) ≥ 1,               1 ≤ |z| ≤ 2.

Now consider the following perturb function of u:

                                v(z) := u(z) + χ(z),                z ∈ C.

Note that the property on χ ensures that v is strictly subharmonic in the annulus

                                         {z ∈ C : 1 ≤ |z| ≤ 2} .

We now seek a solution to the equation

                                     ∂F (z) = ∂ψ(z),            z ∈ C.

Since ∂ψ is supported in {1 ≤ |z| ≤ 2} and ∆v ≳ 1, Hörmander’s Theorem 7.8 allows us to
exhibit a solution F ∈ L2loc (C) which satisfies the weighted estimate:

                                                                         e−v(z)
               Z                                 Z

We shall now argue that the desired candidate is simply given by the difference

                                          f := ψ − F + F (0),


                                                     139
Indeed, since ∂f = 0, Weyl’s Lemma ensures that f is an entire analytic function in C.
Moreover, since ψ and f are smooth, so is F . In particular, this ensures that F (0) is
well-defined. Furthermore, we also have

                                  f (0) = ψ(0) − F (0) + F (0) = 1.

Thus the required normalization holds.

Step 2: The estimate on f :
It now only remains to show that for all |λ| ≫ 1:

                                        |f (λ)| ≤ C + u(λ).

Let B(λ) denote the disc of radius 1 centered at point λ ∈ C, and choose λ so that B(λ)
does not meet supp(χ) ∪ supp(ψ). Since |f |2 is subharmonic, and f = −F + F (0) on B(λ),
we have                            Z

                                  u(z) ≤ u(λ) + L,       z ∈ B(λ).

   The proof of Theorem 7.6 is now an immediate consequence of Proposition 7.7 and
