7.4    A complex analytic perspective to the BM-Theorem
In this subsection, we shall outline a somewhat more canonical approach towards proving the
Medium BM1 Theorem, which involves the notion of outer functions in Hardy spaces. But
first, we shall attempt to describe the reason why it is typically referred to as a multiplier
Theorem. To this end, let a > 0 be a number, and Ca denote the so-called Cartwright class
of order a, consisting of entire analytic functions f satisfying the following conditions:

  (i) f is of exponential-type a: for any ε > 0 there exists a constant C(ε) > 0 such that

                              |f (z)| ≤ C(ε) exp ((a + ε)|z|) ,       z ∈ C.



                                                 140
 (ii)
                                            log+ |f (x)|
                                          Z
                                                         dx < ∞,
                                          R   1 + x2
        where log+ x := max (0, log x).

We shall refer to Cart := ∪a>0 Ca as the Cartwright class, which forms an algebra under
pointwise multiplication. A deep result by M. G. Krein (See Ch. 1.6 in [77]) asserts that
f ∈ Cart if and only if the restrictions f± := f |C± belong to the Nevanlinna classes
N (C± ). In other words, functions in the Cartwright class are entire functions belonging
to Nevanlinna classes of the upper and lower half-planes, and thus one actually has that
log |f | ∈ L1 (dP ) whenever f ∈ Cart. Now a classical result in the theory of Hardy spaces
asserts that Nevanlinna class functions can always be factored as quotients of two bounded
analytic functions, where the numerator is zero free. It turns out that in the symmetric
framework of Cart, much more can be said. The following result is somewhat clarifies why
the term multiplier appears in the statement of the BM1 Theorem, and the original result
of Beurling and Malliavin.

Theorem 7.10 (The BM-multiplier Theorem). For any f ∈ Cart and any ε > 0, there
exists a multiplier mε ∈ Bε such that f mε is bounded on R.

   Here Bε denotes the functions of exponential type ε, which are essentially bounded in
R, thus contained in Cε . The terms multiplier comes from the fact that multiplication of f
with mε multiplies a function f ∈ Ca into the space Ba+ε . Phrased differently, whenever
f ∈ Ca and ε > 0, one can always factorize
                                   g
                              f=     ,        g ∈ Ba+ε ,   m ∈ Bε .
                                   m
Moving forward, we shall outline a second proof of the BM-1 Theorem 7.6, which is somewhat
more canonical and utilizes the factorization of functions in the Hardy spaces. The proof
is principally taken from the work of V. Havin, J. Mashreghi and F. Nazarov in [77], and
further details and historical notes related to it can be found there.

Step 1: Reformulating using Paley-Wiener:
We shall first use the notion of Paley–Wiener spaces in order to reformulate the BM1-
Problem.
   We start with a weight w ∈ L2 (R) with log w ∈ L1 (dP ). This allows us to construct an
outer function W ∈ H 2 (C+ ) with |W | = w a.e on R. The main goal is to find a non-trivial
function f ∈ L2 (R) with supp(fb) ⊆ [0, a] such that f /w ∈ L∞ (R), under the additional
assumption on w.
   Define the translated Paley-Wiener space by

                       Ha2 := {f ∈ L2 (R) : supp(fb) ⊆ [0, a]},       a > 0.

It is not difficult to show that f ∈ Ha2 if and only if f (x), f (x)e2πiax are boundary elements
of H 2 (C+ )-functions. In order to move forward, we shall need practical sufficient condition
that allows us to identify the modulus of functions in Ha2 . This simple observation is a
special case of a general Theorem by K. Dyakonov in [31].



                                                 141
Lemma 7.11 (Dyakonov’s Lemma). Let ψ ∈ L2 (R) positive with R log ψdP > −∞, and
                                                                     R

such that ψ 2 e2πiax is outer. Then ψ is the modulus of a function in Ha2 .

Proof. Let F be an outer function in H 2 (C+ ) with |F | = ψ a.e on R. Then F 2 is also an
outer function whose modulus equals ψ 2 a.e on R. Now by assumption, ψ 2 (x)e2πiax is also
outer, and with modulus equal to ψ 2 a.e on R, therefore there exists a unimodular constant
c such that
                       cF 2 (x) = F (x)F (x)e2πiax ,     a.e x ∈ R.

Dividing by F , which is non-zero a.e on R, we see that F (x), F (x)e2πiax = cF (x) ∈ H 2 (C+ ),
hence we conclude that F ∈ Ha2 with |F | = ψ a.e on R.

   With this observation at hand, we recall that our goal is not necessarily to exhibit a
function f ∈ Ha2 with |f | = w a.e on R, as we settle for

                             |f (x)| = m(x)w(x),        a.e x ∈ R,

for some non-negative bounded function m ∈ L∞ (R). Note that since both f, w are assumed
to have integrable logarithm wrt dP on R, we can reformulate the BM-problem by using
Dyakonov’s Lemma with ψ = mw, as follows:
    Reformulating the BM1-Problem:
Find a positive function m ∈ L∞ (R) with log m ∈ L1 (dP ), such that

                     mw ∈ L2 (R),       and      m2 w2 e2πiax        is outer.

Step 2: Reducing to a problem on conjugate transform:
Now recall that F is an outer function if and only if

                                                       ^
                                  log F = log |F | + ilog |F |,

where fe denotes the harmonic conjugate transform of f (see explicit definition below). Using
Dyakonov’s lemma, it suffices to find log m ∈ L1 (dP ) with w ∈ L∞ (R) and positive, such
that

  log m2 (x)w2 (x) + 2πiax = log m2 (x)w2 (x) + ilog m
                                                   ^  2 w 2 (x) + constant        (mod 2π)
                                              ⇐⇒
                                  ^
                                  log         ]
                                      m(x) + log w(x) = πax + constant           (mod π).    (35)

It is natural to attempt removing the slightly ”annoying” periodicity which is built-in in the
above equation, and simple ask whether it is possible to solve the equation with one single
constant in all of R:

                     ^
                     log         ]
                         m(x) + log w(x) = πax + constant,            x ∈ R.                 (36)

However, a classical result of A. Kolmogorov presents a significant obstacle to this approach.




                                              142
Theorem 7.12 (Kolmogorov). For any f ∈ L1 (dP ), we have
                                                
                      λ · P {x ∈ R : |fe(x)| > λ} → 0,                λ → +∞.

   Now if (36) where to hold, then invoking Kolmogorov’s Theorem with f = log m + log w,
we get
                                                                  
  λ · P ({x ∈ R : |πax + const| > λ}) = λ · P {x ∈ R : |fe(x)| > λ} → 0,                 λ → +∞

but the corresponding quantity of the linear function πax + constant is explicitly seen to be
only big-O of 1/λ as λ → +∞, which makes (36) unattainable. Therefore, in order to solve
the equation (35), we also have to makes sure that we can choose a non-trivial function
k : R → Z such that

                        ^
                        log        Ω(x) + πax − πk(x),
                            m(x) = e                                      x ∈ R,

where Ω = log w1 . In order to compensate for the growth of Ω(x)
                                                               e    + πax, it is natural to
choose k(x) as the integer part of e
                                   Ω(x)/π +ax, thus the problem reduces to finding a positive
bounded function m on R which satisfies the equation:

                                ^
                                log m(x) = u(x) ⇐⇒ m(x) = e−eu(x)                               (37)

where u(x) := Ω(x)
              e    + πax − πk(x) − π/2 satisfying ∥u∥L∞ (R) ≤ π/2.

Step 3: Explicit estimates:
We have now arrived at a stage where we can explicitly consider what the equation really
entails, using the explicit formula for the conjugate transform:

   As was also mentioned in the previous subsection, the statement of Proposition 7.13 has


                                                145
the drawback that a-admissibility of w depends on the supremum norm of the (uniform)
Lipschitz constant of Ω,e a quantity that is extremely difficult to confirm in practice. The
reader may therefore object that this result sits rather far from the strong BM-Theorem,
whose hypotheses involve only the qualitative Lipschitz continuity of Ω, and for which
admissibility actually holds for every a > 0.
   It was proved in [77] (see Theorem 2 therein) that the strong BM1 Theorem can actually
be obtained from Proposition 7.13, relying on the following striking assertion, one whose
plausibility is far from obvious and demands some courage to even conjecture.

Theorem 7.15 (Nazarov’s Majorization Theorem). Let 0 < ω ≤ 1 with log ω ∈ L1 (dP )
which is uniformly Lipchitz continuous on R. For any a > 0, there exists a function 0 <
ωa ≤ 1 with the following properties:

  (i) ωa ≤ ω,

 (ii) The function Ωa := log ω1a ∈ L1 (dP ),

(iii) Ωa and Ω
             fa are both uniformly Lipschitz continuous on R with

                                                                    ′
                                  sup |Ω′a (x)| ≤ a,       sup |Ω
                                                                fa (x)| ≤ a.
                                  x∈R                      x∈R


    As indicated, this result is admittedly technical and requires some intricate properties
of the Hilbert transform, which we shall not include here. We therefore refer the reader to
[77]. However, the reader should have no problem on reducing the strong BM1 Theorem to
that of Proposition 7.13, using Nazarov’s Majorization Theorem.


7.5    The Second BM-Theorem on completeness of exponentials
The second Beurling-Malliavin Theorem bears a crucial piece of the puzzle of the ambitious
program of determining Fourier uniqueness pairs, as we encountered in Chapter 2, namely
when do we have the following manifestation of the uncertainty principle:

            f ∈ L2 (R),          ess supp(f ) ⊆ [−a, a],         fb|Λ = 0       =⇒ f ≡ 0,

where Λ is a subset of R and a > 0. Note that the compact support of f makes fb into an
entire analytic functions, hence to avoid redundancy, one restricts the attention to discrete
subsets of real numbers Λ. Of course, problem remains unaltered if the roles of f and fb
are interchanged. We shall now illustrate two classical reformulations of this problem. Fix
a > 0 and note that since f ∈ L2 (R), the Plancherel’s Theorem allows us to rephrase the
problem as for which discrete sets Λ ⊂ R do we have
                           Z a
                 fb(λ) =         fb(ξ)e−iξλ dξ = 0      ∀λ ∈ Λ              =⇒ fb ≡ 0?
                            −a

In other words, we are asking for which pairs of a > 0 and discrete subsets of real numbers
Λ is the linear span of the associated family of exponentials

                                     E(Λ) = Span{eiλt : λ ∈ Λ}



                                                  146
dense in L2 ([−a, a])? This implies that the problem is equivalent to a certain completeness
problem of exponentials in L2 of a segment on the real line.

    Using the Paley-Wiener Theorem, one can easily show that the Fourier transform maps
f ∈ L2 (R) with ess supp(f ) ⊆ [−a, a] unitarily on the space of entire analytic functions
fb ∈ L2 (R) of exponential-type at most a > 0. Formally, we identity

                        PWa := {f ∈ L2 (R) : ess supp(fb) ⊆ [−a, a]}

as a closed subspace of L2 (R), but which according to Paley-Wiener Theorem consists of
entire analytic functions of exponential-type at most a. In this framework, our problem can
be reformulated as characterizing the discrete subsets Λ ⊂ R for which the following holds:

                         fb ∈ PWa            fb|Λ = 0           =⇒      f ≡ 0.

This is readily a uniqueness problem for analytic functions in PWa , and these two seemingly
different perspectives makes the initial problem structurally rich. It turns out that the
problem becomes simpler if we drop the strict prescription of a > 0 and instead consider
the quantity:
                       ρ(Λ) := sup{a > 0 : E(Λ) dense in L2 ([−a, a])}.

We shall now introduce a more geometric quantity, which is intimately related to this prob-
lem. A sequence of disjoint intervals {In }n on the real line is said to be long if

                                X               |In |2
                                                              = +∞.
                                  n
                                          1 + (dist(In , 0))2

    For instance, if a system {In }n of disjoint intervals are uniformly bounded away from
the origin, then one can show that {In }n is long if and only if their length relative to their
distance to the origin fails to be square-summable:

                                  X                       2
                                               |In |
                                                                = +∞.
                                      n
                                            dist(In , 0)

On the contrary, a sequence of disjoint interval {In }n can never be long if their total lengths
is too small                            X
                                           |In |2 < ∞.
                                              n

This somewhat justifies their label. With this notion at hand, we introduced the so-called
upper BM-density of a discrete sequence Λ ⊂ R as the quantity

           D∗ (Λ) := sup {d ≥ 0 : ∃ long {In }n such that #(Λ ∩ In ) ≥ d|In |, ∀n} .

Roughly speaking, the upper BM-density of a discrete sequence Λ ⊂ R measures the largest
possible density of Λ among the family of long intervals on the real line.
    The second Beurling–Malliavin Theorem relates the completeness problem on exponen-
tials with frequencies in Λ, or equivalently the uniqueness problem of in Paley-Wiener spaces,
to a more metric/geometric density condition of Λ.


                                                   147
Theorem 7.16 (BM2-Theorem). For any discrete sequence Λ ⊂ R the following identity
holds:
                              ρ(Λ) = πD∗ (Λ).

   The proof of the BM2-Theorem hinges on the BM-Multiplier Theorem in the form of a
multiplier, but it is beyond the scope of these notes. Besides the original source in [14], a
careful treatment of the matter can be found in the book of P. Koosis in [53], and in V.
Havin and B. Jöricke in [36, Ch. 4]. We also refer the reader to a survey of N. Makarov and
A. Poltoratski in [73], where a different approach to the BM2-Theorem, using the kernels of
Toeplitz operators is provided.


7.6    Further results
There are also versions of the BM1-Theorem where uniform admissibility is substituted by
admissibility in p-th mean. More specifically, we for which weights w does there exists a
non-trivial compactly supported f ∈ L2 (R) such that

                                        |fb(ξ)|p
                                      Z
                                              p
                                                 dξ < ∞,       p ≥ 1.                       (38)
                                      R w(ξ)

We declare such weights w to be BM (p)-majorant. From this perspective, one can view
the BM1-Theorem as the p = ∞ case of the above problem. It turns out that admissibility
in p-th mean is slightly different than uniform admissibility. This is somewhat visible from
the simple observation that while w(ξ0 ) = 0 forces any fb(ξ0 ) in the uniform setting, while
this is not necessarily the case in regime of p-th mean. It is not difficult to show that any
BM-majorant f is also a BM (p)-majorant, for any p ≥ 1. Indeed, if f ∈ L2 (R) is non-trivial
a compact supported function with

                                          |f (ξ)| ≤ w(ξ),   ξ ∈ R,

then the function fε (x) = f ∗ 1[0,ε] ∗ 1[0,ε] (x), with ε > 0 has only slightly larger supported
than f , and computing the Fourier transform one easily obtains

                |fbε (ξ)|p           |fb(ξ)|p sin2p (ξ/ε)            sin2p (ξ/ε)
             Z                    Z                                Z
                        p
                           dξ =            p
                                             ·            dξ ≤                   dξ < ∞.
               R w(ξ)              R w(ξ)         ξ 2p             R     ξ 2p
Now the main result of the original paper of Beurling and Mallivian in [13] goes as follows.

Theorem 7.17 (BM1-Mean). Let w be a bounded weight in R with log w ∈ L1 (dP ). Set
W (x) = (log w(x))/x and assume that the following energy estimate holds:

                                            |W (x) − W (y)|2
                                  Z Z
                                                             dxdy < ∞.                       (E)
                                  R       R     |x − y|2

Then for every p ≥ 1 and a > 0, there exists a non-trivial function f in R with

                                                |fb(ξ)|p
                                             Z
                                                      p
                                                         dξ < ∞,
                                              R w(ξ)

such that supp(f dm) ⊆ [0, a].


                                                     148
     Note that the energy condition in (E) is a global condition, which accounts for integra-
bility at infinity, and is independent of p. The proof is, again, long, and we refer the reader
to [55] and [37] for details.
     Despite the fact that a BM (p)-majorant may not necessarily be a BM -majorant, one
still obtains the following relation.

Theorem 7.18 (Beurling–Malliavin, [13]). Let w ∈ L∞ (R) be a weight satisfying the con-
ditions of BM1-Mean. For any ε > 0, the regularized weight wε defined by

                         wε (x) := exp ((Pε ∗ log w)(x)) ,      x ∈ R,

is a BM-majorant.

    Several dimensional analogues of the BM-multiplier theorem where obtained by I. Vasi-
lyev in [95]. More recently, A. Bergman gave a simpler proof, which essentially reduced the
problem to the classical one-variable setting. The paper is short and elementary, see [9]. We
also mention the work of A. Cohen in [23], where we utilizes a several variable BM-multiplier
Theorem, in order to extend to aforementioned fractal uncertainty principle by J. Bourgain
and S. Dyatlov, [23].
    Other extensions to the BM-1 Theorem pertain to viewing the Paley-Wiener spaces
  2
Ha (R) as a special case of so called Model space. Given an inner function θ in C+ , the
model space Kθ2 in C+ is defined as the closed subspace of f ∈ H 2 (C+ ) with the property
that
                                       f, θf ∈ H 2 (C+ ).

The generalized BM-problem then turns into describing admissible pairs (θ, w) for which
one can exhibit a non-trivial function f ∈ Kθ2 with

                                  |f (x)| ≤ w(x),      x ∈ R.

Note the classical BM-problem corresponds to the inner function θ(z) = eiaz with a > 0.
    In this generality, the BM-problem becomes very difficult, as the model spaces are no
longer characterized by a growth condition of Paley–Wiener type. However, several partial
result special classes of meromorphic inner functions θ have been obtain. We refer the reader
to the work of Y. Belov, V. Havin, and J. Mashreghi in [6, 7, 34, 35]. A more novel approach
involving Toeplitz kernels was realized by N. Makarov and A. Poltoratski in [73], which was
later surveyed in [74].
    We mention some recent important developments in harmonic analysis which stem from
the BM-1 Theorem. A new refined version of the uncertainty principle for Fourier transforms
supported on fractal sets was proved by J. Bourgain and S. Dyatlov in [18]. Recall that a
manifestation of the classical uncertainty principal asserts that for any pair of closed sets
X, Y ⊂ R and any f ∈ L2 (R), we have

                            ∥1Y F(f 1X )∥L2 ≲ |X|1/2 |Y |1/2 ∥f ∥L2 .

This estimate roughly quantifies the assertion that if a function is spatially localized in a
small set X, then no small set Y in the frequency domain can capture a large portion of
its Fourier energy. This bound can in general not be improved, and is easily deduced from


                                              149
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality applied to the inner integral.
   Given a number 0 < δ < 1 and a closed set X ⊂ R is called δ-regular on scales a to b, if
there is a probability measure µX supported in X such that

                               µX (I) ≤ C|I|δ ,     a ≤ |I| ≤ b,

and if in addition I is centered in X, the opposite inequality also holds, µX (I) ≥ C −1 |I|δ .
In view of Frostman’s mass distribution principle, this roughly means that the set X has
Hausdorff dimension δ between scales a to b. For instance, one can show that the any small
h-neighborhood of the classical Cantor 1/3-set is a log 2/ log 3-regular set on scales h to 1.

Theorem 7.19 (The Fractal UP). Let δ ∈ (0, 1) and assume X ⊆ [−1, 1] be a δ-regular set
on scales 1/N to 1, while Y ⊆ [−N, N ] is a δ-regular set on scales 1 to N . Then there is a
number β = β(δ) > 0, such that

                       ∥1Y F(1X f )∥L2 ≲ N −β ∥f ∥L2 ,     ∀f ∈ L2 (R).

   Roughly speaking, the fractal uncertain principle asserts that a function cannot be both
localized in position and frequency, near a fractal set. We also mention a gentle survey on
the matter by S. Dyatlov, see [32]. Its known that the number β has a rather wild depen-
dence on δ, and recent accounts on these matters were investigated in [47].

    Then general completeness problem on exponentials

                             E(Λ) := {eixλ : λ ∈ Λ},      Λ⊂C

in L2 (µ) for a general finite Borel measure µ on R, was surprisingly solved by A. Poltoratski
in [84]. This problem and a certain related spectral gap problem of measures were long
thought to be ”transcendental”, that is, not having characterization. We also mention
a recent interesting paper on connecting this problem to a uniqueness problem by Polya
and Levinson, [78]. We mention that an equivalence of the classical BM2 Theorem and
the Mitkovski–Poltoratski Theorem was recently announced in [4] for a class of separates
sequences Λ ⊂ R:
                                   d(Λ) := inf′
                                                 |λ − λ′ | > 0.
                                          λ,λ ∈Λ
                                           λ̸=λ′

For general discrete sequence Λ ⊂ R, the work of A. Poltoratski in [84] requires the different
notion of energy.


8     Appendix
8.1    Hardy spaces
We summarize some key features of Hardy spaces. We begin with the unit disc D, and later
explain how the corresponding theory on the upper half-plane C+ is obtained via conformal
mapping.




                                             150
   For 1 ≤ p < ∞, we denote by H p (D) the space of analytic functions f on D with
                                               Z
                          ∥f ∥pH p := sup             |f (rζ)|p dm(ζ) < ∞.
                                      0<r<1       T


For p = ∞, we define H ∞ (D) as the Banach space of bounded analytic functions on D
equipped with
                                ∥f ∥H ∞ := sup |f (z)|.
                                                       z∈D

   A central feature of Hardy spaces is that they admit both an analytic and a boundary-
value interpretation. The following result clarifies on aspect of it.

Proposition 8.1. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞. Then every function in H p (D) arises as the Poisson
extension of a function in

                        H p (T) := {f ∈ Lp (T) : fb(n) = 0 for n < 0}.

Conversely, for any f ∈ H p (T), the Poisson integral

                                        1 − |z|2
                                      Z
                       P (f )(z) :=             2
                                                  f (ζ) dm(ζ),                z∈D
                                      T |ζ − z|

defines a function in H p (D), and we have
                                                   Z
                                 ∥P (f )∥pH p =           |f (ζ)|p dm(ζ).
                                                      T

    The Poisson extension solves the Dirichlet problem in D with boundary data f ∈ Lp (T),
producing a harmonic function. The additional condition fb(n) = 0 for n < 0 forces analyt-
icity. Indeed, using the identity

                   1 − |z|2      1        1
                            =        +        − 1,                   ζ ∈ T,     z ∈ D,
                   |ζ − z|2   1 − ζz   1 − ζz

one obtains                      Z                 ∞
                                    f (ζ)         X
                   P (f )(z) =            dm(ζ) =     fb(n)z n ,               z ∈ D.
                                 T 1 − ζz         n=0

   The following result shows that functions in H p (D) admit well-defined boundary traces
almost everywhere, and that H p (T) may be viewed as the natural trace space of H p (D).

Theorem 8.2. Let F ∈ H p (D). Then for dm-almost every ζ ∈ T, the limit

                                               lim           F (z)
                                               z→ζ
                                          |z−ζ|<α(1−|z|)


exists (for any α > 0). This is called the non-tangential limit of F at ζ. Furthermore, the
resulting boundary function f belongs to H p (T), and F is recovered as its Poisson extension:

                                          F (z) = P (f )(z).

   It it is customary to identify H p (D) and H p (T) and simply write H p when no confusion


                                                   151
arises. The following simple estimate is crucial in theory of Hardy spaces.

Theorem 8.3 (Jensen–Poisson inequality). If f ∈ H 1 (T), then

                                                              1 − |z|2
                                           Z
                     log |P (f )(z)| ≤          log |f (ζ)|            dm(ζ),      z ∈ D.
                                            T                 |ζ − z|2

    In particular, it follows that functions in H 1 have finite logarithmic integral on T. More
generally, one can show that this property holds for functions in the Nevanlinna class N ,
defined by                            Z
                                     sup           log+ |f (rζ)|dm(ζ) < ∞.
                                    0<r<1      T

Another classical result asserts that every f ∈ N can be written as f = g/h with g, h ∈ H ∞
and h zero-free in D.

Sketch of proof of Theorem 8.3. Applying Jensen’s lemma at the origin gives
                                              Z
                            log |P (f )(0)| ≤   log |f (ζ)|dm(ζ).
                                                          T

Composing with the automorphism

                                                               z−λ
                                                   φz (λ) =
                                                              1 − λz

and using invariance of the Poisson kernel yields the general estimate.

   The Jensen–Poisson inequality naturally leads to the inner–outer factorization. Given
f ∈ H p , define the associated outer factor of f
                                       Z                           
                                              ζ +z
                      Of (z) = exp                 log |f (ζ)| dm(ζ) ,             z ∈ D.
                                            T ζ −z

Then Of is analytic and zero-free in D, satisfies

                                 log |Of (z)| = P (log |f |)(z),          z ∈ D.

Using Jensen’s inequality for convex functions, and Fubini’s Theorem we have

                                                     1 − r2
  Z                          Z     Z                              
        |Of (rξ)|p dm(ξ) =     exp p log |f (ζ)|             dm(ζ)   dm(ξ)
    T                        T        T            |ζ − rξ|2
                                          1 − r2
                                  Z Z
                                ≤                2
                                                   dm(ξ)|f (ζ)|p dm(ζ) = ∥f ∥pLp ,          0 < r < 1.
                                   T T  |ζ − rξ|

This implies that Of belongs to the Hardy space H p (D), hence has finite non-tangential
limits equal to F ∈ H p (T). However, standard properties of Poisson kernels imply that

                                  |F (ζ)| = |f (ζ)|,           dm − a.e ζ ∈ T.                      (39)

Invoking the Jensen–Poisson Inequality, we get

                                       |f (z)| ≤ |Of (z)|           z ∈ D.


                                                         152
This means that the quotient

                                              f (z)
                                  Θf (z) :=               z∈D
                                              Of (z)

defines a bounded analytic function on D with supz∈D |Θf (z)| ≤ 1, and whose boundary
values are unimodular:

                            lim |Θf (rζ)| = 1,         dm − a.e ζ ∈ T.
                           r→1−


Such functions are called inner functions. We record the following result, which is essentially
a polar decomposition of functions in H p .

Theorem 8.4 (Inner–outer factorization). Every f ∈ H p (D) admits a factorization

                                         f = Θf · Of

on D, where Θf is inner and Of is outer. This factorization is unique up to multiplication
by a unimodular constant.

    Inner functions admit a further canonical decomposition which separates their “zero
structure” from their “boundary singularities.” More precisely, any inner function Θ on D
can be written uniquely (up to a unimodular constant) as

                               Θ(z) = BΛ (z) Sµ (z),        z ∈ D,

where the two factors encode distinct phenomena.
    The first factor BΛ is the Blaschke product, determined by the zeros Λ ⊂ D of Θ. It is
given by
                                      Y |λ| z − λ
                             BΛ (z) =                   z ∈ D.
                                           λ 1 − λz
                                      λ∈Λ

Each factor is a Möbius transformation mapping D to itself and having modulus one on
T. The infinite product converges to a non-trivial analytic function if and only if the zeros
satisfy the Blaschke condition      X
                                        (1 − |λ|) < ∞.
                                      λ∈Λ

This Blaschke condition expresses that the zeros do not accumulate too rapidly toward the
boundary. The function BΛ is itself inner, and it is precisely the part of Θ that accounts
for its zeros inside D.
    The second factor Sµ is the singular inner function, which is zero-free in D and captures
the remaining boundary behavior. It is given by the formula
                                     Z            
                                         ζ +z
                        Sµ (z) = exp −        dµ(ζ) ,                z∈D
                                       T ζ −z

where µ is a positive finite Borel measure on T that is singular with respect to Lebesgue
measure.
   The terminology reflects the fact that Sµ arises from a “singular distribution of mass”
on the boundary. In comparison with the outer factor Of of f , the singular inner factor


                                               153
Sµ is also zero-free on D, but now log |Sµ | is the Poisson extension of a positive singular
measure, while log |Of | was the Poisson extension of an integrable function f ∈ L1 (T).
   Thus, the factorization
                                        Θ = BΛ · Sµ

separates the contributions of interior zeros and singular boundary behavior. In particular,
an inner function is completely determined by the location of its zeros in D together with a
singular measure on T.

    We now turn to the upper half-plane C+ = {z ∈ C : Im(z) > 0}. For 1 ≤ p < ∞, we
define H p (C+ ) as the space of analytic functions F on C+ such that
                                         Z
                                 sup          |F (x + iy)|p dx < ∞.
                                 y>0      R


Functions in H p (C+ ) arise as Poisson extensions
                                     Z
                                                        y        dt
                  P (f )(x + iy) =       f (t)                      ,      x + iy ∈ C+
                                     R            (x − t)2 + y 2 π

of an element in f in

                        H p (R) := {f ∈ Lp (R) : fb(ξ) = 0 a.e ξ < 0 }.

If p > 2, the Fourier transform may need to be interpreted in the sense of tempered distri-
butions. Conversely, every F ∈ H p (C+ ) has limit along every cone in C+ with vertex at
a.e x ∈ R, and the limit gives rise to an unique element f ∈ H p (R), for which P (F ) = f .
Hence, as in the disc, one has non-tangential boundary limits almost everywhere, and an
isometric and isomorphic identification H p (C+ ) ∼
                                                  = H p (R).
    The Hardy space theory on C+ is easily obtained from that on D via the conformal map

                                                             1+z
                         φ : D → C+               ϕ(z) = i       ,      z ∈ D.
                                                             1−z

    In this setting, non-trivial functions f in H p satisfy the logarithmic integrability condi-
tion:                               Z
                                                    dt
                                       log|f (t)|         > −∞.
                                     R            1 +  t2
   Under this correspondence, the inner and outer factorizations transfer as follows. Every
F ∈ H p (C+ ) admits a factorization

                             F (z) = B(z) S(z) O(z),                 z ∈ C+

where:
   - B is the associated Blaschke product over the zeros {zk } ⊂ C+ of F
                                              Y z − zk
                                B(z) =                          z ∈ C+ ,

satisfying the Blaschke condition:
                                     X Im zk

                                      |zk |2 + 1

- S is the associated singular inner function
                                  Z               
                                       1 + tz
                       S(z) = exp −i          dµ(t) ,            z ∈ C+

where µ is a positive Borel measure singular wrt dx and finite wrt the Poisson measure:
                                       Z

                                 Z                         

   In summary, the inner–outer factorization persists in C+ , with the geometry of the
boundary T replaced by R and the Poisson kernel adapted accordingly. For further details,
we refer the reader to [33, 54].
